LANGGRAPH SYALLABUS

Chapter 1: LangGraph Fundamentals

Topic 1: What Is LangGraph?

LangGraph is a low-level orchestration framework and runtime used to build long-running, stateful workflows and AI agents. It represents application logic as a graph in which nodes perform operations, edges control execution flow, and shared state carries information between the steps.

a) Definition

--> LangGraph is a framework for designing AI applications as explicit graphs. Each node performs a specific task, such as calling a model, retrieving documents, executing a tool, validating an answer, or requesting human approval. Edges determine which node executes next.

--> LangGraph is stateful, which means information can be preserved and updated throughout workflow execution. The state may contain messages, retrieved documents, tool results, counters, routing decisions, approvals, errors, and the final answer.

--> LangGraph is primarily an orchestration framework rather than a model or retrieval framework. It does not provide its own language model. Instead, it coordinates model calls, tools, retrievers, databases, APIs, and deterministic Python functions.

--> LangGraph supports workflows that may run for an extended period, pause, resume, branch, loop, stream results, or involve human participation. Its core capabilities include durable execution, human-in-the-loop support, memory, streaming, and integration with observability systems.

b) Purpose

--> LangGraph provides explicit control over complex AI workflows. Instead of allowing a model to decide every action freely, developers define permitted nodes, transitions, loops, limits, approval points, and failure paths in application code.

--> It combines deterministic operations with model-driven decisions. Business-rule validation, authorization, calculations, and routing can remain predictable, while language models handle tasks such as query classification, summarization, planning, extraction, and answer generation.

--> It supports workflows that cannot be represented effectively as simple linear chains. Applications can branch according to state, retry failed operations, repeat retrieval, send sensitive actions for approval, or coordinate several specialized agents.

--> It provides the infrastructure needed to make agents more reliable in production. State persistence, checkpoints, streaming, interrupts, and detailed execution traces help applications recover from selected failures and expose what happened during execution.

c) Basic Architecture

                         USER INPUT
                              |
                              v
                            START
                              |
                              v
                         SHARED STATE
                              |
                              v
                       PROCESSING NODE
                              |
                  +-----------+-----------+
                  |                       |
                  v                       v
             TOOL NODE               MODEL NODE
                  |                       |
                  +-----------+-----------+
                              |
                              v
                      VALIDATION NODE
                              |
                   +----------+----------+
                   |                     |
                 Valid                 Invalid
                   |                     |
                   v                     v
                  END              RETRY OR REVIEW

Architecture Explanation

The workflow receives an initial input.
 The input is converted into the graph’s state structure. The state defines which values are available to nodes during execution.

Execution begins at the virtual START node.
 START does not contain application logic. It identifies the first real node or nodes that should execute.

A node receives the current state and performs an operation.
 The node normally returns only the state fields it wants to add or update.

The graph merges the node’s update into the shared state.
 The merging behavior may use direct replacement or a reducer, depending on the state field.

An ordinary or conditional edge selects the next node.
 Conditional routing can inspect the updated state and choose a different path at runtime.

Execution continues until it reaches END.
 The final state is returned to the caller or streamed as execution progresses.

d) Core LangGraph Concepts

1. State

State is the shared data structure that moves through the graph. It contains the information required by nodes and represents the workflow’s current condition.

Nodes should normally return partial state updates rather than rebuilding the complete state. LangGraph merges those updates according to the state schema and any configured reducers.

Example state:

	class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    	  question: str
    	  category: str
    	  answer: str

2. Nodes

A node is a Python function, Runnable, or compatible operation that performs one unit of work. Common nodes include model invocation, retrieval, tool execution, validation, summarization, classification, and human review.

Every node receives the current state and returns an update. Nodes should have focused responsibilities so their behavior is easier to test, trace, replace, and reuse.

Example:

	def classify_question(state: WorkflowState) -> dict:
    		return {
        		"category": "technical",
    		}

3. Edge 

An edge connects one node to another and defines the permitted execution path. Ordinary edges are appropriate when the next step is always known.

Edges make control flow visible and explicit. This is different from hiding the complete workflow inside one large function or relying on an unconstrained model to decide every transition.

Example:
	
	builder.add_edge(
    		"classify_question",
   		 "generate_answer",
	)

4. Conditional Edges

A conditional edge selects the next node by inspecting the current state. It enables branching, loops, retries, escalation, fallback, and termination. The routing function should return a stable route name rather than execute the next operation itself. LangGraph maps the route name to a destination node.

Example:
		def route_question(state: WorkflowState) -> str:
    			if state["category"] == "technical":
        			return "technical_answer"

    			return "general_answer"

5. START and END

START is the virtual entry point of a LangGraph workflow. An edge from START defines the first application node that executes. END is the virtual termination point. Reaching END indicates that the workflow has completed and its resulting state can be returned.

START
  |
  v
Node A
  |
  v
Node B
  |
  v
END

6. Compiled Graph

The graph builder defines the workflow, while the compiled graph is its executable form. Developers register nodes and edges on StateGraph, then call compile().

Compilation validates important parts of the graph structure and creates an object that supports execution methods. The resulting graph can be invoked or streamed using supported runtime methods.

graph = builder.compile()
result = graph.invoke(initial_state)

e) Code Example

from typing import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    category: str
    answer: str


def classify_question(state: WorkflowState) -> dict:
    question = state["question"].lower()

    technical_terms = (
        "langgraph",
        "python",
        "agent",
        "workflow",
        "state",
    )

    is_technical = any(
        term in question
        for term in technical_terms
    )

    category = (
        "technical"
        if is_technical
        else "general"
    )

    return {
        "category": category,
    }


def route_question(state: WorkflowState) -> str:
    if state["category"] == "technical":
        return "technical"

    return "general"


def generate_technical_answer(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:
    answer = (
        "LangGraph is a framework for building "
        "stateful, graph-based workflows and agents."
    )

    return {
        "answer": answer,
    }


def generate_general_answer(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:
    answer = (
        "This question was classified as a "
        "general request."
    )

    return {
        "answer": answer,
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(WorkflowState)

    builder.add_node(
        "classify",
        classify_question,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "technical_answer",
        generate_technical_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "general_answer",
        generate_general_answer,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify",
        route_question,
        {
            "technical": "technical_answer",
            "general": "general_answer",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "technical_answer",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "general_answer",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state: WorkflowState = {
        "question": "What is LangGraph?",
        "category": "",
        "answer": "",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH FUNDAMENTALS")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Category:", result["category"])
    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is LangGraph?

LangGraph is a low-level orchestration framework and runtime for building stateful, long-running AI agents and workflows. It represents execution through shared state, nodes, edges, conditional routing, and termination points.

Q2. What are the main building blocks of LangGraph?

The main building blocks are: State, Nodes, Edges, Conditional edges, Reducers, START, END, Checkpointers, Stores, The compiled graph.

Q3. What does a LangGraph node return?

A node normally returns a partial state update. LangGraph merges that update into the existing state according to the state schema and any reducers configured for the modified fields.

Q4. Why does LangGraph use shared state?

Shared state allows later nodes to access information produced by earlier nodes. It also enables routing, retries, persistence, human review, and continuation across a multi-step workflow.

Q5. When should LangGraph be used?

LangGraph should be used when an application requires custom stateful orchestration, conditional branches, loops, retries, human intervention, persistence, multi-agent coordination, or detailed execution control. For a simple fixed sequence or standard tool-calling loop, an ordinary function, LangChain Runnable, or LangChain agent may be sufficient.

Topic 2: Why LangGraph?

a) Definition

“Why LangGraph?” refers to the architectural problems that graph-based orchestration solves in complex AI systems. Simple chains work well when every request follows one fixed sequence, but real agents often need to branch, repeat steps, call different tools, preserve state, pause for approval, and recover from failures.

LangGraph provides explicit control over the application’s execution path. Developers define which nodes exist, what information they receive, how state is updated, which transitions are permitted, and under what conditions the workflow should terminate.

LangGraph makes complex agent behavior easier to inspect and govern. Named nodes and explicit edges expose the workflow structure instead of hiding all decisions inside one prompt, one agent executor, or one large Python function.

LangGraph provides infrastructure for stateful and long-running applications. Its capabilities include durable execution, memory, streaming, human-in-the-loop workflows, and integration with tracing and observability tools.


b) Purpose

The primary purpose of LangGraph is to provide reliable orchestration for AI applications whose execution path changes at runtime. A request may need retrieval, tool execution, validation, another model call, human approval, or safe termination depending on the current state.

LangGraph separates workflow control from model reasoning. The model may classify a request or propose an action, but deterministic application functions decide which nodes can execute, whether authorization passes, how many retries are allowed, and when the workflow must stop.

It supports persistent execution when a workflow cannot finish in one uninterrupted request. An application can save state, wait for external input or human approval, and later resume from the appropriate point instead of restarting the complete workflow.

It improves production reliability by making retries, fallbacks, limits, approval steps, and failure paths explicit. These behaviors become part of the graph rather than being scattered across nested conditions and exception handlers.


c) Problems LangGraph Solves

1. Conditional Routing

Different requests may require different processing paths. LangGraph conditional edges allow the current state to determine the next node. Without graph-based routing, developers may create deeply nested if statements that become difficult to debug and maintain.

2. Cycles and Iterative Workflows

Some workflows must repeat a step until a condition is satisfied. A loop is useful for: RAG retries, Reflection, Planning revisions, Tool-result validation
Error recovery, Multi-step research, Output correction. 
LangGraph can represent cycles directly through edges that return execution to an earlier node.

3. Shared State Management

Complex workflows need a consistent place to store intermediate information. Without structured state, information may be passed manually through several functions, prompts, global variables, or loosely defined dictionaries. LangGraph provides a clear state schema and controlled update mechanism.

4. Human-in-the-Loop

Some operations should not execute automatically. Human intervention is valuable for: Financial transactions, Sending emails, Database updates, Production deployments, Permission changes, Publishing content, Resolving conflicting evidence.

LangGraph supports controlled interruption and resumption for these workflows. Human-in-the-loop oversight is one of its central production capabilities.

5. Durable Execution

Long-running workflows may be interrupted by: Process restarts, Network failures, Model-provider outages, Tool timeouts, Human approval delays, Infrastructure deployment, Application crashes.

Durable execution allows the workflow to preserve progress and continue from a checkpoint.

6. Failure Recovery

A production workflow needs more than one generic exception handler. LangGraph makes recovery behavior visible in the workflow structure.

This allows separate handling for: Retryable failures, Permanent failures, Authorization failures, Missing information, Invalid model output
External provider outages.

7. Multi-Agent Coordination

A complex system may contain several specialized agents: Each agent can have: Its own state, Its own tools, Its own model, Its own responsibility, Controlled communication paths.

LangGraph supports single-agent, multi-agent, sequential, hierarchical, and custom graph structures through low-level orchestration primitives.

8. Streaming and Progress Visibility

A graph may contain several long-running steps: Classify, Retrieve, Analyze, Generate, Verify. LangGraph can expose execution updates so the application can show: Classifying request..., Retrieving evidence..., Analyzing documents..., Generating response..., Verifying citations...

Graph execution consists of named nodes whose status and streamed output can be exposed to a user interface.

d) Architecture Diagram

                    WHY LANGGRAPH?
                    ==============

                       User Request
                            |
                            v
                       Shared State
                            |
                            v
                     Classifier Node
                            |
          +-----------------+-----------------+
          |                 |                 |
          v                 v                 v
      RAG Node          Tool Node       Direct Model
          |                 |                 |
          v                 v                 v
    Verify Answer      Validate Tool      Check Output
          |              Result               |
          |                 |                 |
          +-----------------+-----------------+
                            |
                            v
                       Decision Node
                      /      |       \
                     /       |        \
                    v        v         v
                  Retry    Human      Complete
                           Review
                             |
                             v
                        Checkpoint
                             |
                             v
                           END


Architecture Explanation

The initial request is stored in shared state:- State provides one controlled place for input, intermediate results, counters, errors, decisions, and final output.

A routing node determines the required workflow path:- Different questions can use retrieval, tools, direct generation, or specialized agents.

Each path performs focused work:- Tool calls, retrieval operations, model calls, and deterministic checks remain separated into named nodes.

A decision node evaluates the result:- The workflow may complete, retry, use a fallback, or pause for human review.

State can be checkpointed:- The workflow can preserve progress and resume rather than repeating every operation.

e) Core Components

1. Explicit State

State makes intermediate information visible and structured. Developers can inspect the user question, tool results, retrieved documents, retry counters, and decision status at any execution stage. Structured state reduces hidden coupling between workflow steps. Each node declares which values it reads and returns only the fields it updates.

2. Conditional Edges

Conditional edges allow runtime routing based on state. The workflow can choose a tool, retriever, fallback, approval path, or termination route according to current conditions. Routing remains controlled by application code. The model may provide a classification, but the routing function maps that classification to an approved graph node.

3. Cycles

Cycles allow nodes to repeat when additional work is required. A failed verification can return to retrieval or generation instead of immediately returning a low-quality answer. Cycles must include strict termination conditions. Maximum attempts, budgets, time limits, or no-progress checks prevent infinite execution.

4. Persistence

Persistence saves graph state during execution. It supports workflow resumption, conversational continuity, approval pauses, and recovery after selected failures. Persistent workflows require a thread identifier and a suitable checkpointer. Different threads must remain isolated so one user’s state is not mixed with another workflow.

5. Interrupts

Interrupts pause execution when external information or human judgment is required. The current graph state can be inspected before the workflow continues. Resumption can include an approval, rejection, correction, or other structured input. The graph then continues from the paused execution point.

6. Observability

Named nodes and state transitions make execution easier to trace. Developers can identify which node ran, what it returned, and which routing decision followed. LangSmith integration provides visualization, tracing, debugging, evaluation, and runtime metrics for LangGraph applications.

f) Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class SupportState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    category: str
    attempts: int
    answer: str
    status: str


def classify_request(state: SupportState) -> dict:
    question = state["question"].lower()

    is_technical = (
        "error" in question
        or "api" in question
    )

    category = (
        "technical"
        if is_technical
        else "general"
    )

    return {
        "category": category,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_request(
    state: SupportState,
) -> Literal["technical", "general"]:
    return state["category"]


def handle_technical(
    state: SupportState,
) -> dict:
    attempts = state["attempts"] + 1

    if attempts == 1:
        return {
            "attempts": attempts,
            "answer": (
                "The first technical attempt "
                "needs more evidence."
            ),
            "status": "retry_required",
        }

    return {
        "attempts": attempts,
        "answer": (
            "Check the API endpoint, credentials, "
            "timeout, and server logs."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def route_after_technical(
    state: SupportState,
) -> Literal["retry", "finish"]:
    if state["status"] == "retry_required":
        return "retry"

    return "finish"


def handle_general(
    state: SupportState,
) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "Your request was handled through "
            "the general support path."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(SupportState)

    builder.add_node(
        "classify",
        classify_request,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "technical",
        handle_technical,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "general",
        handle_general,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify",
        route_request,
        {
            "technical": "technical",
            "general": "general",
        },
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "technical",
        route_after_technical,
        {
            "retry": "technical",
            "finish": END,
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "general",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    result = graph.invoke(
        {
            "question": (
                "Why is my API returning an error?"
            ),
            "category": "",
            "attempts": 0,
            "answer": "",
            "status": "pending",
        }
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("WHY LANGGRAPH EXAMPLE")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Category:", result["category"])
    print("Attempts:", result["attempts"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

g) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. Why do we need LangGraph?

LangGraph is needed when an AI application requires stateful execution, conditional routing, loops, persistence, human intervention, multi-agent coordination, or explicit failure recovery. A simple chain is usually insufficient for workflows whose execution path changes at runtime.

Q2. Why is LangGraph better than nested if statements?

Nested conditions can implement branching, but they become difficult to understand when the application includes state, loops, retries, persistence, streaming, and human approval. LangGraph represents these transitions explicitly as named nodes and edges, making the workflow easier to inspect, test, and trace.

Q3. Why is state important in LangGraph?

State preserves information across nodes and iterations. It allows later steps to use earlier model responses, tool results, retrieved evidence, retry counters, approval decisions, and error information.

Q4. Why are cycles useful in LangGraph?

Cycles allow a workflow to repeat retrieval, generation, validation, reflection, or tool execution when the first attempt is insufficient. Every cycle should include limits and a safe termination condition.

Q5. When should LangGraph not be used?

LangGraph may not be necessary for a short, fixed pipeline that does not need branching, loops, persistence, human input, or recovery. In those cases, a normal Python function, LangChain Runnable, or higher-level LangChain agent may be simpler.


Topic 3: LangGraph vs LangChain Agents

LangChain agents and LangGraph are complementary rather than competing technologies. LangChain provides a higher-level framework for quickly creating model-and-tool agents, while LangGraph provides lower-level orchestration primitives for custom, stateful, long-running workflows.

Current LangChain agents are built on top of LangGraph. Developers can therefore begin with the higher-level agent abstraction and move to direct LangGraph orchestration when they need custom execution paths, persistence, approval workflows, or fine-grained state control.

a) Definition

A LangChain agent is a higher-level abstraction that runs a language model in a loop and allows it to select and invoke tools. LangChain provides standardized integrations for models, tools, middleware, structured content, and common agent behavior, reducing the amount of orchestration code developers need to write.

LangGraph is a low-level orchestration framework and runtime for building customized stateful agents and workflows. Developers explicitly define state, nodes, edges, conditional routes, loops, interrupts, and persistence behavior.

LangChain agents provide an existing agent architecture, while LangGraph allows developers to design their own architecture. A LangChain agent is appropriate for standard model-tool loops, whereas LangGraph is appropriate when the execution lifecycle requires substantial customization.

LangChain agents use LangGraph internally, but developers do not need to understand LangGraph to use them. This allows teams to start with LangChain and adopt lower-level LangGraph features only when their requirements become more advanced.

b) Purpose of the Comparison

The purpose of this comparison is to determine the appropriate abstraction level for an agent application. Using LangGraph for a simple tool-calling assistant may create unnecessary complexity, while forcing a complex workflow into a standard agent loop may reduce control and maintainability.

The comparison helps distinguish agent capabilities from workflow orchestration. LangChain simplifies common agent functionality, while LangGraph provides explicit control over the sequence, state transitions, recovery paths, approval stages, and termination conditions.

It also prevents the incorrect assumption that developers must choose only one framework. LangChain models, tools, retrievers, prompts, and agents can be used as nodes or subgraphs inside a larger LangGraph application.

The choice affects development speed, customization, debugging, persistence, and operational responsibility. Higher-level abstractions reduce initial code, while lower-level orchestration provides greater flexibility but requires more workflow design.

                LANGCHAIN AGENT
                ===============

                    User Input

                         v
                 Prebuilt Agent Loop

                    +----+----+
                    |         |
                    v         v
                  Model      Tool
                    |         |
                    +----+----+
                         |

                  Final Response

==============================================

                  LANGGRAPH
                  =========

                    User Input
                         |
                         v
                     Shared State
                         |
                         v
                  Custom Classifier
                    /    |     \
                   /     |      \
                  v      v       v
                RAG     Tool   Direct Model
                  |      |       |
                  v      v       v
               Verify  Approve  Validate
                  |      |       |
                  +------+-------+
                         |
                         v
                        END 



Topic 4: Stateful Workflows

A stateful workflow preserves information as execution moves from one node to another. In LangGraph, state represents the current snapshot of the application, nodes read that state and return updates, and reducers determine how those updates are merged.

Stateful execution is essential for agents that must remember messages, retrieved evidence, tool results, decisions, retry counts, approvals, and errors throughout a multi-step workflow.

a. Definition

A stateful workflow is a workflow that preserves and updates information throughout its execution. Each node receives the current state, performs one operation, and returns a partial update that becomes available to later nodes.

The state represents the workflow’s current snapshot. It may contain the original request, intermediate results, execution counters, routing decisions, errors, and final response. LangGraph typically defines this structure using a shared state schema.

Nodes do not normally modify the state directly. They return dictionaries containing the fields they want to update, and LangGraph applies those updates according to each field’s reducer.

State can also be persisted beyond one uninterrupted execution. Checkpointers preserve thread-specific state snapshots for conversation continuity, human-in-the-loop workflows, fault tolerance, and execution resumption.

b. Purpose

State allows later nodes to use information produced by earlier nodes. A generation node can access documents returned by a retrieval node, while a validation node can inspect the generated answer and its supporting evidence.

State supports conditional routing and loops. A routing function can inspect fields such as status, retry_count, or approval_required and decide whether execution should continue, repeat, pause, or terminate.

State makes workflow behavior observable. Developers can inspect which fields existed before a node ran, which updates the node returned, and how those updates influenced the next route.

Persisted state supports workflows that span several requests or interruptions. A workflow can save a checkpoint, wait for human input or recover from failure, and continue using the same thread_id.

c. Architecture Diagram

                     STATEFUL WORKFLOW
                     =================

                        Initial Input
                             |
                             v
                         Graph State
          Question | Evidence | Attempts | Answer
                             |
                             v
                        Retrieval Node
                             |
                   Returns partial update
                             |
                             v
                        Updated State
                             |
                             v
                       Validation Node
                             |
                 +-----------+-----------+
                 |                       |
             Sufficient              Insufficient
                 |                       |
                 v                       v
          Generation Node          Increment Attempt
                 |                       |
                 |                       v
                 |                 Retrieval Node
                 |                       |
                 +-----------+-----------+
                             |
                             v
                         Final State
                             |
                             v
                            END

Architecture Explanation

The graph is invoked with an initial state.
 The input must contain the fields required by the state schema or defaults expected by the workflow.

A node receives the current state.
 It can read any field defined in the schema.

The node returns a partial state update.
 It does not need to return fields that remain unchanged.

Reducers merge the update into the existing state.
 By default, a new value replaces the previous value. A custom reducer can append, combine, or resolve concurrent updates.

Conditional edges inspect the updated state.
 They determine whether the workflow should continue, repeat a node, follow another branch, or reach END.

A checkpointer can save state snapshots.
 This allows the same workflow thread to continue across invocations or interruptions.

d) Code Example

from operator import add
from typing import Annotated, Literal, TypedDict

from langgraph.checkpoint.memory import InMemorySaver
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class ResearchState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    evidence: Annotated[list[str], add]
    attempts: int
    status: str
    answer: str


KNOWLEDGE_BASE = [
    "LangGraph represents workflows using state, nodes, and edges.",
    "Reducers control how node updates are merged into graph state.",
]


def retrieve_evidence(state: ResearchState) -> dict:
    attempt = state["attempts"] + 1

    if attempt <= len(KNOWLEDGE_BASE):
        evidence = [KNOWLEDGE_BASE[attempt - 1]]
    else:
        evidence = []

    total_evidence = len(state["evidence"]) + len(evidence)

    status = (
        "ready"
        if total_evidence >= 2
        else "searching"
    )

    return {
        "evidence": evidence,
        "attempts": attempt,
        "status": status,
    }


def route_after_retrieval(
    state: ResearchState,
) -> Literal["retrieve_again", "generate", "stop"]:
    if state["status"] == "ready":
        return "generate"

    if state["attempts"] >= 3:
        return "stop"

    return "retrieve_again"


def generate_answer(state: ResearchState) -> dict:
    combined_evidence = " ".join(state["evidence"])

    return {
        "answer": (
            f"LangGraph is stateful because nodes share "
            f"and update graph state. {combined_evidence}"
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def stop_without_answer(state: ResearchState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The workflow stopped because sufficient "
            "evidence was not found."
        ),
        "status": "stopped",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(ResearchState)

    builder.add_node(
        "retrieve",
        retrieve_evidence,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "generate",
        generate_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "stop",
        stop_without_answer,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "retrieve",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "retrieve",
        route_after_retrieval,
        {
            "retrieve_again": "retrieve",
            "generate": "generate",
            "stop": "stop",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "generate",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "stop",
        END,
    )

    checkpointer = InMemorySaver()

    return builder.compile(
        checkpointer=checkpointer
    )


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    config = {
        "configurable": {
            "thread_id": "research-thread-101",
        }
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        {
            "question": (
                "How does LangGraph manage state?"
            ),
            "evidence": [],
            "attempts": 0,
            "status": "pending",
            "answer": "",
        },
        config=config,
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("STATEFUL LANGGRAPH WORKFLOW")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Attempts:", result["attempts"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Evidence:")

    for item in result["evidence"]:
        print("-", item)

    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

e) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is state in LangGraph?

State is the shared data structure representing the current snapshot of a LangGraph application. It contains the values that nodes read, update, and use for routing decisions.

Q2. What does a LangGraph node return?

A node normally returns a partial state update as a dictionary. LangGraph merges the returned fields into the existing state according to the configured reducers.

Q3. What is a reducer in LangGraph?

A reducer defines how a new update is combined with an existing state value. Without a custom reducer, the new value normally replaces the old value. A reducer such as operator.add can accumulate list values.

Q4. What is the difference between a checkpointer and a store?

A checkpointer persists graph-state snapshots for one thread and supports continuity, interruption, time travel, and fault recovery. A store saves application-defined information that may be accessed across different threads, such as persistent user preferences or shared knowledge.

Q5. Why is thread_id important?

The thread_id identifies which persisted workflow state belongs to a particular conversation or continuing execution. Invoking the graph with the same thread ID allows the configured checkpointer to retrieve the appropriate thread-specific checkpoints.

Topic 5: Graph-Based Execution

Graph-Based Execution means representing an application as a collection of executable nodes connected through fixed or conditional edges. Nodes perform work, shared state carries data, and edges determine which operation executes next.

LangGraph uses a message-passing execution model inspired by Pregel. Execution proceeds through discrete super-steps, where eligible nodes may run in parallel during the same super-step, while dependent nodes execute in later super-steps.

a) Definition

Graph-Based Execution represents workflow logic as an executable directed graph. Each node performs a defined operation, while each edge describes a permitted transition between operations.

Nodes receive the current graph state and return partial state updates. A node may call a model, retrieve documents, execute a tool, validate an answer, perform a calculation, or run ordinary Python logic.

Edges control execution flow. Fixed edges represent predictable transitions, while conditional edges choose the next node dynamically by examining the updated state.

The graph can represent sequential, branching, looping, and parallel workflows. This makes graph execution suitable for complex applications that cannot be represented efficiently as one linear chain.

b) Purpose

Graph-Based Execution makes complex control flow explicit. Developers can visually and programmatically understand which nodes exist, which transitions are allowed, and where execution can branch, repeat, or terminate.

It separates processing logic from routing logic. Nodes focus on performing work, while edges and routing functions focus on deciding what should happen next.

It supports dynamic execution based on runtime state. The same graph can take different paths depending on the user’s request, retrieved evidence, validation results, authorization status, retry count, or tool outcome.

It improves testing, debugging, and observability. Individual nodes and routing functions can be tested independently, while execution traces show which path the graph followed for a particular request.

c) Architecture Diagram

                    GRAPH-BASED EXECUTION
                    =====================

                         Initial Input
                              |
                              v
                            START
                              |
                              v
                      Classification Node
                              |
                +-------------+-------------+
                |                           |
                v                           v
         Knowledge Request           Calculation Request
                |                           |
                v                           v
         Retrieval Node              Calculator Node
                |                           |
                v                           |
         Evidence Check                     |
             /       \                      |
            /         \                     |
           v           v                    |
      Sufficient   Insufficient             |
           |           |                    |
           v           v                    |
      Generate      Retrieve Again          |
           |           |                    |
           +-----------+--------------------+
                              |
                              v
                         Final Response
                              |
                              v
                             END


Architecture Explanation

Execution starts from START. START is a virtual entry point that connects the initial graph input to the first executable node.

The classification node reads the state. It identifies which workflow path is suitable for the current request.

A conditional edge selects a branch. Knowledge questions proceed to retrieval, while calculation requests proceed to a deterministic calculation node.

The retrieval branch can contain a cycle. If the evidence is insufficient and the retry limit has not been reached, execution returns to retrieval.

The branches eventually converge. Both paths produce a final answer and transition to END.

The final state is returned. It includes the original request, selected route, intermediate results, counters, status, and final answer.

d) Core Components

1. Node

A node is an executable function or Runnable that performs one focused operation. It receives the current state and may call models, tools, retrievers, databases, APIs, or ordinary Python functions. A node should return state updates instead of directly mutating the state. Returning explicit updates makes data flow easier to test and trace.

2. Fixed Edge

A fixed edge creates an unconditional transition between two nodes. When the source node completes, the destination node becomes eligible to run. Fixed edges are suitable for predictable execution sequences. Examples include retrieval followed by reranking or generation followed by output validation.

3. Conditional Edge

A conditional edge uses a routing function to choose the next node according to current state. The routing function returns a route value mapped to a destination.

Conditional edges support branching, retry loops, fallback, escalation, and dynamic termination. Routing values should be stable and every possible value should map to a valid node or END.

4. Super-Step

A super-step is one iteration of LangGraph’s message-passing execution model. Nodes that can run concurrently are executed in the same super-step. State updates are coordinated between super-steps. Nodes whose inputs depend on earlier operations become active during a later super-step.

5. Compiled Graph

The compiled graph is the executable version of the graph definition. Before execution, developers define the state, register nodes, and connect edges using StateGraph. Compilation performs structural checks and accepts runtime capabilities such as checkpointers and breakpoints. A graph must be compiled before it can be invoked.

e) Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class ExecutionState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    category: str
    attempts: int
    evidence: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def classify_request(state: ExecutionState) -> dict:
    question = state["question"].lower()

    if "calculate" in question or "total" in question:
        category = "calculation"
    else:
        category = "knowledge"

    return {
        "category": category,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_request(
    state: ExecutionState,
) -> Literal["knowledge", "calculation"\]:
    return state["category"]


def retrieve_evidence(state: ExecutionState) -> dict:
    attempts = state["attempts"] + 1

    if attempts == 1:
        return {
            "attempts": attempts,
            "evidence": "",
            "status": "insufficient",
        }

    return {
        "attempts": attempts,
        "evidence": (
            "LangGraph executes workflows using "
            "state nodes, and edges."
        ),
        "status": "sufficient",
    }


def route_after_retrieval(
    state: ExecutionState,
) -> Literal["retry", "generate", "stop"]:
    if state["status"] == "sufficient":
        return "generate"

    if state["attempts"] >= 2:
        return "stop"

    return "retry"


def generate_knowledge_answer(
    state: ExecutionState,
) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": state["evidence"],
        "status": "completed",
    }


def calculate_answer(state: ExecutionState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": "The calculated total is 100.",
        "status": "completed",
    }


def stop_workflow(state: ExecutionState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The workflow stopped because sufficient "
            "evidence was not found."
        ),
        "status": "stopped",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(ExecutionState)

    builder.add_node("classify", classify_request)
    builder.add_node("retrieve", retrieve_evidence)
    builder.add_node("generate", generate_knowledge_answer)
    builder.add_node("calculate", calculate_answer)
    builder.add_node("stop", stop_workflow)

    builder.add_edge(START, "classify")

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify",
        route_request,
        {
            "knowledge": "retrieve",
            "calculation": "calculate",
        },
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "retrieve",
        route_after_retrieval,
        {
            "retry": "retrieve",
            "generate": "generate",
            "stop": "stop",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge("generate", END)
    builder.add_edge("calculate", END)
    builder.add_edge("stop", END)

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    result = graph.invoke(
        {
            "question": "How does LangGraph execute workflows?",
            "category": "",
            "attempts": 0,
            "evidence": "",
            "answer": "",
            "status": "pending",
        }
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("GRAPH-BASED EXECUTION")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Category:", result["category"])
    print("Attempts:", result["attempts"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Graph-Based Execution in LangGraph?

Graph-Based Execution represents application logic using state, nodes, and edges. Nodes perform work, while fixed or conditional edges determine which node runs next according to the current state.

Q2. What is the difference between a fixed edge and a conditional edge?

A fixed edge always routes execution from one node to one predefined destination. A conditional edge invokes a routing function that examines state and dynamically chooses the next destination.

Q3. What is a super-step in LangGraph?

A super-step is one iteration of LangGraph’s message-passing execution model. Nodes that are eligible to run in parallel participate in the same super-step, while dependent nodes run during later super-steps.

Q4. How does a LangGraph workflow terminate?

A workflow normally terminates after its execution path reaches END. At the execution-engine level, processing finishes when all nodes are inactive and no messages remain in transit.

Q5. How can infinite loops be prevented?

Use maximum iteration counts, timeouts, cost budgets, duplicate-action detection, no-progress detection, and explicit stop or escalation nodes. Routing should always provide a controlled termination path when another iteration is not allowed.

Topic 6: LangGraph Architecture

LangGraph Architecture defines how state, nodes, edges, reducers, runtime configuration, persistence, and execution work together to create a stateful application. Its core Graph API uses three fundamental elements: shared state represents the current application snapshot, nodes perform work and return state updates, and edges determine which nodes execute next.

a. Definition

LangGraph Architecture is the structural design used to define and execute graph-based AI workflows. The workflow is represented through a state schema, processing nodes, fixed or conditional edges, and explicit entry and termination points.

StateGraph is the main graph-building abstraction. It is parameterized by a developer-defined state schema, which controls the data that nodes can read and update during execution.

The compiled graph acts as the application runtime. Developers first define state, register nodes, connect edges, and then call compile() before invoking or streaming the workflow. Compilation also accepts runtime capabilities such as checkpointers and breakpoints.

LangGraph execution follows a message-passing model. Nodes receive state through incoming edges, execute their logic, return updates, and activate downstream nodes. Eligible nodes may run in the same super-step, while dependent nodes run in later super-steps.


b. Purpose

The primary purpose of LangGraph Architecture is to separate workflow data, processing logic, and execution control. State stores information, nodes perform focused operations, and edges manage transitions, making the application easier to understand and maintain.

The architecture supports non-linear workflows. Applications can contain branches, loops, parallel operations, retries, human-review paths, fallbacks, and several termination conditions.

It provides a controlled boundary between deterministic logic and model-driven behavior. Language models may classify requests or generate responses, while routing, authorization, budgets, and execution limits remain controlled by application code.

It provides production capabilities around the application graph. Checkpointing, persistence, streaming, interrupts, runtime configuration, and observability can be attached to the compiled graph without placing all infrastructure logic inside individual nodes.

c. Architecture Diagram

                     LANGGRAPH ARCHITECTURE
                     ======================

                         Graph Input
                              |
                              v
                        Input Schema
                              |
                              v
                            START
                              |
                              v
                         StateGraph
                              |
             +----------------+----------------+
             |                |                |
             v                v                v
        Model Node       Retrieval Node     Tool Node
             |                |                |
             +----------------+----------------+
                              |
                              v
                       State Updates
                              |
                              v
                           Reducers
                              |
                              v
                    Updated State Snapshot
                              |
                              v
                    Routing and Edge Layer
                  /           |            \
                 /            |             \
                v             v              v
            Next Node      Parallel Nodes    END
                |
                v
                       Runtime Execution
                |
        +-------+--------+----------+----------+
        |                |          |          |
        v                v          v          v
   Checkpointer        Store     Streaming   Interrupts
        |                |          |          |
        +----------------+----------+----------+
                         |
                         v
                    Final Graph State

Architecture Explanation

The graph receives input according to its input schema. By default, the state schema also determines the graph’s input and output structure, although separate schemas can be defined when required.

Execution begins at START. An edge from START activates the first application node.

Nodes read the current state and perform work. A node may invoke a model, retrieve documents, call an API, execute a tool, validate an answer, or run deterministic Python logic.

Nodes return partial state updates. LangGraph applies those updates according to each state field’s reducer.

Edges select the next execution step. Fixed edges define predictable transitions, while conditional edges inspect state and select a route dynamically.

The runtime executes activated nodes through super-steps. Independent nodes may execute within the same super-step, while nodes depending on their results execute later.

Optional runtime services extend graph behavior. Checkpointers save thread state, stores maintain application-defined long-term information, streaming exposes progress, and interrupts allow controlled pauses.

Execution reaches END and returns the final state.


d) Detailed Architecture Components

1. State Schema

The state schema defines the shared communication structure used by graph nodes. Each key acts as a channel through which nodes read existing values and return updates.

A clear schema improves type safety and reduces hidden dependencies. Explicit fields such as documents, retry_count, and approval_status are easier to maintain than one generic nested dictionary.

2. Nodes

Nodes contain the application’s processing logic. They receive the current state and return updates after performing calculations, model calls, retrieval, tools, or validation.

Nodes should remain focused and independently testable. A workflow is easier to debug when retrieval, generation, verification, and authorization are separate nodes.

3. Edges

Edges define permitted transitions between nodes. Fixed edges represent unconditional execution, while conditional edges use state-dependent routing. Edges separate workflow control from node processing logic. This makes branches, loops, retries, fallbacks, and termination visible in the graph structure.

4. Reducers

Reducers determine how returned node updates are merged into the existing state. They can replace values, append list items, merge messages, or apply another field-specific strategy.

Reducers are especially important for loops and parallel branches. Without an appropriate reducer, concurrent updates to one field may conflict or overwrite each other.

5. Compiled Runtime

The compiled runtime converts the graph definition into an executable application. It coordinates activation, state transfer, update merging, routing, and termination.

Runtime features can be configured during compilation. These may include checkpointers, stores, breakpoints, and other execution services required by the application. 

6. Persistence Services

A checkpointer saves state snapshots so one workflow thread can continue across invocations or interruptions. It enables durable execution and human-review workflows. A store saves application-defined data outside a single thread’s state. It is suitable for long-term memory and information shared across sessions.

e) Code Example

from operator import add
from typing import Annotated, Literal, TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class ArchitectureState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    category: str
    evidence: Annotated[list[str], add]
    attempts: int
    answer: str
    status: str


KNOWLEDGE_BASE = {
    "langgraph": [
        "LangGraph uses state, nodes, and edges.",
        "LangGraph supports conditional and cyclic workflows.",
    ]
}


def classify_question(state: ArchitectureState) -> dict:
    question = state["question"].lower()

    category = (
        "langgraph"
        if "langgraph" in question
        else "unknown"
    )

    return {
        "category": category,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_category(
    state: ArchitectureState,
) -> Literal["retrieve", "unsupported"\]:
    if state["category"] == "langgraph":
        return "retrieve"

    return "unsupported"


def retrieve_evidence(state: ArchitectureState) -> dict:
    attempt = state["attempts"] + 1
    facts = KNOWLEDGE_BASE.get(state["category"], [])

    if attempt <= len(facts):
        new_evidence = [facts[attempt - 1]]
    else:
        new_evidence = []

    total = len(state["evidence"]) + len(new_evidence)

    status = (
        "ready"
        if total >= 2
        else "searching"
    )

    return {
        "evidence": new_evidence,
        "attempts": attempt,
        "status": status,
    }


def route_after_retrieval(
    state: ArchitectureState,
) -> Literal["retry", "generate", "stop"\]:
    if state["status"] == "ready":
        return "generate"

    if state["attempts"] >= 3:
        return "stop"

    return "retry"


def generate_answer(state: ArchitectureState) -> dict:
    answer = " ".join(state["evidence"])

    return {
        "answer": answer,
        "status": "completed",
    }


def unsupported_answer(state: ArchitectureState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The workflow does not contain knowledge "
            "for this category."
        ),
        "status": "unsupported",
    }


def stop_workflow(state: ArchitectureState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The workflow stopped because sufficient "
            "evidence was not found."
        ),
        "status": "stopped",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(ArchitectureState)

    builder.add_node("classify", classify_question)
    builder.add_node("retrieve", retrieve_evidence)
    builder.add_node("generate", generate_answer)
    builder.add_node("unsupported", unsupported_answer)
    builder.add_node("stop", stop_workflow)

    builder.add_edge(START, "classify")

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify",
        route_category,
        {
            "retrieve": "retrieve",
            "unsupported": "unsupported",
        },
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "retrieve",
        route_after_retrieval,
        {
            "retry": "retrieve",
            "generate": "generate",
            "stop": "stop",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge("generate", END)
    builder.add_edge("unsupported", END)
    builder.add_edge("stop", END)

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    result = graph.invoke(
        {
            "question": "How is LangGraph architected?",
            "category": "",
            "evidence": [],
            "attempts": 0,
            "answer": "",
            "status": "pending",
        }
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH ARCHITECTURE")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Category:", result["category"])
    print("Attempts:", result["attempts"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Evidence:")

    for fact in result["evidence"]:
        print("-", fact)

    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

f) Code Explanation

ArchitectureState:- The state schema defines all values shared by the workflow: question, category, evidence, attempts, answer, status. The evidence field uses operator.add, so each retrieval update is accumulated rather than replacing earlier facts.

Classification Node:- classify_question() identifies which knowledge category should be used. It returns only the fields it changes.

Category Router:-  route_category() maps the classification to either: Retrieval, Unsupported response. The router does not retrieve or generate anything itself.

Retrieval Node:- retrieve_evidence() returns one new fact for each attempt. The reducer adds that fact to the existing evidence list.

Retrieval Router:- route_after_retrieval() creates three possible transitions: Retry retrieval, Generate the answer, Stop safely.

Compilation:- builder.compile() converts the graph definition into an executable application. LangGraph documentation states that compilation performs structural checks and is required before using the graph.

g) Interview Questions And Answers

Q1. What are the main architectural elements of LangGraph?

The main elements are state, nodes, edges, conditional routing, reducers, START, END, the compiled runtime, checkpointers, stores, and streaming. State stores information, nodes perform work, and edges determine what executes next.

Q2. What is the role of StateGraph?

StateGraph is the primary graph-building class. It is parameterized by a state schema and is used to register nodes, fixed edges, conditional edges, and termination paths before compilation.

Q3. Why is graph compilation required?

Compilation converts the graph definition into an executable application. It performs structural checks and is where runtime services such as checkpointers and breakpoints can be configured.

Q4. How does the LangGraph runtime execute nodes?

The runtime uses message passing. A node becomes active when it receives state through an incoming edge, executes its function, and returns updates to downstream nodes. Eligible parallel nodes run in the same super-step, while dependent nodes execute in later super-steps.

Q5. What is the difference between a checkpointer and a store?

A checkpointer persists thread-specific graph snapshots for continuity, interruption, and recovery. A store retains application-defined long-term data that may be shared across several workflow threads.

Topic 7: Execution Lifecycle

The LangGraph execution lifecycle describes what happens from the moment a graph receives input until it returns its final state. The lifecycle includes state initialization, node activation, node execution, update merging, edge evaluation, checkpointing, streaming, and termination.

LangGraph uses a message-passing model inspired by Pregel. Execution proceeds in discrete super-steps: independent nodes may run during the same super-step, while nodes that depend on prior results execute during later super-steps. The graph terminates when all nodes are inactive and no messages remain in transit.

a. Definition

The execution lifecycle is the complete sequence through which a compiled LangGraph application processes an input. It begins when the graph is invoked or streamed and ends when execution reaches END, is interrupted, or fails with an unhandled error.

The lifecycle is state-driven. Each active node receives the current state, performs its operation, and returns a partial update that LangGraph merges into the state before evaluating the next transition.

Execution follows the graph topology. Fixed edges move execution to predefined nodes, while conditional edges inspect updated state and dynamically select the next path.

The lifecycle can include persistence and resumption. When compiled with a checkpointer, LangGraph can save thread-scoped state snapshots that support conversation continuity, human review, fault recovery, and continued execution.


b. Purpose

The execution lifecycle provides a predictable model for understanding how a LangGraph workflow runs. It explains when nodes receive state, when updates become visible, and when routing functions are evaluated.

It helps developers debug workflow behavior. If a node did not execute, the lifecycle reveals whether it was never activated, a conditional route selected another branch, or an earlier node failed.

It supports safe loops and recovery paths. Retry counters, status values, maximum iterations, and error results can be stored in state and evaluated before another node is activated.

It enables production features such as checkpointing, streaming, human intervention, and observability. These capabilities are associated with specific lifecycle stages rather than being independent of graph execution.


c. Execution Life Cycle Diagram

                    LANGGRAPH EXECUTION LIFECYCLE
                    =============================

                         Define State
                              |
                              v
                       Create StateGraph
                              |
                              v
                     Register Nodes and Edges
                              |
                              v
                         Compile Graph
                              |
                              v
                      Invoke or Stream Graph
                              |
                              v
                   Initialize State and Runtime
                              |
                              v
                         Activate START
                              |
                              v
                     Execute Active Node
                              |
                              v
                  Return Partial State Update
                              |
                              v
                    Apply Reducers to State
                              |
                              v
                     Save Checkpoint if Used
                              |
                              v
                    Evaluate Outgoing Edges
                              |
               +--------------+--------------+
               |              |              |
               v              v              v
          Execute Next     Run Parallel     Interrupt
             Node             Nodes           Graph
               |              |              |
               +--------------+--------------+
                              |
                              v
                    Continue Through Steps
                              |
               +--------------+--------------+
               |                             |
               v                             v
             END                       Unhandled Error
               |                             |
               v                             v
       Return Final State             Execution Fails

Architecture Explanation

The developer defines the application state:- The state schema specifies which values move through the graph and how updates are merged.
Nodes and edges are registered with StateGraph:- Nodes contain processing logic, while edges define possible transitions.
The graph is compiled:- Compilation performs structural checks and creates the executable graph. Runtime services such as checkpointers can also be supplied during compilation.
The graph is invoked or streamed with initial input:- The runtime initializes the graph state and activates nodes connected to START.
Active nodes execute and return state updates:- Nodes should return updates instead of directly mutating the state.
Reducers merge updates into the current state:- The resulting state snapshot is used by later routing functions and nodes.
Outgoing edges are evaluated:- The graph may continue sequentially, activate parallel nodes, enter a loop, pause at an interrupt, or terminate at END.
Execution returns the final state:- If streaming is used, intermediate updates may also be exposed while the workflow runs.


d) Core Components

1 Initial State

The initial state contains the input values required to begin graph execution. It should include all fields needed by the first node or provide valid initial values for those fields.

Initial state should be validated at the application boundary. Missing or malformed identifiers, counters, and request metadata should not flow deeply into the graph before detection.

2. Active Node

An active node is a node selected to execute during the current super-step. It becomes active after receiving a message or state through an incoming edge. The active node reads state and returns updates. It should not manually invoke its downstream node because edge evaluation belongs to the graph runtime.

3. State Update

A state update is the partial result returned by a node. It contains only the fields that the node creates or modifies. Updates are merged according to state reducers. Replacement is appropriate for current status, while accumulation may be appropriate for messages, evidence, or audit events.

4. Routing Decision

A routing decision selects the next destination after a node finishes. It may be represented by a fixed edge or a conditional routing function. Routing should be deterministic at the graph-control level. A model can produce a category, but application code should map that category to approved destination nodes.

5. Checkpoint

A checkpoint stores a state snapshot for a workflow thread. It allows the application to inspect, continue, or resume the workflow from persisted state. Checkpoint storage requires appropriate security and lifecycle controls. State may contain user messages, retrieved evidence, model output, and tool results.

6. Terminal Condition

A terminal condition determines when no additional workflow node should execute. It may be a transition to END, a stop state, an interrupt, or an unrecoverable failure. Every loop requires a reachable terminal condition. Maximum iterations, cost budgets, no-progress detection, and timeouts prevent uncontrolled execution.

e) Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict
from langgraph.checkpoint.memory import InMemorySaver
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class LifecycleState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    attempts: int
    evidence: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def retrieve_evidence(state: LifecycleState) -> dict:
    attempts = state["attempts"] + 1

    if attempts == 1:
        return {
            "attempts": attempts,
            "evidence": "",
            "status": "retry_required",
        }

    return {
        "attempts": attempts,
        "evidence": (
            "LangGraph activates nodes, applies state "
            "updates, evaluates edges, and continues "
            "until the workflow reaches END."
        ),
        "status": "evidence_ready",
    }


def route_after_retrieval(
    state: LifecycleState,
) -> Literal["retry", "generate", "stop"]:
    if state["status"] == "evidence_ready":
        return "generate"

    if state["attempts"] >= 2:
        return "stop"

    return "retry"


def generate_answer(state: LifecycleState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": state["evidence"],
        "status": "completed",
    }


def stop_workflow(state: LifecycleState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The workflow stopped because sufficient "
            "evidence was not available."
        ),
        "status": "stopped",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(LifecycleState)

    builder.add_node("retrieve", retrieve_evidence)
    builder.add_node("generate", generate_answer)
    builder.add_node("stop", stop_workflow)

    builder.add_edge(START, "retrieve")

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "retrieve",
        route_after_retrieval,
        {
            "retry": "retrieve",
            "generate": "generate",
            "stop": "stop",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge("generate", END)
    builder.add_edge("stop", END)

    checkpointer = InMemorySaver()

    return builder.compile(
        checkpointer=checkpointer
    )


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    config = {
        "configurable": {
            "thread_id": "lifecycle-thread-101",
        }
    }

    initial_state: LifecycleState = {
        "question": (
            "What happens during LangGraph execution?"
        ),
        "attempts": 0,
        "evidence": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state,
        config=config,
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH EXECUTION LIFECYCLE")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Attempts:", result["attempts"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Evidence:", result["evidence"])
    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is the LangGraph execution lifecycle?

The execution lifecycle is the complete process through which a compiled graph accepts initial state, activates nodes, applies returned updates, evaluates edges, persists checkpoints when configured, and continues until termination.

Q2. What happens after a node returns an update?

LangGraph merges the returned fields into the existing state according to the configured reducers. It then evaluates the node’s outgoing fixed or conditional edges to determine which nodes should become active next.

Q3. What is a super-step?

A super-step is one iteration of LangGraph’s message-passing execution model. Independent active nodes may execute during the same super-step, while nodes depending on their results execute in later super-steps.

Q4. What is the difference between invoke() and stream()?

invoke() runs the graph and returns the final state after completion. stream() exposes intermediate execution events or updates while the graph is running, making it suitable for progress displays and long-running workflows.

Q5. When does a LangGraph workflow terminate?

A workflow normally terminates when its selected execution path reaches END. At the runtime level, execution finishes when no nodes remain active and no messages are in transit.


Chapter 2: StateGraph


Topic 1: StateGraph Fundamentals

StateGraph is the primary builder used to create graph-based workflows in LangGraph. Its nodes communicate through shared state: every node reads the current state and returns a partial update, while edges determine which node executes next. A StateGraph must be compiled before it can be invoked or streamed.

a. Definition

StateGraph is a graph builder designed for workflows whose nodes communicate through shared state. The state represents the current application snapshot and may contain user input, messages, retrieved documents, tool results, counters, decisions, statuses, and final output.

Each node follows the general pattern State -> Partial<State>. A node receives the current state, performs one focused operation, and returns only the state fields it wants to create or update.

Edges define the graph’s execution flow. Fixed edges create predictable transitions, while conditional edges inspect the updated state and dynamically choose the next node.

StateGraph itself is only a builder and cannot be executed directly. Calling compile() creates a CompiledStateGraph that supports synchronous, asynchronous, streaming, and batch execution interfaces.

b. Purpose

StateGraph provides a structured way to coordinate multiple processing steps through shared application data. A retrieval node can add documents to state, a generation node can read those documents, and a verification node can inspect the generated answer.

It separates processing logic from workflow routing. Nodes focus on performing work, while edges and routing functions control sequencing, branching, loops, fallbacks, and termination.

StateGraph supports non-linear workflows that ordinary sequential pipelines cannot represent cleanly. Applications can repeat retrieval, branch according to request type, route failures to recovery nodes, or pause for human review.

It creates an executable and observable workflow structure. Named nodes, explicit state fields, and controlled transitions make the application easier to test, trace, debug, and maintain.


c. StateGraph Architecture

                       STATEGRAPH
                       ==========

                      Initial Input
                           |
                           v
                         START
                           |
                           v
                    Shared Graph State
                           |
                           v
                    Classification Node
                           |
               +-----------+-----------+
               |                       |
               v                       v
         Knowledge Route         General Route
               |                       |
               v                       v
         Retrieval Node          Response Node
               |
               v
         Generation Node
               |
               +-----------+-----------+
                           |
                           v
                          END
                           |
                           v
                      Final State

The graph receives an initial state. The initial values must conform to the state schema expected by the workflow.

Execution begins from START. START is a virtual entry point connected to the first executable node.

The active node reads the current state. It may use one field, several fields, or the complete state.

The node returns a partial update. State fields not returned by the node remain unchanged.
