LangGraph applies the update to shared state. The field’s reducer determines whether the new value replaces, appends to, or otherwise combines with the existing value.

An edge selects the next node. This may be a fixed transition or a conditional routing decision.

Execution continues until a terminal path reaches END. The compiled graph then returns the final state.


d. Core Components

1. State Schema

The state schema declares the fields shared across the workflow. It defines the names and expected types of values that nodes can read and update. It provides a common communication contract between nodes. Nodes do not need separate custom argument chains because shared workflow information remains available through state.

2. Nodes

Nodes contain the graph’s processing logic. They may perform deterministic calculations, model calls, retrieval, tool execution, validation, database queries, or API operations. Nodes should return partial state updates instead of mutating the supplied state directly. This makes their effects explicit and easier to test.

3. Fixed Edges

A fixed edge creates an unconditional transition between two nodes. Whenever the source node completes, the destination node becomes eligible to execute. Fixed edges are appropriate when workflow order is known in advance. Examples include retrieval followed by generation or generation followed by validation.

4. Conditional Edges

A conditional edge chooses the next node according to current state. A routing function returns a stable label mapped to a registered destination. Conditional edges support branching, loops, retries, fallback, escalation, and dynamic termination. Every possible route should map to a valid graph destination.

5. Reducers

Reducers determine how new node updates are combined with existing state values. The general reducer pattern is (existing_value, new_value) -> merged_value. Without a custom reducer, a new value generally replaces the previous value. Custom reducers can accumulate evidence, messages, audit events, or results received from parallel nodes.

6. Compiled Graph

The compiled graph is the executable form of a StateGraph. It supports methods such as invoke(), stream(), ainvoke(), and astream(). Compilation is also the point where runtime services are attached. These can include checkpointers, stores, caches, interrupts, debugging options, and a graph name.

e. Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    category: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def classify_question(state: WorkflowState) -> dict:
    question = state["question"].lower()

    technical_terms = (
        "langgraph",
        "python",
        "workflow",
        "stategraph",
    )

    is_technical = any(
        term in question
        for term in technical_terms
    )

    category = (
        "technical"
        if is_technical
        else "general"
    )

    return {
        "category": category,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_question(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> Literal["technical", "general"]:
    return state["category"]


def technical_answer(state: WorkflowState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "StateGraph builds workflows whose nodes "
            "communicate through shared state."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def general_answer(state: WorkflowState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request was processed through "
            "the general workflow."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(WorkflowState)

    builder.add_node(
        "classify",
        classify_question,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "technical_answer",
        technical_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "general_answer",
        general_answer,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify",
        route_question,
        {
            "technical": "technical_answer",
            "general": "general_answer",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "technical_answer",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "general_answer",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state: WorkflowState = {
        "question": "What is StateGraph in LangGraph?",
        "category": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("STATEGRAPH FUNDAMENTALS")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Category:", result["category"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

e) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is StateGraph in LangGraph?

StateGraph is the main graph-building class for workflows whose nodes communicate through shared state. Nodes read the current state and return partial updates, while edges determine which nodes execute next.

Q2. Can a StateGraph builder be invoked directly?

No. StateGraph is a builder and must first be compiled. The resulting CompiledStateGraph supports invocation, streaming, batching, and asynchronous execution.

Q3. What is the difference between fixed and conditional edges?

A fixed edge always routes execution to one predefined node. A conditional edge evaluates the current state and chooses a destination dynamically through a routing function.

Q4. Why are reducers important in StateGraph?

Reducers control how new node updates are combined with existing state values. They are especially important when values must accumulate or when several parallel nodes update the same state field.

Q5. What is the node signature in StateGraph?

Conceptually, a node follows: State -> Partial State. It receives the current state and returns a dictionary containing the fields it wants to update.

Topic 2: Defining State

State defines the information that flows through a LangGraph workflow. It acts as the communication contract between nodes: each node receives the current state, performs an operation, and returns a partial state update that becomes available to later nodes.


a. Definition

State is the shared data structure representing the current snapshot of a graph execution. It can contain user input, messages, retrieved documents, tool results, counters, routing decisions, errors, approval status, and the final output.

A state schema defines the names and expected types of the values stored in state. LangGraph supports schemas defined with TypedDict, dataclasses, and Pydantic models.

Nodes communicate by reading state and returning partial updates. A node does not need to return the entire state and should return an update dictionary instead of directly mutating the state object.

State also defines how updates are merged. Individual fields may use default replacement behavior or reducer functions that combine existing and incoming values.

b. Purpose

State allows nodes to share information across the workflow. A classifier can write a category, a retriever can read that category and add documents, and a generator can use those documents to create an answer.

State provides the data required for conditional routing. Routing functions can inspect fields such as status, retry_count, evidence_found, or approval_required to determine which node should run next.

State makes node responsibilities explicit. Developers can identify which fields each node reads and writes, making the workflow easier to test, trace, and maintain.

A clearly designed state schema reduces workflow coupling. Instead of passing loosely structured dictionaries or many positional arguments between functions, nodes use one documented graph-wide contract.

c. Architecture Diagram

                      DEFINING STATE
                      ==============

                        User Input
                             |
                             v
                    Initial Graph State
         Question | Category | Evidence | Answer
                             |
                             v
                     Classification Node
                             |
             Returns: Category and Status
                             |
                             v
                       Updated State
                             |
                             v
                       Retrieval Node
                             |
              Returns: Retrieved Evidence
                             |
                             v
                       Updated State
                             |
                             v
                       Generation Node
                             |
                  Returns: Final Answer
                             |
                             v
                         Final State


d. Core Components

1. State Schema

The state schema defines the fields shared by graph nodes and their expected types. It acts as the workflow’s communication contract. The schema should contain only values needed across node boundaries. Temporary variables used by one node should remain inside that node.

2. Input State

Input state provides the initial values required to begin execution. It commonly contains the user request, authenticated context, identifiers, and initial control values. External values should be validated before or at the graph boundary. TypedDict provides static type information but does not automatically perform runtime validation.

3. Intermediate State

Intermediate state stores results produced by earlier nodes for later operations. Examples include request classification, retrieved documents, tool observations, and verification verdicts. Intermediate fields should use meaningful names and focused data types. One generic data dictionary makes dependencies difficult to identify.

4. Control State

Control fields influence graph routing and termination. Status values, counters, Boolean flags, budgets, and approval decisions help determine the next node. Control values should use stable and predictable options. Literal types can document the permitted routing statuses and reduce spelling inconsistencies.

5. Output State

Output state stores the result intended for the caller. It may contain the final answer, citations, structured data, a confidence level, or a safe failure response. Internal debugging data should not automatically become part of public output. Separate input and output schemas can limit which fields cross the graph boundary.

e) Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class RequestState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    category: Literal["", "technical", "general"]
    status: Literal[
        "pending",
        "classified",
        "completed",
        "unsupported",
    ]
    answer: str
    error_message: str


def classify_request(state: RequestState) -> dict:
    question = state["question"].strip().lower()

    if not question:
        return {
            "category": "",
            "status": "unsupported",
            "error_message": "The question cannot be empty.",
        }

    technical_terms = (
        "langgraph",
        "stategraph",
        "workflow",
        "node",
        "edge",
    )

    category = (
        "technical"
        if any(term in question for term in technical_terms)
        else "general"
    )

    return {
        "category": category,
        "status": "classified",
        "error_message": "",
    }


def route_request(
    state: RequestState,
) -> Literal["technical", "general", "unsupported"]:
    if state["status"] == "unsupported":
        return "unsupported"

    return state["category"]


def create_technical_answer(state: RequestState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "LangGraph state is the shared data structure "
            "used by nodes during graph execution."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def create_general_answer(state: RequestState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The question was handled through "
            "the general response path."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def create_unsupported_answer(state: RequestState) -> dict:
    message = state["error_message"]

    if not message:
        message = "The request is not supported."

    return {
        "answer": message,
        "status": "unsupported",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(RequestState)

    builder.add_node(
        "classify",
        classify_request,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "technical",
        create_technical_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "general",
        create_general_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "unsupported",
        create_unsupported_answer,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify",
        route_request,
        {
            "technical": "technical",
            "general": "general",
            "unsupported": "unsupported",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "technical",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "general",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "unsupported",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state: RequestState = {
        "question": "How does LangGraph state work?",
        "category": "",
        "status": "pending",
        "answer": "",
        "error_message": "",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(initial_state)

    print("=" * 60)
    print("DEFINING STATE")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Category:", result["category"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Answer:", result["answer"])
    print("Error:", result["error_message"] or "None")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is state in LangGraph?

State is the shared data structure representing the current snapshot of a graph execution. Nodes read state, perform work, and return partial updates that become available to downstream nodes.

Q2. Which schema types can define state?

State can be defined using types such as TypedDict, a dataclass, or a Pydantic model. TypedDict is lightweight, while Pydantic is useful when runtime validation is required.

Q3. What should be stored in graph state?

Store values that must be shared across nodes, used for routing, persisted for recovery, or returned as output. Temporary variables needed by only one node should normally remain local to that node.

Q4. Should nodes mutate state directly?

No. Nodes should return dictionaries containing their state updates. The LangGraph runtime then applies those updates according to the state schema and configured reducers.

Q5. What is the difference between state and runtime context?

State contains mutable workflow information that nodes update during execution. Runtime context contains run-scoped dependencies or configuration, such as a user ID or database connection, that nodes may access without treating them as mutable graph state.


Topic 3: TypedDict State

a. Definition

TypedDict defines the expected structure of a Python dictionary for static type checking. Each annotated field becomes a state key that nodes can read or update during graph execution.

A TypedDict state uses normal dictionary access. Nodes read fields with expressions such as state["question"] and return dictionaries such as {"status": "completed"}.

TypedDict provides type hints without converting state into a custom runtime object. It is lightweight and well suited to straightforward graph-state schemas.

TypedDict does not automatically validate input at runtime. Invalid values can still enter the graph unless the application adds explicit validation or uses a runtime-validation model such as Pydantic.

b. Purpose

TypedDict documents the common state contract used by all graph nodes. Developers can clearly see which keys exist, what values they contain, and which information moves through the workflow.

It improves development-time safety. IDEs and static type checkers can flag misspelled keys, missing required fields, incorrect return annotations, and incompatible value types.

It keeps node implementation simple. Developers continue using standard dictionaries without needing constructors, conversion logic, or specialized attribute access.

It supports LangGraph reducers through Annotated. Individual fields can define custom merge behavior for accumulated messages, evidence, audit records, or parallel branch results.

c. Architecture Diagram

                     TYPEDDICT STATE
                     ===============

                       State Schema
        Question | Category | History | Answer | Status
                            |
                            v
                       Initial State
                            |
                            v
                     Classification Node
                 Reads: question and history
                 Writes: category and status
                            |
                            v
                       Updated State
                            |
                            v
                       Response Node
                 Reads: question and category
                 Writes: answer and history
                            |
                            v
                        Final State

d. Core Components

1. Field Names

Field names identify the values exchanged by graph nodes. Names such as documents, retry_count, and approval_status clearly communicate their purpose. Field names form part of the graph-wide contract. Changing one requires reviewing every node and router that reads or updates that field.

2. Field Types

Field types describe the expected values associated with each key. Examples include str, int, bool, list[str], and custom structured types. Specific types improve maintainability. Using list[str] is clearer than using an unrestricted list or Any.

3. Literal Control Fields

Literal fields enumerate expected routing or status values. They make the workflow’s valid control states visible in the schema. They reduce route inconsistencies during development. A type checker can distinguish completed from an accidental spelling such as completeed.

4. Reducer Annotation

A reducer annotation defines how an incoming update is combined with an existing field value. This enables list accumulation and other field-specific merge behavior. Reducers must be selected deliberately. Accumulating a field that should be replaced can preserve obsolete values or cause state to grow indefinitely.

5. Initial State

The initial state provides starting values before the first node executes. Required keys should receive values compatible with their declared types. Clear initialization reduces hidden assumptions inside nodes. Counters can begin at zero, lists as empty lists, and statuses with an explicit initial value.

e. Code Example

from operator import add
from typing import Annotated, Literal, TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class SupportState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    category: Literal["", "technical", "general"]
    events: Annotated[list[str], add]
    answer: str
    status: Literal["pending", "classified", "completed"]


def classify_question(state: SupportState) -> dict:
    question = state["question"].lower()
    technical_terms = (
        "langgraph",
        "stategraph",
        "python",
        "workflow",
    )

    category = (
        "technical"
        if any(term in question for term in technical_terms)
        else "general"
    )

    return {
        "category": category,
        "events": [f"Question classified as {category}."],
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_question(
    state: SupportState,
) -> Literal["technical", "general"\]:
    if state["category"] == "technical":
        return "technical"

    return "general"


def technical_response(state: SupportState) -> dict:
    return {
        "events": ["Technical answer generated."],
        "answer": (
            "TypedDict provides a lightweight schema "
            "for LangGraph shared state."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def general_response(state: SupportState) -> dict:
    return {
        "events": ["General answer generated."],
        "answer": (
            "The request followed the general "
            "response path."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(SupportState)

    builder.add_node(
        "classify",
        classifyquestion,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "technical",
        technical_response,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "general",
        general_response,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify",
        route_question,
        {
            "technical": "technical",
            "general": "general",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "technical",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "general",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    result = graph.invoke(
        {
            "question": (
                "How is TypedDict used in LangGraph?"
            ),
            "category": "",
            "events": [],
            "answer": "",
            "status": "pending",
        }
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("TYPEDDICT STATE")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Category:", result["category"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Events:")

    for event in result["events"]:
        print("-", event)

    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

f. Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is a TypedDict state in LangGraph?

A TypedDict state is a dictionary-shaped schema that declares the keys and value types shared by graph nodes. Nodes read values using dictionary access and return dictionaries containing partial state updates.

Q2. Does TypedDict validate state at runtime?

No. TypedDict primarily provides static type information for development tools and type checkers. Use explicit validation or Pydantic when runtime validation is required.

Q3. Why use Literal in a TypedDict state?

Literal documents the permitted values for routing fields and statuses. It improves static checking and helps ensure conditional-edge routes use predictable labels.

Q4. How are reducers added to TypedDict fields?

The field type is wrapped in Annotated together with a reducer function. For example, Annotated[list[str], operator.add] appends incoming list updates to the existing list.

Q5. When should TypedDict be preferred over Pydantic?

Use TypedDict when a lightweight dictionary state and static type hints are sufficient. Use Pydantic when strict runtime validation, constraints, parsing, or structured validation errors are required.

Topic 4: State Updates

State Updates describe how nodes modify the shared state during LangGraph execution. Each node receives the current state, performs one focused operation, and returns a dictionary containing only the fields it wants to change.

In a StateGraph, the conceptual node signature is State -> Partial<State>. LangGraph applies the returned update according to the reducers configured for the affected state fields.

a. Definition

A state update is a partial dictionary returned by a LangGraph node. It identifies the state fields that should be added or changed after the node completes.

A node may read the complete state but does not need to return every field. Fields omitted from the node’s update remain unchanged in the graph state.

Nodes should return updates rather than directly mutating the supplied state object. This preserves predictable data flow and makes each node’s contribution easier to inspect, test, and trace.

The result of an update depends on the field’s reducer. Without a custom reducer, a new value normally replaces the previous value. With a reducer, the old and new values are combined.

b. Purpose

State updates allow nodes to communicate without manually passing separate arguments between every function. A classifier can update category, a retriever can update documents, and a generator can update answer.

Partial updates keep node responsibilities focused. A node returns only the fields it owns instead of reconstructing the complete workflow state after every operation.

State updates drive conditional routing. Once a node updates fields such as status, retry_count, or evidence_sufficient, a routing function can inspect those values and select the next node.

Explicit updates improve observability and debugging. Developers can compare the state before and after each node to understand what changed and why the graph followed a particular route.

c. Architecture Diagram

                       STATE UPDATES
                       =============

                        Current State
         Question | Attempts | Evidence | Answer | Status
                              |
                              v
                        Active Node
                  Reads required fields
                              |
                              v
                      Performs Operation
                              |
                              v
                    Returns Partial Update
            Attempts | Evidence | Status
                              |
                              v
                    Reducer Processes Update
                              |
                              v
                        Updated State
                              |
                              v
                    Routing Function Reads State
                    /             |             \
                   v              v              v
                Retry          Generate         Stop

d. Core Components

1. Current State

Current state is the complete workflow snapshot supplied to an active node. It contains values produced by the initial input and previously executed nodes. Nodes should read only the fields needed for their responsibility. Excessive dependencies make nodes difficult to test and reuse.

2. Partial Update

A partial update is the dictionary returned by a node. It contains only the state fields that the node creates or modifies. Partial updates make node ownership visible. A retrieval node can clearly own documents and retrieval_status without rewriting unrelated fields.

3. Updated Field

An updated field is a state key included in a node’s returned dictionary. Its new value is processed according to the field’s reducer. Updated values should match the field’s declared type. Returning a string for a field declared as list[str] creates an inconsistent state contract.

4. Reducer

A reducer determines how an existing field value and an incoming update are combined. The reducer’s general contract is (existing, incoming) -> merged. Reducers are particularly important for accumulated or parallel updates. Lists of evidence, messages, and events commonly require intentional merging behavior.

5. Routing Field

A routing field stores information used to choose the next graph node. Common examples are status, category, approved, and retry_count. Routing values should use stable options. Predictable values make conditional-edge mappings easier to validate and maintain.

e. Code Example

from operator import add
from typing import Annotated, Literal, TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class UpdateState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    attempts: int
    evidence: str
    events: Annotated[list[str], add]
    answer: str
    status: Literal[
        "pending",
        "retry_required",
        "ready",
        "completed",
        "stopped",
    ]


def retrieve_evidence(state: UpdateState) -> dict:
    next_attempt = state["attempts"] + 1

    if next_attempt == 1:
        return {
            "attempts": next_attempt,
            "evidence": "",
            "events": [
                "First retrieval returned no evidence."
            ],
            "status": "retry_required",
        }

    return {
        "attempts": next_attempt,
        "evidence": (
            "LangGraph nodes return partial "
            "state updates."
        ),
        "events": [
            "Second retrieval returned evidence."
        ],
        "status": "ready",
    }


def route_after_retrieval(state: UpdateState) -> str:
    if state["status"] == "ready":
        return "generate"

    if state["attempts"] >= 2:
        return "stop"

    return "retry"


def generate_answer(state: UpdateState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": state["evidence"],
        "events": [
            "Answer generated from evidence."
        ],
        "status": "completed",
    }


def stop_workflow(state: UpdateState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The workflow stopped because supporting "
            "evidence was not found."
        ),
        "events": [
            "Workflow stopped."
        ],
        "status": "stopped",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(UpdateState)

    builder.add_node(
        "retrieve",
        retrieve_evidence,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "generate",
        generate_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "stop",
        stop_workflow,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "retrieve",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "retrieve",
        route_after_retrieval,
        {
            "retry": "retrieve",
            "generate": "generate",
            "stop": "stop",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "generate",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "stop",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state: UpdateState = {
        "question": (
            "How do nodes update LangGraph state?"
        ),
        "attempts": 0,
        "evidence": "",
        "events": [
            "Workflow started."
        ],
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH STATE UPDATES")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Attempts:", result["attempts"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Evidence:", result["evidence"])
    print("Events:")

    for event in result["events"]:
        print("-", event)

    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is a state update in LangGraph?

A state update is a partial dictionary returned by a graph node. It contains the fields that the node wants to add or modify in the shared state.

Q2. Must a node return the complete state?

No. A node normally returns only the fields it changes. LangGraph preserves existing values for fields omitted from the update.

Q3. Should nodes mutate state directly?

No. Nodes should return update dictionaries rather than directly changing the supplied state object. Official Graph API guidance explicitly recommends returning updates.

Q4. How are state updates merged?

Each field uses its configured reducer. Without a custom reducer, the incoming value normally replaces the existing value. With a reducer, the values are combined.

Q5. How do state updates affect conditional routing?

A node updates control fields such as status, category, or retry_count. After the update is merged, the conditional routing function reads those values and selects the next destination.

Topic 5: State Merging

State Merging defines how LangGraph combines node updates with the existing shared state. Each state key may use default replacement behavior or a custom reducer whose general contract is (existing_value, incoming_value) -> merged_value.

State merging becomes especially important when a workflow accumulates messages or evidence, executes loops, or runs multiple branches that update the same state field.

a. Definition

State Merging is the process of combining a node’s partial update with the graph’s existing state. Nodes return only the fields they want to change, and LangGraph determines the resulting value of each updated field.

Without a custom reducer, an incoming value normally replaces the existing value. This behavior is appropriate for fields representing the current status, answer, route, selected model, retry count, or error.

A custom reducer combines the existing and incoming values instead of replacing the earlier value. Reducers are commonly used to accumulate messages, retrieved evidence, workflow events, citations, and parallel branch results.

Reducers are configured separately for individual state fields. This allows one state field to use replacement while another field accumulates values inside the same workflow.

b. Purpose

State merging preserves information produced during multiple workflow steps. A retrieval loop can add new evidence during every iteration without discarding evidence collected previously.

It supports parallel graph execution. Independent nodes may return updates for the same list field, and an appropriate reducer can combine those branch results into one shared value.

Merging gives each state field behavior appropriate to its meaning. A historical field such as audit_events should accumulate, while a current-value field such as status should normally be replaced.

It creates predictable communication between nodes. Downstream nodes receive a consolidated state snapshot rather than needing to manually combine outputs from every earlier operation.

c. Architecture Diagram

                       STATE MERGING
                       =============

                        Existing State
          Evidence | Events | Answer | Status
                              |
                +-------------+-------------+
                |                           |
                v                           v
        Retrieval Node A            Retrieval Node B
                |                           |
                v                           v
        Evidence Update A            Evidence Update B
                |                           |
                +-------------+-------------+
                              |
                              v
                         Reducer Layer
                 Existing Value + New Values
                              |
                              v
                         Merged State
          Combined Evidence | Events | Answer | Status
                              |
                              v
                       Generation Node
                              |
                              v
                         Final Answer

Architecture Explanation

The graph begins with an existing state snapshot.
One or more nodes return partial state updates.
LangGraph processes every updated field through its configured reducer.
Fields without custom reducers use replacement behavior.
Fields with custom reducers combine the earlier and incoming values.
The resulting state becomes available to downstream nodes.
When several parallel nodes update the same field, that field requires compatible merge behavior.

d. Core Components

1. Existing Value

The existing value is the current content of a state field before a new node update is applied. It may come from the initial graph input or from previous nodes. Reducers receive this value as their first input. The reducer decides whether to preserve, transform, append to, or otherwise combine it.

2. Incoming Value

The incoming value is the field value returned by the currently executing node. It represents the node’s proposed state update. Its type must be compatible with the state field and reducer. A list reducer cannot safely combine a list with an unrelated scalar value.

3. Reducer

The reducer is the function responsible for combining the existing and incoming values. Its return value becomes the new state-field value.

Reducers should be deterministic and free of unrelated side effects. Their responsibility is state combination, not model invocation, retrieval, logging, or external writes.

4. Merged State

Merged state is the consolidated workflow snapshot available after updates have been applied. Downstream nodes and routing functions read this resulting snapshot. It may contain both replaced and accumulated fields. Each field follows its own configured state-update behavior.

5. Parallel Update

A parallel update occurs when multiple active branches return values for the same field during one execution stage. LangGraph needs a compatible reducer to combine those values.

Parallel merging must avoid assumptions about branch completion order. When order matters, results should contain sequence metadata and be explicitly sorted before use. 

e. Code Example

from operator import add
from typing import Annotated, TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class MergeState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    evidence: Annotated[list[str], add]
    audit_events: Annotated[list[str], add]
    answer: str
    status: str


def retrieve_project_evidence(
    state: MergeState,
) -> dict:
    return {
        "evidence": [
            "Project Atlas deploys on October 5, 2026."
        ],
        "audit_events": [
            "Project evidence retrieved."
        ],
    }


def retrieve_platform_evidence(
    state: MergeState,
) -> dict:
    return {
        "evidence": [
            "Project Atlas is hosted on Microsoft Azure."
        ],
        "audit_events": [
            "Platform evidence retrieved."
        ],
    }


def generate_answer(state: MergeState) -> dict:
    context = " ".join(
        state["evidence"]
    )

    return {
        "answer": context,
        "audit_events": [
            "Answer generated from merged evidence."
        ],
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(MergeState)

    builder.add_node(
        "retrieve_project",
        retrieve_project_evidence,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "retrieve_platform",
        retrieve_platform_evidence,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "generate",
        generate_answer,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "retrieve_project",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "retrieve_platform",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        [
            "retrieve_project",
            "retrieve_platform",
        ],
        "generate",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "generate",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    result = graph.invoke(
        {
            "question": (
                "When does Project Atlas deploy, "
                "and where is it hosted?"
            ),
            "evidence": [],
            "audit_events": [
                "Workflow started."
            ],
            "answer": "",
            "status": "pending",
        }
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH STATE MERGING")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Evidence:")

    for item in result["evidence"]:
        print("-", item)

    print("Audit events:")

    for event in result["audit_events"]:
        print("-", event)

    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is State Merging in LangGraph?

State Merging is the process of combining a node’s partial update with the existing shared state. Each field follows default replacement behavior or a custom reducer configured in the state schema.

Q2. What happens when a field has no reducer?

The incoming node value normally replaces the existing value for that field. This is suitable for current-value fields such as status, answer, retry count, or selected route.

Q3. Why are reducers important for parallel nodes?

Parallel nodes may update the same state field during the same execution stage. A reducer tells LangGraph how those branch updates should be combined instead of allowing one value to overwrite another.

Q4. When should a custom reducer be used?

Use a custom reducer when default replacement or simple list concatenation does not meet the requirement. Examples include duplicate removal, bounded history, score-based selection, timestamp conflict resolution, and merging structured records.

Q5. What is a reducer in StateGraph?

A reducer is a function that accepts the existing field value and an incoming update and returns the merged result. Its general contract is: (existing value, incoming value) -> merged value. Each state key can optionally define its own reducer.

Topic 6: Shared State

Shared State is the common data structure through which nodes communicate in a LangGraph workflow. Instead of passing separate arguments manually between every function, each node receives the current state, reads the fields it needs, and returns partial updates for downstream nodes.

In a StateGraph, nodes conceptually follow the contract State -> Partial<State>. State represents the current application snapshot, nodes perform work, and edges determine which node receives the updated state next.

a. Definition

Shared State is the graph-wide data structure available to nodes during workflow execution. It can contain the user request, classification, retrieved documents, tool results, counters, validation outcomes, approval decisions, errors, and final answer.

Every node receives a snapshot of the current shared state. A node reads only the fields required for its operation and returns an update for the fields it owns.

Shared State evolves as nodes execute. LangGraph applies node updates according to the reducer configured for each field, producing a new state snapshot for downstream processing.

Shared State is scoped to a graph execution or thread. When persistence is configured, state snapshots can be associated with a thread so the workflow can continue across invocations or interruptions.

b. Purpose

Shared State provides a common communication channel between graph nodes. A classifier can write category, a retriever can read the category and write documents, and a generator can read the documents and write the final answer.

It eliminates complicated manual argument passing. Nodes do not need to invoke downstream functions directly or know the complete workflow because the graph runtime delivers the current state.

Shared State enables dynamic routing. Conditional edges inspect fields such as status, retry_count, approval_required, or evidence_sufficient to select the next node.

It improves traceability and recoverability. Developers can inspect the complete workflow snapshot at a node boundary, and a checkpointer can persist that snapshot when continuity is required.

c. Architecture Diagram

                       SHARED STATE
                       ============

                        User Request
                             |
                             v
                        Initial State
          Question | Category | Documents | Answer
                             |
                             v
                    Classification Node
                     Reads: question
                     Writes: category
                             |
                             v
                        Shared State
          Question | Category | Documents | Answer
                             |
                             v
                       Retrieval Node
                Reads: question and category
                     Writes: documents
                             |
                             v
                        Shared State
          Question | Category | Documents | Answer
                             |
                             v
                       Generation Node
                Reads: question and documents
                       Writes: answer
                             |
                             v
                         Final State

d. Core Components

1. Shared State Schema

The shared state schema defines the graph-wide fields available to nodes. It specifies the keys, types, and reducer behavior used during execution. The schema should remain explicit and focused. A large generic dictionary hides node dependencies and weakens type checking.

2. State Reader

A state reader is a node that consumes one or more state fields. For example, a generator reads the question and retrieved documents. Nodes should read only the fields needed for their responsibility. This reduces coupling and makes unit testing easier.

3. State Writer

A state writer is a node that returns updates for specific fields. A retriever may write documents, while a verifier writes verification_status. A node should not rewrite unrelated fields. Returning focused partial updates makes graph behavior easier to inspect.

4. Control Field

A control field determines routing or termination. Typical examples include status, retry_count, approved, and evidence_sufficient. Control fields should use stable values. Predictable values simplify conditional-edge mappings and reduce accidental routing errors.

5. Shared-State Reducer

A reducer defines how updates to a shared field are combined. It is especially important when loops or parallel branches contribute to one list. The reducer should reflect the field’s meaning. Current status usually uses replacement, while evidence history may use accumulation.

e. Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class SharedState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    category: Literal["", "technical", "general"]
    documents: list[str]
    answer: str
    status: Literal[
        "pending",
        "classified",
        "evidence_ready",
        "no_evidence",
        "completed",
    ]


def classify_question(state: SharedState) -> dict:
    question = state["question"].lower()

    technical_terms = (
        "langgraph",
        "state",
        "node",
        "workflow",
    )

    category = (
        "technical"
        if any(term in question for term in technical_terms)
        else "general"
    )

    return {
        "category": category,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_category(
    state: SharedState,
) -> Literal["retrieve", "general"\\]:
    if state["category"] == "technical":
        return "retrieve"

    return "general"


def retrieve_documents(state: SharedState) -> dict:
    knowledge_base = [
        (
            "Shared state allows LangGraph nodes "
            "to communicate through a common schema."
        ),
        (
            "Each node reads current state and "
            "returns partial state updates."
        ),
    ]

    query_terms = set(
        state["question"].lower().split()
    )

    matches = []

    for document in knowledge_base:
        document_terms = set(
            document.lower().split()
        )

        if query_terms.intersection(document_terms):
            matches.append(document)

    if not matches:
        return {
            "documents": [],
            "status": "no_evidence",
        }

    return {
        "documents": matches,
        "status": "evidenceready",
    }


def route_after_retrieval(
    state: SharedState,
) -> Literal["generate", "no_evidence"\]:
    if state["status"] == "evidence_ready":
        return "generate"

    return "no_evidence"


def generate_answer(state: SharedState) -> dict:
    answer = " ".join(state["documents"])

    return {
        "answer": answer,
        "status": "completed",
    }


def general_answer(state: SharedState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request followed the general path."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def no_evidence_answer(state: SharedState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "No supporting evidence was found."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(SharedState)

    builder.add_node(
        "classify",
        classify_question,
    )
    builder.add_node(
        "retrieve",
        retrieve_documents,
    )
    builder.add_node(
        "generate",
        generate_answer,
    )
    builder.add_node(
        "general",
        general_answer,
    )
    builder.add_node(
        "no_evidence",
        no_evidence_answer,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify",
        route_category,
        {
            "retrieve": "retrieve",
            "general": "general",
        },
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "retrieve",
        route_after_retrieval,
        {
            "generate": "generate",
            "no_evidence": "no_evidence",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "generate",
        END,
    )
    builder.add_edge(
        "general",
        END,
    )
    builder.add_edge(
        "no_evidence",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state: SharedState = {
        "question": (
            "How does shared state work in LangGraph?"
        ),
        "category": "",
        "documents": [],
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(initial_state)

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH SHARED STATE")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Category:", result["category"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Documents:")

    for document in result["documents"]:
        print("-", document)

    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Shared State in LangGraph?

Shared State is the common data structure through which graph nodes communicate. Each node receives the current state, reads the fields it needs, and returns partial updates for downstream nodes.

Q2. How does one node access another node’s result?

The earlier node writes its result into shared state. After LangGraph applies that update, downstream nodes can read the corresponding state field.

Q3. Is Shared State the same as a global variable?

No. Graph state is scoped to a graph execution or persisted thread and follows an explicit schema and update model. A global variable exists at process level and can accidentally share information across unrelated requests.

Q4. What is the difference between Shared State and runtime context?

Shared State contains mutable workflow information that nodes can update. Runtime context contains run-scoped dependencies or configuration, such as a user identifier or database client, that should not be treated as mutable graph data.

Q5. How should large data be handled in Shared State?

Store stable identifiers, references, or concise summaries in state. Keep large documents, files, embeddings, and tool payloads in authorized external storage and load them only when required.


Topic 7: Graph Compilation

Graph Compilation converts a StateGraph builder into an executable CompiledStateGraph. Before compilation, the graph is only a structural definition containing its state schema, nodes, edges, reducers, and routing logic. After compilation, it supports execution methods such as invoke(), stream(), batch(), ainvoke(), and astream().


a. Definition

Graph Compilation transforms a StateGraph definition into an executable graph runtime. The builder describes the workflow, but it cannot process input directly until compile() has been called.

Compilation performs structural checks on the graph. For example, it can identify certain invalid graph structures, such as nodes that are not properly connected to the workflow.

Compilation is the stage where runtime services can be attached. These include a checkpointer, store, cache, debugging configuration, graph name, and interrupt points placed before or after selected nodes.

The result is a CompiledStateGraph that implements the Runnable interface. It can be invoked, streamed, batched, or executed asynchronously.

b. Purpose

The primary purpose of compilation is to separate graph construction from graph execution. Developers first design the state, nodes, and transitions, then compile that design into an application that can process runtime input.

Compilation validates important aspects of the graph before execution begins. This helps detect selected structural problems during application startup rather than after a production request reaches the workflow.

It configures graph-wide runtime behavior in one location. Persistence, caching, interruption points, debugging, and long-term storage can be attached without embedding that infrastructure code inside every node.

Compilation produces a reusable executable graph. The graph should normally be built and compiled once, then reused for many invocations instead of being reconstructed for every request.

c. Architeture Diagram

                    GRAPH COMPILATION
                    =================

                       State Schema
                            |
                            v
                    StateGraph Builder
                            |
          +-----------------+-----------------+
          |                 |                 |
          v                 v                 v
       Add Nodes         Add Edges      Add Conditions
          |                 |                 |
          +-----------------+-----------------+
                            |
                            v
                         compile()
                            |
            +---------------+---------------+
            |               |               |
            v               v               v
      Structural Checks   Runtime Setup   Optional Services
                                          Checkpointer
                                          Store
                                          Cache
                                          Interrupts
                                          Debug Mode
                            |
                            v
                   CompiledStateGraph
                            |
          +-----------------+-----------------+
          |                 |                 |
          v                 v                 v
       invoke()          stream()          batch()
          |                 |                 |
          +-----------------+-----------------+
                            |
                            v
                      Final Graph State


d) Core Components

1. Graph Builder

The graph builder stores the workflow definition before execution. It contains the state schema, node registrations, edges, conditional routes, and termination paths. The builder is intended for construction rather than runtime processing. It must be compiled before using execution methods.

2. Structural Validation

Compilation checks important properties of the graph structure. It helps identify selected connectivity and configuration problems before runtime. Compilation does not validate every possible business or runtime error. Incorrect tool arguments, external service failures, malformed model output, and logical routing mistakes still require application testing.

3. CompiledStateGraph

CompiledStateGraph is the executable result created by compile(). It implements the Runnable interface and supports invocation, streaming, batching, and asynchronous execution. The compiled graph can be reused across several requests. Each invocation receives its own input, execution configuration, and thread identity where persistence is enabled.

4. Checkpointer

A checkpointer stores fully versioned graph-state snapshots. It enables pause, resume, replay, and thread-scoped short-term memory. Checkpointed execution requires a thread identifier. The identifier is used to store and retrieve checkpoints belonging to the correct workflow execution.

5. Interrupt Configuration

Interrupt configuration pauses the graph before or after selected nodes. It is useful for debugging and controlled intervention points. An interrupt does not represent normal graph completion. The workflow remains paused until the application follows the appropriate continuation or resumption procedure.

e) Code Example

from typing import TypedDict

from langgraph.checkpoint.memory import InMemorySaver
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class CompilationState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    processed_question: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def normalize_question(
    state: CompilationState,
) -> dict:
    normalized = " ".join(
        state["question"].strip().split()
    )

    return {
        "processed_question": normalized,
        "status": "normalized",
    }


def generate_answer(
    state: CompilationState,
) -> dict:
    answer = (
        "Graph compilation converts a StateGraph "
        "builder into an executable graph."
    )

    return {
        "answer": answer,
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(CompilationState)

    builder.add_node(
        "normalize",
        normalize_question,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "generate",
        generate_answer,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "normalize",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "normalize",
        "generate",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "generate",
        END,
    )

    checkpointer = InMemorySaver()

    compiled_graph = builder.compile(
        checkpointer=checkpointer,
        name="graph_compilation_example",
    )

    return compiled_graph


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    config = {
        "configurable": {
            "thread_id": "compilation-thread-101",
        }
    }

    initial_state: CompilationState = {
        "question": (
            "  What is graph compilation?  "
        ),
        "processed_question": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state,
        config=config,
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH GRAPH COMPILATION")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print(
        "Processed question:",
        result["processed_question"],
    )
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f) Interview Questions And Examples

Q1. What is Graph Compilation in LangGraph?

Graph Compilation converts a StateGraph builder into a CompiledStateGraph. The compiled graph is executable and supports invocation, streaming, batching, and asynchronous execution.

Q2. Can a StateGraph builder be executed directly?

No. StateGraph is a builder class and must be compiled before use. The resulting compiled graph provides the runtime execution methods.

Q3. What can be configured during compilation?

Compilation can configure a checkpointer, cache, store, interrupts before or after nodes, debug mode, a graph name, and supported stream transformers.

Q4. Why is thread_id required with a checkpointer?

The thread_id identifies which workflow thread owns the saved checkpoints. It allows the checkpointer to store and retrieve the correct thread-specific graph state.

Q5. What is the difference between StateGraph and CompiledStateGraph?

StateGraph defines the workflow’s state, nodes, and edges. CompiledStateGraph is the executable runtime created from that definition and supports methods such as invoke() and stream().


Chapter 3: Nodes

Topic 1: Node Fundamentals

Nodes are the executable units of a LangGraph workflow. They receive the current graph state, perform a focused computation or side effect, and return a partial state update. Edges then determine which node executes next.

A node can contain ordinary Python logic, an LLM invocation, document retrieval, tool execution, validation, database access, or another compiled graph. This lets a workflow combine deterministic operations with flexible model-driven steps.


a) Definition

A node is a function or compatible Runnable that performs one unit of work inside a graph. The node receives the current state, executes its assigned operation, and returns fields that should be added to or updated in the shared state.

Nodes contain processing logic, while edges contain transition logic. A node may classify a request or retrieve documents, but an ordinary or conditional edge determines which node becomes active afterward.

A node should generally return a partial state update instead of rebuilding or directly mutating the complete state. This keeps its contribution explicit and allows the graph runtime to apply the configured state reducers.

Nodes can be deterministic or model-driven. For example, authorization and calculation nodes can use predictable Python logic, while classification and generation nodes can invoke an LLM. LangGraph is designed to combine both approaches in the same graph.

b) Purpose

Nodes divide a complex workflow into focused and manageable operations. Retrieval, reranking, generation, verification, and approval can be implemented separately instead of being hidden inside one large function.

Nodes provide clear boundaries for testing and debugging. Each node can be tested with a controlled state input and inspected for the exact partial update it returns.

Nodes enable reusable workflow components. A validation, retrieval, or formatting node can be connected to different execution paths as long as its expected input and output state remain compatible.

Named nodes improve visibility during execution. Traces and streaming updates can show which node started, completed, failed, or produced a particular state update.

c) Architecture Diagram

                     LANGGRAPH NODE
                     ==============

                       Current State
                            |
                            v
                        Active Node
                +-----------+-----------+
                |                       |
                v                       v
          Read State Fields       Access Runtime
                |                       |
                +-----------+-----------+
                            |
                            v
                    Perform One Operation
           Python | LLM | Retriever | Tool | API
                            |
                            v
                   Return Partial Update
                            |
                            v
                     Reducer Processing
                            |
                            v
                        Updated State
                            |
                            v
                      Outgoing Edge
                   /        |        \
                  v         v         v
              Next Node   Retry     END

Architecture Diagram

1. An incoming edge activates the node. At the beginning of execution, a node connected to START becomes active.

2. The node receives the current state. It reads only the fields necessary for its responsibility.

3. The node carries out one operation. This may be deterministic logic, retrieval, model generation, validation, or an external service call.

4. The node returns a partial update. Fields omitted from the update remain unchanged.

5. LangGraph merges the update into shared state. Each field follows either replacement behavior or its configured reducer.

6. The graph evaluates outgoing edges. The next step may be a fixed node, a conditionally selected node, a retry path, or END.

d) Core Components

1. Node Function

The node function contains one unit of workflow logic. It receives state and may also receive supported runtime information or configuration. The function should remain focused and independently testable. A node that performs classification, retrieval, generation, and validation together is difficult to reuse and troubleshoot.

2. Node Name

The node name uniquely identifies the operation in the graph. It appears in graph structure, streaming updates, traces, and error investigation. Names should describe the operation clearly. Prefer retrieve_documents or verify_answer instead of step_1 or process.

3. Input State

Input state is the graph snapshot received when the node becomes active. It contains initial input and updates returned by earlier nodes. The node should depend on as few fields as practical. Minimal dependencies reduce coupling and simplify unit testing.

4. Partial Update

A partial update is the dictionary returned by the node. It identifies the fields the node wants to add, replace, or merge. Its values must conform to the state schema and reducer behavior. Returning an incompatible type can cause downstream runtime problems.

5. Side Effect

A side effect changes an external system rather than only calculating a state value. Examples include sending email, updating a database, creating a ticket, or approving a transaction. Side-effecting nodes should be idempotent or protected by an idempotency key. A retry must not accidentally execute the same external action more than once.

e) Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class NodeState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    category: Literal["", "technical", "general"]
    answer: str
    status: Literal[
        "pending",
        "classified",
        "completed",
    ]


def classify_node(state: NodeState) -> dict:
    question = state["question"].lower()

    technical_terms = (
        "langgraph",
        "node",
        "stategraph",
        "workflow",
    )

    category = (
        "technical"
        if any(term in question for term in technical_terms)
        else "general"
    )

    return {
        "category": category,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_after_classification(
    state: NodeState,
) -> Literal["technical", "general"\]:
    if state["category"] == "technical":
        return "technical"

    return "general"


def technical_node(state: NodeState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "A LangGraph node receives state, performs "
            "one operation, and returns a partial update."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def general_node(state: NodeState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The question followed the general "
            "response path."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(NodeState)

    builder.add_node(
        "classify",
        classify_node,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "technical",
        technical_node,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "general",
        general_node,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify",
        route_after_classification,
        {
            "technical": "technical",
            "general": "general",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "technical",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "general",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state: NodeState = {
        "question": "What is a node in LangGraph?",
        "category": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH NODE FUNDAMENTALS")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Category:", result["category"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is a node in LangGraph?

A node is an executable function or Runnable that receives the current graph state, performs an operation, and returns a state update. Nodes do the application work, while edges determine which node executes next.

Q2. What can a LangGraph node contain?

A node can contain ordinary Python logic, an LLM call, retrieval, tool execution, validation, database access, an API call, or another compiled graph.

Q3. Should a node return the complete state?

Normally, no. A node should return only the fields it wants to add or update. The graph runtime preserves unchanged fields and applies reducers to updated fields.

Q4. What is the difference between a node and an edge?

A node performs computation or a side effect. An edge controls execution flow by determining which node becomes active next.

Q5. How should production nodes be designed?

Production nodes should have focused responsibilities, clear names, minimal state dependencies, explicit updates, bounded remote calls, structured error handling, and idempotent side effects.

Topic 2: LLM Nodes

An LLM Node is a LangGraph node that invokes a language model as one step in a larger workflow. It reads prompt data or messages from graph state, calls a chat model, and returns the model’s response as a partial state update.

LangGraph does not provide its own language model. It provides orchestration, while model integrations usually come from LangChain or another model SDK. LangGraph’s main strength is combining model-driven nodes with deterministic nodes inside one controlled graph.


a. Definition

An LLM Node is a graph node whose primary operation is invoking a language model. The node receives state, constructs the model input, calls the model, and returns the generated result as a state update.

The node may use a chat model that accepts messages and returns an AI message. LangChain chat-model integrations provide a common message-based interface across supported providers.

An LLM node can perform more than answer generation. It can classify requests, rewrite queries, extract structured information, summarize documents, create plans, evaluate responses, or decide whether a tool is required.

LLM nodes should be used only where language understanding or generation adds value. Authorization, budgets, mathematical calculations, retry limits, and strict business rules should normally remain deterministic.

b. Purpose

LLM nodes add semantic understanding to graph workflows. They can interpret user intent, understand different phrasings, summarize unstructured information, and generate natural-language responses.

They allow model behavior to be placed inside a controlled execution path. A model can classify or generate content, while LangGraph determines which nodes may execute before and after the model call.

LLM nodes support specialization. Different nodes can use different prompts, models, temperature settings, output schemas, and token limits according to their specific responsibilities.

They make model calls observable as separate workflow operations. Teams can trace latency, token usage, failures, output quality, and the routing decisions influenced by each invocation.


c. Architecture Diagram

                         LLM NODE
                         ========

                       Current State
            Question | Context | Messages | Status
                             |
                             v
                         LLM Node
                             |
                 +-----------+-----------+
                 |                       |
                 v                       v
           Read State Fields       Select Prompt
                 |                       |
                 +-----------+-----------+
                             |
                             v
                    Construct Messages
                             |
                             v
                    Invoke Chat Model
                             |
                 +-----------+-----------+
                 |                       |
              Success                  Failure
                 |                       |
                 v                       v
          Extract Response        Retry, Fallback,
                 |                or Error Route
                 v
              Return Update
            Answer | Status
                 |
                 v
                      Updated State
                             |
                             v
                  Verification or END

Architecture Explanation

The node receives the current state. This may contain a question, retrieved evidence, conversation messages, model configuration, or previous results.

The node prepares the model input. It can construct system and user messages or use a prompt template.

The selected chat model is invoked. Chat models consume a message sequence and return a model-generated message.

The response is extracted and validated. The node may use plain text or structured output, depending on the task.

The node returns a partial update. The generated response, classification, or structured result becomes part of graph state.

The graph controls subsequent execution. The answer may be verified, routed to a tool, regenerated, or returned to the caller.

d) Core Components

1. Chat Model

The chat model performs language understanding or generation inside the node. It consumes messages and returns an AI-generated message. The model should be selected according to task complexity, latency, context size, tool requirements, and budget. One model is not necessarily appropriate for every graph node.

2. Prompt Construction

Prompt construction converts selected state fields into instructions and messages for the model. It defines the task, available evidence, output requirements, and restrictions. Only necessary and authorized state fields should enter the prompt. Internal errors, credentials, unrestricted documents, and unrelated history should be excluded.

3. Model Response

The model response contains the generated text, structured result, or requested tool calls. The node must convert the result into a graph-state update. The response should be validated before controlling sensitive routing or actions. Free-form model text should not be treated directly as authorization or permission.

4. Output Parser or Schema

An output parser or structured schema converts model output into an application-friendly form. It is particularly useful for categories, entities, plans, and verification results. Parsing failures need an explicit recovery policy. The graph can retry with corrective instructions, use a fallback, or route to a safe-stop node.

5. Model-Failure Policy

The failure policy defines what happens when the model call or response validation fails. It may permit bounded retry, route to a fallback model, request human review, or terminate safely. The policy should enforce latency, retry, and cost limits. Repeated model calls must not continue indefinitely.

e) Code Example

from typing import Literal, TypedDict

from langchain_core.language_models.fake_chat_models import (
    FakeListChatModel,
)
from langchain_core.messages import (
    HumanMessage,
    SystemMessage,
)
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class LLMState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    context: str
    answer: str
    status: Literal[
        "pending",
        "validated",
        "completed",
        "invalid",
    ]


model = FakeListChatModel(
    responses=[
        (
            "An LLM node reads graph state, invokes a "
            "language model, and returns the model response "
            "as a partial state update."
        )
    ]
)


def validate_input(state: LLMState) -> dict:
    if not state["question"].strip():
        return {
            "status": "invalid",
            "answer": "The question cannot be empty.",
        }

    return {
        "status": "validated",
    }


def route_after_validation(
    state: LLMState,
) -> Literal["call_model", "invalid"\]:
    if state["status"] == "validated":
        return "call_model"

    return "invalid"


def call_model(state: LLMState) -> dict:
    messages = [
        SystemMessage(
            content=(
                "Answer using only the supplied context. "
                "Do not add unsupported information."
            )
        ),
        HumanMessage(
            content=(
                f"Question: {state['question']}\n"
                f"Context: {state['context']}"
            )
        ),
    ]

    response = model.invoke(messages)

    return {
        "answer": str(response.content),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def invalid_response(state: LLMState) -> dict:
    return {
        "status": "invalid",
    }


def build_graph():
