    builder = StateGraph(LLMState)

    builder.add_node(
        "validate_input",
        validate_input,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "call_model",
        call_model,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "invalid",
        invalid_response,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "validate_input",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "validate_input",
        route_after_validation,
        {
            "call_model": "call_model",
            "invalid": "invalid",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "call_model",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "invalid",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state: LLMState = {
        "question": "What is an LLM node?",
        "context": (
            "An LLM node invokes a language model as "
            "one controlled step in a LangGraph workflow."
        ),
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH LLM NODE")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is an LLM node in LangGraph?

An LLM node is a graph node that reads state, prepares model input, invokes a language model, and returns the result as a partial state update.

Q2. Does LangGraph provide its own LLM?

No. LangGraph focuses on orchestration. Developers normally use LangChain model integrations, another model SDK, or a custom model client inside graph nodes.

Q3. What should an LLM node return?

It should return a dictionary containing the graph-state fields it wants to update. For example, an answer-generation node may return answer and status.

Q4. Why use structured output in an LLM node?

Structured output constrains the model response to an application-defined schema. It is useful for categories, entities, routing decisions, verification verdicts, and tool arguments.

Q5. How should LLM-node failures be handled?

Use explicit timeouts, bounded retries, validated fallbacks, structured error state, and safe termination or escalation paths. Do not allow repeated model calls to continue without iteration, latency, token, and cost limits.

Topic 3: Tool Nodes

A Tool Node executes tools inside a LangGraph workflow. A tool can perform deterministic calculations, query a database, call an API, retrieve business data, or interact with another controlled system.

The prebuilt ToolNode executes tool calls, supports multiple tools, can run independent tool calls in parallel, provides configurable error handling, and supports advanced state or store injection. For standard ReAct-style agents, LangChain’s higher-level agent abstraction uses ToolNode internally.

a. Definition

A Tool Node is a prebuilt LangGraph node that executes tools requested by a model or supplied programmatically. It reads tool calls, locates the matching registered tools, validates their arguments, executes them, and returns the results as ToolMessage objects.

Tools connect the language-model workflow to external capabilities. They may perform calculations, retrieve current records, search documents, query databases, call APIs, or execute controlled business operations.

ToolNode manages the execution mechanics around tools. It supports plain functions and LangChain BaseTool instances, multiple tool calls, customizable tool-error handling, state injection, persistent-store injection, and command-based state updates.

A tool call is a proposal, not automatic authorization. Sensitive operations should pass through deterministic authentication, authorization, argument validation, business-rule validation, and approval checks before execution.

b. Purpose

Tool Nodes allow an agent to access information or perform operations that the language model cannot complete reliably from its internal knowledge. Examples include reading current order status, calculating an exact result, retrieving account data, or checking live inventory.

They separate model reasoning from deterministic execution. The model can decide that a tool is needed and construct proposed arguments, while the actual tool performs the operation using application code.

ToolNode reduces the amount of boilerplate required for custom tool-execution workflows. Developers do not need to manually parse every tool call, locate the corresponding function, execute it, and convert the result into a tool message.

Tool Nodes provide an observable execution boundary. Tool name, sanitized arguments, duration, success status, errors, and output can be traced independently from the model call that requested the tool.

c. Architecture Diagram

                       TOOL NODE
                       =========

                        User Request
                             |
                             v
                           LLM Node
                             |
                      Generates Tool Call
                Name | Arguments | Tool Call ID
                             |
                             v
                    Authorization Guardrail
                             |
                             v
                          ToolNode
                 +-----------+-----------+
                 |                       |
                 v                       v
          Validate Tool Name      Validate Arguments
                 |                       |
                 +-----------+-----------+
                             |
                             v
                       Execute Tool
                             |
                 +-----------+-----------+
                 |                       |
              Success                  Failure
                 |                       |
                 v                       v
           ToolMessage          Controlled Error Result
                 |                       |
                 +-----------+-----------+
                             |
                             v
                        Shared State
                             |
                             v
                           LLM Node
                             |
                             v
                       Final Response

d. Core Components

1. Tool Definition

The tool definition describes one callable capability and its argument schema. Its name and description help the model or application understand when and how it should be invoked. A tool should perform one focused operation. Prefer separate tools such as get_order_status and cancel_order rather than one broad manage_order function.

2. Tool Call

A tool call is a structured request containing a tool name, arguments, and call identifier. It may be generated by a model or created programmatically. Tool arguments must be validated before execution. Correct structure does not prove that the user is authorized to perform the requested operation.

3. ToolNode

ToolNode locates and executes the tools requested by incoming calls. It converts normal tool outputs into messages that the workflow can return to the model. It also supports advanced execution features. These include parallel calls, error handling, injected state, injected stores, and command-based updates.

4. ToolMessage

A ToolMessage carries a tool result back into the message history. Its tool-call ID connects the result with the corresponding AI-generated request. Tool results should be concise and structured where possible. Returning unrestricted database rows or oversized API payloads increases context size and may expose sensitive data.

5. Tool Guardrail

A tool guardrail validates authorization, arguments, resource ownership, business limits, and approval status before execution. Guardrails must be enforced by deterministic application code. A language model must not grant itself permission to execute a sensitive operation.


e. Code Example

from langchain_core.messages import AIMessage
from langchain_core.tools import tool
from langgraph.graph import END, START, MessagesState, StateGraph
from langgraph.prebuilt import ToolNode


@tool
def get_project_status(project_name: str) -> str:
    """Return the current status of a project."""

    projects = {
        "Project Atlas": "Ready for deployment",
        "Project Orion": "Development in progress",
    }

    return projects.get(
        project_name,
        "Project was not found.",
    )


def prepare_tool_call(state: MessagesState) -> dict:
    tool_call_message = AIMessage(
        content="",
        tool_calls=[
            {
                "name": "get_project_status",
                "args": {
                    "project_name": "Project Atlas",
                },
                "id": "call_101",
                "type": "tool_call",
            }
        ],
    )

    return {
        "messages": [
            tool_call_message,
        ]
    }


def build_graph():
    tool_node = ToolNode(
        tools=[
            get_project_status,
        ]
    )

    builder = StateGraph(
        MessagesState
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "prepare_tool_call",
        prepare_tool_call,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "tools",
        tool_node,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "prepare_tool_call",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "prepare_tool_call",
        "tools",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "tools",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    result = graph.invoke(
        {
            "messages": [],
        }
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH TOOL NODE")
    print("=" * 60)

    for message in result["messages"]:
        print("Message type:", message.type)

        if message.content:
            print("Content:", message.content)

        if getattr(message, "tool_calls", None):
            print("Tool calls:", message.tool_calls)

        if getattr(message, "tool_call_id", None):
            print(
                "Tool call ID:",
                message.tool_call_id,
            )

        print("-" * 60)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f. Interview Questions And Answers

Q1. What is a Tool Node in LangGraph?

A Tool Node is a prebuilt node that executes tools requested through structured tool calls and returns their results to the graph. It can handle multiple tools, parallel calls, state injection, store injection, and configurable errors.

Q2. What input does ToolNode commonly receive?

It commonly receives graph state containing a messages list whose latest AIMessage includes one or more tool calls. It can also receive direct tool-call lists for programmatic execution and testing.

Q3. What is tools_condition?

tools_condition is a prebuilt routing function for the common model-tool loop. It routes to tool execution when the latest AI message contains tool calls and otherwise ends the workflow.

Q4. Can ToolNode execute multiple tools in parallel?

Yes. ToolNode supports parallel execution when an incoming model message contains multiple independent tool calls.

Q5. Is a model-generated tool call automatically authorized?

No. A tool call is only a proposed operation. Deterministic application controls must validate identity, permission, arguments, business limits, resource ownership, and approval requirements before sensitive execution.


Topic 4: Custom Nodes

Custom Nodes are developer-defined functions that perform application-specific work inside a LangGraph workflow. They are useful when the application requires deterministic validation, authorization, data transformation, calculations, retrieval, formatting, confidence scoring, or business rules that do not belong inside an LLM or prebuilt tool node.

A node receives the current state, performs computation or a side effect, and returns a partial state update. Custom nodes can also receive an injected Runtime object containing run-scoped context, a persistent store, streaming utilities, previous results, and execution information.

a. Definition

A Custom Node is a developer-defined function registered as an executable step in a StateGraph. It implements logic specific to the application instead of relying on a prebuilt model, retriever, or tool node.

A custom node normally receives shared graph state and returns a partial update. The update is merged into the existing state according to the reducers configured for the returned fields.

Custom nodes may perform deterministic computation or controlled side effects. Examples include validating input, calculating totals, filtering retrieved evidence, querying an internal service, checking permissions, or formatting output.

A custom node can optionally receive runtime dependencies separately from mutable graph state. LangGraph injects the Runtime object into graph nodes, giving them access to immutable context, persistent storage, streaming utilities, previous values, and execution information.

b. Purpose

Custom Nodes implement business logic that should remain deterministic and auditable. Authorization, numeric limits, schema checks, status transitions, and cost controls should not depend on an LLM’s probabilistic output.

They connect a LangGraph workflow to application services. A node can call an approved database repository, enterprise API, vector retriever, cache, or validation component and return the result to shared state.

Custom Nodes divide complex workflows into independently testable operations. Teams can test input validation, project lookup, evidence checking, and answer formatting separately.

They provide explicit boundaries for reliability controls. Timeouts, retries, idempotency, error classification, telemetry, and security validation can be designed according to each node’s responsibility.

c. Architecture Diagram

                      CUSTOM NODE
                      ===========

                       Current State
                Request | Data | Status
                            |
                            v
                     Custom Node
              +-------------+-------------+
              |                           |
              v                           v
       Read State Fields          Access Runtime
                               Context | Store | Stream
              |                           |
              +-------------+-------------+
                            |
                            v
                   Application Logic
        Validate | Transform | Retrieve | Calculate
                            |
                +-----------+-----------+
                |                       |
              Success                 Failure
                |                       |
                v                       v
         Return Data Update      Return Error Update
                |                       |
                +-----------+-----------+
                            |
                            v
                      Updated State
                            |
                            v
                    Conditional Routing
                  /          |          \
                 v           v           v
            Continue       Retry        Stop

Architecture Explanation

An incoming edge activates the custom node.

The node receives the current shared state. It reads only the fields needed for its operation.

The node may also receive runtime context. Context can provide immutable values such as tenant ID, user ID, environment, feature settings, or service configuration.

The node performs one focused operation.

It returns a partial update describing its result.

The update is merged into shared state.

A fixed or conditional edge determines the next operation.

d. Core Components

1. Node Function

The node function implements one focused application operation. It follows the graph’s state and update contract. Its responsibilities should be clear from its name and output fields. A function named authorize_request should not also generate the final answer.

2. Input Dependency

An input dependency is a state or runtime value required by the node. The node should clearly document which fields and context values it expects. Minimal dependencies improve reusability and testing. A node that reads ten unrelated state fields is strongly coupled to the complete workflow.

3. Partial Update

A partial update contains the state fields created or changed by the node. The graph runtime merges it into shared state. Update values must match the state schema. Incorrect types can cause routing, persistence, or downstream processing failures.

4. Runtime Context

Runtime context supplies immutable invocation-specific values and dependencies. It prevents identity, environment, and service configuration from being hardcoded or stored in process globals. Context should not contain mutable workflow progress. Values that evolve across nodes belong in state.

5. Error Result

An error result represents an expected operational outcome such as not found, invalid, unauthorized, or temporarily unavailable. Error values should support deterministic routing. Stable status options allow the graph to retry, reject, fall back, or terminate safely.

e. Code Example

from dataclasses import dataclass
from typing import Literal, TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph
from langgraph.runtime import Runtime


@dataclass(frozen=True)
class RequestContext:
    tenant_id: str
    user_role: str


class ProjectState(TypedDict):
    project_name: str
    project_status: str
    source_id: str
    answer: str
    status: Literal[
        "pending",
        "valid",
        "invalid",
        "authorized",
        "unauthorized",
        "found",
        "not_found",
        "completed",
    ]
    error_message: str


PROJECTS = {
    ("TENANT-A", "Project Atlas"): {
        "status": "Ready for deployment",
        "source_id": "PROJECT-SYSTEM-101",
    },
    ("TENANT-B", "Project Orion"): {
        "status": "Development in progress",
        "source_id": "PROJECT-SYSTEM-202",
    },
}


def validate_input(state: ProjectState) -> dict:
    project_name = state["project_name"].strip()

    if not project_name:
        return {
            "status": "invalid",
            "error_message": (
                "The project name cannot be empty."
            ),
        }

    return {
        "project_name": project_name,
        "status": "valid",
        "error_message": "",
    }


def route_after_validation(
    state: ProjectState,
) -> Literal["authorize", "failure"]:
    if state["status"] == "valid":
        return "authorize"

    return "failure"


def authorize_request(
    state: ProjectState,
    runtime: Runtime[RequestContext],
) -> dict:
    allowed_roles = {
        "engineer",
        "manager",
    }

    if runtime.context.user_role not in allowed_roles:
        return {
            "status": "unauthorized",
            "error_message": (
                "The current role cannot view "
                "project status."
            ),
        }

    return {
        "status": "authorized",
        "error_message": "",
    }


def route_after_authorization(
    state: ProjectState,
) -> Literal["lookup", "failure"]:
    if state["status"] == "authorized":
        return "lookup"

    return "failure"


def lookup_project(
    state: ProjectState,
    runtime: Runtime[RequestContext],
) -> dict:
    key = (
        runtime.context.tenant_id,
        state["project_name"],
    )

    project = PROJECTS.get(key)

    if project is None:
        return {
            "status": "not_found",
            "error_message": (
                "No authorized project record was found."
            ),
        }

    return {
        "project_status": project["status"],
        "source_id": project["source_id"],
        "status": "found",
        "error_message": "",
    }


def route_after_lookup(
    state: ProjectState,
) -> Literal["format_answer", "failure"]:
    if state["status"] == "found":
        return "format_answer"

    return "failure"


def format_answer(state: ProjectState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            f"{state['project_name']} is currently "
            f"{state['project_status']}. "
            f"Source: {state['source_id']}."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def failure_response(state: ProjectState) -> dict:
    return {
        "answer": (
            state["error_message"]
            or "The request could not be completed."
        ),
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(
        state_schema=ProjectState,
        context_schema=RequestContext,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "validate_input",
        validate_input,
    )
    builder.add_node(
        "authorize",
        authorize_request,
    )
    builder.add_node(
        "lookup",
        lookup_project,
    )
    builder.add_node(
        "format_answer",
        format_answer,
    )
    builder.add_node(
        "failure",
        failure_response,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "validate_input",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "validate_input",
        route_after_validation,
        {
            "authorize": "authorize",
            "failure": "failure",
        },
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "authorize",
        route_after_authorization,
        {
            "lookup": "lookup",
            "failure": "failure",
        },
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "lookup",
        route_after_lookup,
        {
            "format_answer": "format_answer",
            "failure": "failure",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "format_answer",
        END,
    )
    builder.add_edge(
        "failure",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main() -> None:
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state: ProjectState = {
        "project_name": "Project Atlas",
        "project_status": "",
        "source_id": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
        "error_message": "",
    }

    context = RequestContext(
        tenant_id="TENANT-A",
        user_role="engineer",
    )

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state,
        context=context,
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH CUSTOM NODES")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Project:", result["project_name"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Answer:", result["answer"])
    print(
        "Error:",
        result["error_message"] or "None",
    )


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f. Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is a Custom Node in LangGraph?

A Custom Node is a developer-defined function or Runnable that performs an application-specific operation inside a graph. It reads current state and returns a partial state update.

Q2. When should a Custom Node be used?

Use it for validation, authorization, deterministic calculations, retrieval, transformation, formatting, business rules, or controlled external-service access.

Q3. What is the difference between state and runtime context?

State contains mutable workflow information that changes as nodes execute. Runtime context contains run-scoped dependencies and configuration, such as tenant identity or service settings.

Q4. How should expected errors be handled in a Custom Node?

Return stable status and error fields for expected outcomes such as invalid input, unauthorized access, or missing records. Conditional edges can route those outcomes to retries, fallbacks, controlled rejection, or human review.

Q5. How should side-effecting Custom Nodes be designed?

They should use deterministic authorization, argument validation, idempotency keys, transactions where applicable, explicit timeouts, audit logging, and human approval for consequential operations.

Topic 5: Input and Output State

a. Definition

Input State is the data accepted when the compiled graph is invoked. It usually contains the user request and other values required to start the workflow.

Internal State is the complete shared state used by nodes during execution. It may contain normalized input, routing categories, retrieved evidence, retry counters, errors, tool results, and other fields that callers do not need to supply or receive.

Output State defines the fields returned by the graph after execution completes. It can expose the final answer and status while hiding internal routing decisions, prompts, counters, and temporary processing data.

Individual nodes follow a similar input-output pattern. They read the current shared state and return partial updates rather than mutating the complete state directly.

b. Purpose

Separate schemas create a controlled public interface for the graph. Callers provide only legitimate input fields and receive only intended output fields.

They prevent internal workflow information from being exposed unnecessarily. Fields such as prompts, routing categories, retrieved document IDs, retry counts, and error details can remain inside the graph.

Input and output boundaries reduce accidental coupling. External applications depend on a small stable interface instead of depending on every field in the internal state schema.

They improve security and maintainability. Sensitive processing information can be excluded from output, while internal fields can evolve without requiring every caller to change.

c. Architecture Diagram

                  INPUT AND OUTPUT STATE
                  ======================

                       Graph Caller
                            |
                            v
                       Input Schema
                       Question Only
                            |
                            v
                          START
                            |
                            v
                   Internal Shared State
        Question | Normalized Query | Category
                 | Answer | Status
                            |
                    +-------+-------+
                    |               |
                    v               v
              Normalize Node   Classification Node
                    |               |
                    +-------+-------+
                            |
                            v
                       Answer Node
                            |
                            v
                           END
                            |
                            v
                      Output Schema
                    Answer | Status
                            |
                            v
                       Graph Caller

Architecture Explanation

1. The caller invokes the graph with data matching the input schema.

2. The input fields become part of the internal shared state.

3. Nodes create additional internal fields during execution.

4. Routing functions use internal control fields such as category or status.

5. The final node writes the answer and completion status.

6. The output schema filters the returned state. 

7. Internal fields remain available during execution but are not returned publicly.


d. Core Components

1. Input Schema

The input schema defines the fields accepted by the graph’s public invocation interface. It should contain only the values necessary to begin execution. A restricted input schema prevents callers from supplying internal control fields. External callers should not directly set approval decisions, authorization results, or verification statuses.

2. Internal State Schema

The internal schema contains all mutable information required by nodes and routing functions. It represents the complete graph execution snapshot. Internal fields should remain explicit and typed. One unrestricted dictionary hides dependencies and weakens maintenance.

3. Output Schema

The output schema determines which final state fields are returned publicly. It can expose the answer, status, citations, or another approved result. It protects internal information from unnecessary disclosure. Temporary prompts, routing fields, private document identifiers, and debugging values can remain hidden.

4. Node Input

Node input is the current state snapshot supplied to an active node. It includes values produced by the initial input and previously completed nodes. The node should read only relevant fields. Minimal read dependencies make node behavior easier to test and reuse.

5. Partial Node Output

Partial node output contains only fields created or changed by the node. LangGraph merges those updates into state. Its values must remain compatible with the internal state schema and configured reducers.

e. Code Example

from typing import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class InputState(TypedDict):
    question: str


class InternalState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    normalized_question: str
    category: str
    answer: str
    status: str


class OutputState(TypedDict):
    answer: str
    status: str


def normalize_input(state):
    question = state.get("question", "")
    normalized_question = " ".join(question.strip().split())

    return {
        "normalized_question": normalized_question,
        "status": "normalized",
    }


def classify_question(state):
    question = state.get(
        "normalized_question",
        "",
    ).lower()

    technical_terms = (
        "langgraph",
        "node",
        "state",
        "workflow",
    )

    is_technical = any(
        term in question
        for term in technical_terms
    )

    category = (
        "technical"
        if is_technical
        else "general"
    )

    return {
        "category": category,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_question(state):
    category = state.get(
        "category",
        "general",
    )

    if category == "technical":
        return "technical"

    return "general"


def technical_answer(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "A LangGraph node reads the current state "
            "and returns a partial state update."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def general_answer(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request followed the general path."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(
        state_schema=InternalState,
        input_schema=InputState,
        output_schema=OutputState,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "normalize",
        normalize_input,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "classify",
        classify_question,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "technical",
        technical_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "general",
        general_answer,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "normalize",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "normalize",
        "classify",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify",
        route_question,
        {
            "technical": "technical",
            "general": "general",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "technical",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "general",
        END,
    )

    graph = builder.compile()

    return graph


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    input_data = {
        "question": (
            "How do LangGraph nodes use state?"
        )
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        input_data
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("INPUT AND OUTPUT STATE")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Returned result:", result)
    print("Answer:", result.get("answer"))
    print("Status:", result.get("status"))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

f. Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Input State in LangGraph?

Input State defines the fields accepted when the graph is invoked. A separate input schema can prevent callers from supplying internal workflow fields.

Q2. What is Output State?

Output State defines which final state fields are returned after graph execution. It can expose the answer and status while hiding internal processing information.

Q3. What happens when separate schemas are not defined?

By default, the graph state schema is also used as the graph’s input and output schema.

Q4. Is a node’s output the same as the graph output schema?

No. A node returns a partial update to internal state. The graph output schema controls which fields from the completed state are returned to the caller.

Q5. Why should input, internal, and output schemas be separated?

They provide a safer and more stable public interface, prevent callers from controlling internal fields, and prevent internal workflow details from being returned unnecessarily.

Topic 6: Node Communication

Node Communication describes how LangGraph nodes exchange information and transfer execution control. Nodes do not normally call one another directly. Instead, they communicate through shared state, while fixed edges, conditional edges, reducers, Command, and Send determine how data and execution move through the graph.

LangGraph models workflows with three main components: shared state, nodes that return state updates, and edges that determine which node executes next. Its runtime uses message passing, and independently activated nodes can run within the same super-step.

a. Definition

Node Communication is the process through which one node makes its results available to other nodes. A node reads current state, performs an operation, and returns a partial update that LangGraph merges into shared state.

Nodes communicate indirectly through shared state rather than direct function calls. A classifier writes a category into state, a retriever reads that category and writes evidence, and a generator reads the evidence to produce an answer.

Edges communicate execution intent. Fixed edges always activate a known destination, while conditional edges inspect updated state and dynamically select the next node.

Advanced communication can combine state updates and routing. LangGraph’s Command can update graph state and direct execution to another node within one returned object.

b. Purpose

Node Communication allows complex workflows to be divided into focused components. Each node can perform one responsibility without knowing the complete implementation of other nodes.

It reduces tight coupling between processing steps. The retriever does not invoke the generator directly. It writes evidence into state, and the graph runtime activates the appropriate next node.

It supports dynamic execution. One node’s status, classification, tool result, or evidence-quality update can determine which node should execute next.

It supports sequential, looping, branching, and parallel workflows. State and reducers carry data, while the graph topology determines how execution progresses.

c. Architecture Diagram

                    NODE COMMUNICATION
                    ==================

                       Initial State
                            |
                            v
                      Classifier Node
                   Reads: user question
                   Writes: category
                            |
                            v
                      Shared State
              Question | Category | Evidence
                            |
                            v
                       Routing Edge
                   /                      \
                  v                        v
          Retrieval Node             General Node
          Reads: category            Reads: question
          Writes: evidence           Writes: answer
                  |
                  v
             Shared State
        Question | Category | Evidence
                  |
                  v
           Generation Node
           Reads: evidence
           Writes: answer
                  |
                  v
             Final State
                  |
                  v
                 END

Architecture Explanation

1. The caller supplies the initial state.
2. The classifier reads the question and returns a category update.
3. LangGraph merges the category into shared state.
4. A conditional edge reads the updated category.
5. The appropriate destination node becomes active.
6. The retrieval node adds evidence to shared state.
7. The generation node reads that evidence and creates the final answer.
8. The workflow reaches END and returns the final state.

d. Core Components

1. Shared State

Shared state carries information between graph nodes. It contains values written by previous nodes and read by downstream nodes. State should have explicit fields and types. Clear schemas make communication dependencies visible and testable.

2. State Update

A state update is the partial dictionary returned by a node. It represents the information that the node contributes to the workflow. LangGraph merges the update into shared state. Fields use replacement behavior or configured reducers.

3. Edge

An edge carries execution from one node to another. Fixed edges represent known transitions, while conditional edges select destinations dynamically. An edge controls execution rather than application processing. The work remains inside the destination nodes.

4. Reducer

A reducer combines updates to a shared state field. It is important when several nodes or repeated iterations contribute to one accumulated value. Reducers should match the field’s semantics. Status usually uses replacement, while evidence and event history may use accumulation.

5. Command

Command combines state updates with navigation instructions. It can update state and identify the node or nodes that should execute next. It is useful for node-owned handoffs. However, ordinary edges are often clearer when update and routing responsibilities should remain separate.

e. Code Example

from typing import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class CommunicationState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    category: str
    evidence: list[str]
    answer: str
    status: str


def classify_question(state):
    question = state["question"].lower()

    technical_terms = (
        "langgraph",
        "node",
        "state",
        "workflow",
    )

    category = (
        "technical"
        if any(
            term in question
            for term in technical_terms
        )
        else "general"
    )

    return {
        "category": category,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_after_classification(state):
    if state["category"] == "technical":
        return "retrieve"

    return "general"


def retrieve_evidence(state):
    knowledge_base = [
        (
            "LangGraph nodes communicate through "
            "shared state."
        ),
        (
            "Edges determine which node executes next."
        ),
    ]

    return {
        "evidence": knowledge_base,
        "status": "evidence_ready",
    }


def route_after_retrieval(state):
    if state["evidence"\]:
        return "generate"

    return "no_evidence"


def generate_answer(state):
    answer = " ".join(
        state["evidence"]
    )

    return {
        "answer": answer,
        "status": "completed",
    }


def general_answer(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request followed the general path."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def no_evidence_answer(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "No supporting evidence was available."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(
        CommunicationState
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "classify",
        classify_question,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "retrieve",
        retrieve_evidence,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "generate",
        generate_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "general",
        general_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "no_evidence",
        no_evidence_answer,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify",
        route_after_classification,
        {
            "retrieve": "retrieve",
            "general": "general",
        },
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "retrieve",
        route_after_retrieval,
        {
            "generate": "generate",
            "no_evidence": "no_evidence",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "generate",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "general",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "no_evidence",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state = {
        "question": (
            "How do LangGraph nodes communicate?"
        ),
        "category": "",
        "evidence": [],
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH NODE COMMUNICATION")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result["question"])
    print("Category:", result["category"])
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Evidence:")

    for item in result["evidence"\]:
        print("-", item)

    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f. Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. How do LangGraph nodes communicate?

Nodes communicate primarily through shared graph state. One node returns a partial state update, LangGraph merges it, and downstream nodes read the updated state.

Q2. Do nodes call one another directly?

Normally, no. Nodes perform their own work and return updates. Fixed or conditional edges determine which node executes next.

Q3. How do parallel nodes communicate their results?

Each parallel node returns a state update. Reducers combine compatible updates into shared state before dependent downstream nodes execute.

Q4. What is Command in node communication?

Command is a LangGraph type that can combine a state update with a navigation instruction through its update and goto values.

Q5. What is the difference between data communication and control communication?

Data communication occurs through shared-state updates, such as storing retrieved evidence. Control communication occurs through edges or commands that determine which node executes next.

Topic 7: Node Design Best Practices

Well-designed nodes are focused, predictable, testable, secure, and observable. LangGraph guidance recommends first breaking a process into discrete steps, with each node responsible for one specific operation and shared state connecting those operations.

LangGraph allows deterministic and model-driven steps in the same workflow. A strong design uses deterministic nodes for strict rules and LLM nodes only where semantic reasoning is useful.

a. Definition

Node Design Best Practices are principles for creating clear, reliable, and maintainable graph operations. They define how nodes should read state, perform work, return updates, handle errors, and interact with external systems.

A well-designed node should have one focused responsibility. Validation, retrieval, generation, and verification should normally be implemented as separate nodes rather than combined into one large operation.

A node should communicate through explicit state fields and partial updates. It should read only the information it requires and return only the fields it changes.

Production nodes must handle reliability and security concerns. Remote calls need timeouts and bounded retries, while side-effecting operations require authorization, idempotency, and audit logging.

b. Purpose

Good node design keeps the workflow understandable as it grows. Clear responsibilities make it easier to determine which node owns a particular operation or state field.

It improves testing and debugging. A focused node can be tested independently with controlled input state and an expected partial update.

It reduces unintended coupling. Nodes that depend on only a few explicit fields are easier to reuse and modify.

It improves production reliability. Validation, failure routing, timeouts, retry limits, and observability can be implemented around meaningful execution boundaries.

c. Architecture Diagram

                  WELL-DESIGNED NODE WORKFLOW
                  ===========================

                         User Input
                              |
                              v
                    validate_input
              Reads: question
              Writes: normalized_question, status
                              |
                     +--------+--------+
                     |                 |
                   Valid             Invalid
                     |                 |
                     v                 v
             retrieve_documents   failure_response
              Reads: normalized       |
              Writes: documents       |
                     |                 |
              +------+-------+         |
              |              |         |
           Evidence       No evidence  |
              |              |         |
              v              v         |
       generate_answer  failure_response
              |              |
              +------+-------+
                     |
                     v
                    END

Architecture Explanation

1. Input validation is isolated from retrieval.
2. The validation node returns a clear status.
3. Routing logic selects retrieval or controlled failure.
4. The retrieval node performs only evidence lookup.
5. Generation occurs only when evidence is available.
6. Expected failures converge at a controlled response node.
7. Every path reaches an explicit termination point.

d. Core Components

1. Single Responsibility

A node should perform one clearly defined operation. Focused nodes are easier to test, reuse, trace, and retry. The responsibility should match the node name. A node named retrieve_documents should not also generate the final answer or approve a transaction.

2. Explicit State Contract

Each node should clearly define the fields it reads and writes. This makes dependencies visible and reduces accidental coupling. Returned values must remain compatible with the state schema. Incorrect types can break routing, reducers, persistence, or downstream nodes.

3. Failure Policy

Every remote or fallible node should define how expected failures are represented. The graph may retry, fall back, escalate, or terminate safely. The policy should distinguish temporary and permanent failures. Authorization denial should not follow the same retry path as a temporary network timeout.

4. Execution Limit

Execution limits prevent nodes and loops from consuming uncontrolled resources. Limits may cover retries, model calls, tool calls, time, tokens, or cost. Reaching a limit should produce a controlled outcome. The workflow can abstain, return verified partial results, or request human review.

5. Observability Boundary

A node boundary should represent a meaningful operation that can be traced independently. Observability should include duration, status, configuration version, and sanitized result metadata. This supports debugging without exposing confidential content.

e. Code Example

from typing import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    normalized_question: str
    documents: list[str]
    answer: str
    status: str
    error_message: str


KNOWLEDGE_BASE = [
    "Focused nodes are easier to test and maintain.",
    "Nodes should return partial state updates.",
]


def validate_input(state):
    question = state["question"].strip()

    if not question:
        return {
            "status": "invalid",
            "error_message": (
                "The question cannot be empty."
            ),
        }

    return {
        "normalized_question": (
            " ".join(question.split())
        ),
        "status": "valid",
        "error_message": "",
    }


def route_after_validation(state):
    if state["status"] == "valid":
        return "retrieve"

    return "failure"


def retrieve_documents(state):
    query_terms = set(
        state["normalized_question"].lower().split()
    )

    matches = []

    for document in KNOWLEDGE_BASE:
        document_terms = set(
            document.lower().split()
        )

        if query_terms.intersection(document_terms):
            matches.append(document)

    if not matches:
        return {
            "documents": [],
            "status": "no_evidence",
            "error_message": (
                "No supporting evidence was found."
            ),
        }

    return {
        "documents": matches,
        "status": "evidence_ready",
        "error_message": "",
    }


def route_after_retrieval(state):
    if state["status"] == "evidence_ready":
        return "generate"

    return "failure"


def generate_answer(state):
    return {
        "answer": " ".join(
            state["documents"]
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def failure_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": state["error_message"],
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(
        WorkflowState
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "validate_input",
        validate_input,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "retrieve_documents",
        retrieve_documents,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "generate_answer",
        generate_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "failure_response",
        failure_response,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "validate_input",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "validate_input",
        route_after_validation,
        {
            "retrieve": "retrieve_documents",
            "failure": "failure_response",
        },
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "retrieve_documents",
        route_after_retrieval,
        {
            "generate": "generate_answer",
            "failure": "failure_response",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "generate_answer",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "failure_response",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    result = graph.invoke(
        {
            "question": (
                "What are node best practices?"
            ),
            "normalized_question": "",
            "documents": [],
            "answer": "",
            "status": "pending",
            "error_message": "",
        }
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("NODE DESIGN BEST PRACTICES")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Status:", result["status"])
    print("Documents:", result["documents"])
    print("Answer:", result["answer"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f. Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is the most important node-design principle?

A node should have one focused responsibility. This makes the node easier to test, reuse, trace, retry, and maintain.

Q2. Why should nodes return partial updates?

Partial updates clearly show which state fields the node owns. LangGraph preserves unchanged fields and applies reducers to fields included in the update.

Q3. Why should routing be separated from node processing?

Separating routing keeps node logic focused and makes transitions visible in the graph. Routing functions should decide the next path without performing unrelated external operations.

Q4. How should side-effecting nodes be designed?

They should use deterministic authorization, input validation, idempotency keys, transactions where applicable, timeouts, audit logging, and human approval for consequential operations.

Q5. What should be monitored for each production node?

Monitor node name, latency, status, errors, retries, model or tool usage, sanitized input and output summaries, and the selected outgoing route.

Chapter 4: Edges

Topic 1: Edge Fundamentals

a. Definition

An edge is a connection that controls execution flow between graph nodes. After a source node finishes and its state update is applied, its outgoing edge activates the next eligible node.

Edges do not normally perform business logic themselves. Validation, retrieval, model invocation, calculations, and formatting belong inside nodes, while edges define transitions between those operations.

Edges may represent fixed or state-dependent transitions. A fixed edge always selects the same destination, while a conditional edge examines current state and dynamically selects a route.

Edges can create more than simple linear workflows. They support entry paths, terminal paths, branches, loops, parallel execution, and synchronization between multiple upstream nodes.

b. Purpose

Edges separate processing logic from control flow. Nodes focus on what work must be performed, while edges determine the order in which that work occurs.

Edges make the workflow structure visible and auditable. Developers can inspect the graph and understand which transitions, retries, branches, and termination paths are possible.

They support state-driven execution. A node can update fields such as status, category, or evidence_sufficient, and the graph can use those updates to select the next operation.

Edges allow LangGraph to manage node activation. Nodes do not need to call downstream functions directly, which preserves graph-level tracing, streaming, checkpointing, and error isolation.

c. Architecture Diagram

                    LANGGRAPH EDGES
                    ===============

                       Graph Input
                            |
                            v
                          START
                            |
                      Entry Edge
                            |
                            v
                     Validation Node
                            |
                   Sequential Edge
                            |
                            v
                     Processing Node
                            |
                 +----------+----------+
                 |                     |
           Conditional Edge      Conditional Edge
                 |                     |
                 v                     v
          Success Node            Failure Node
                 |                     |
            Terminal Edge         Terminal Edge
                 |                     |
                 +----------+----------+
                            |
                            v
                           END


Architecture Explanation

1. START represents the virtual entry point. An entry edge connects it to the first executable node.
2. The first node performs its operation. Its partial update is merged into shared state.
3. A sequential edge activates the known next node.
4. A conditional edge may inspect updated state. It can route execution to success, failure, retry, or another approved path.
5. The selected terminal node completes its operation.
6. An edge to END terminates that execution path.

d. Core Components

1. Source Node

The source node is the operation from which an edge originates. Its execution and state update must complete before a fixed downstream transition occurs. A source node may have one or several outgoing edges. Multiple outgoing edges can create parallel branches or conditional alternatives.

2. Destination Node

The destination node is activated by the incoming edge. It receives the graph state after the source node’s updates have been applied. The destination should depend only on fields guaranteed to exist at that point. Missing state assumptions can create runtime errors.

3. Entry Edge

An entry edge connects START to the first application node. It defines where graph processing begins. A graph may start with one or several branches. Multiple starting branches should be independent or use compatible reducers for shared updates.

4. Terminal Edge

A terminal edge connects a completed application path to END. It indicates that no additional node is required on that branch. Successful and controlled-failure paths may have separate terminal nodes. Both can connect to END after producing suitable final state.

5. Transition Path

A transition path is the sequence of nodes and edges followed during one graph execution. Different inputs may follow different paths in the same graph. Tracing the selected path helps explain the resulting answer or failure.

e. Code Example

from typing import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class EdgeState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    normalized_question: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def normalize_question(state):
    question = state.get("question", "")

    normalized = " ".join(
        question.strip().split()
    )

    return {
        "normalized_question": normalized,
        "status": "normalized",
    }


def create_answer(state):
    normalized_question = state.get(
        "normalized_question",
        "",
    )

    answer = (
        "An edge determines which LangGraph node "
        "executes next. Processed question: "
        + normalized_question
    )

    return {
        "answer": answer,
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(EdgeState)

    builder.add_node(
        "normalize_question",
        normalize_question,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "create_answer",
        create_answer,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "normalize_question",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "normalize_question",
        "create_answer",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "create_answer",
        END,
    )

    graph = builder.compile()

    return graph


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state = {
        "question": "What is an edge in LangGraph?",
        "normalized_question": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(initial_state)

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH EDGE FUNDAMENTALS")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result.get("question"))
    print(
        "Normalized question:",
        result.get("normalized_question"),
    )
    print("Status:", result.get("status"))
    print("Answer:", result.get("answer"))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f. Interview Questions And Answers

Q1. What is an edge in LangGraph?

An edge is a graph connection that determines which node becomes active after another node completes. Nodes perform the work, while edges control execution flow.

Q2. What is a sequential edge?

A sequential edge is an unconditional transition from one node to another. Whenever the source node finishes, the destination node becomes eligible to execute.

Q3. When is a conditional edge required?

A conditional edge is required when the next node depends on current state, such as request category, evidence availability, authorization, retry count, or tool result.

Q4. Can one node have multiple outgoing edges?

Yes. Multiple outgoing edges can activate independent parallel branches or represent alternative state-dependent routes. Parallel updates to the same state fields require compatible reducers.

Q5. What is the difference between an edge and a state update?

A state update changes workflow data. An edge changes workflow control by determining which node executes next.

Topic 2: START Node

a. Definition

START is a virtual node representing the beginning of graph execution. When the compiled graph is invoked, nodes connected to START become eligible to execute.

It is not a normal processing node. It does not validate input, call a model, retrieve documents, or return state updates.

An edge from START defines the graph’s entry path. In a basic workflow, START connects directly to one initial node.

START can connect to multiple nodes when several independent operations should begin from the same initial state. Those nodes can execute in the same super-step when the graph topology and state reducers support parallel execution.

b. Purpose

START provides an explicit workflow entry point. A developer can inspect the graph and immediately determine which operation begins execution.

It separates graph input from application processing. The caller supplies initial state, START activates the entry path, and the first real node performs the actual work.

It supports different entry structures. A graph may begin with one validation node, several independent retrieval nodes, or routing logic that chooses an initial processing path.

It helps LangGraph manage execution consistently. The runtime uses graph connections to determine which inactive nodes should become active after receiving state through their incoming edges.

c. Architecture Diagram

                         USER INPUT
                              |
                              v
                       Initial State
                              |
                              v
                            START
                              |
                         Entry Edge
                              |
                              v
                      validate_input
                              |
                              v
                     normalize_request
                              |
                              v
                       process_request
                              |
                              v
                             END

Architecture Explanation
1.The caller invokes the compiled graph with initial state.
2. The state enters the graph through the virtual START point.
3. The edge from START activates validate_input.
4. The validation node reads the initial state and returns an update.
5. Subsequent edges continue normal workflow execution.
6. The graph eventually reaches END and returns its final state.

d. Core Components

1. Initial State

Initial state is the data supplied when the compiled graph is invoked. It should contain the fields required by the first executable node. External input should be validated at the graph boundary. The caller should not be permitted to control trusted internal fields such as authorization or approval decisions.

2. START Constant

START is LangGraph’s reserved virtual entry point. It represents the beginning of graph-style execution.  It should not be replaced by a user-defined node named START. Developers import and use the provided constant.

3. Entry Edge

The entry edge connects START to the first executable node. It determines which operation receives initial state. Several entry edges can activate independent branches. State reducers must support any overlapping updates from those branches.

4. First Executable Node

The first executable node performs the workflow’s first real operation. It commonly validates, normalizes, authenticates, initializes, or classifies the request. It receives the graph’s invocation state through the edge from START. Its returned update becomes available to downstream nodes.

e. Code Example

from typing import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class StartState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    normalized_question: str
    answer: str
    status: str
    error_message: str


def validate_input(state):
    question = state.get("question", "").strip()

    if not question:
        return {
            "status": "invalid",
            "error_message": "The question cannot be empty.",
        }

    normalized_question = " ".join(
        question.split()
    )

    return {
        "normalized_question": normalized_question,
        "status": "valid",
        "error_message": "",
    }


def route_after_validation(state):
    if state.get("status") == "valid":
        return "create_answer"

    return "invalid_response"


def create_answer(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "START is the virtual entry point "
            "of a LangGraph workflow."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def invalid_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": state.get(
            "error_message",
            "The request is invalid.",
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(StartState)

    builder.add_node(
        "validate_input",
        validate_input,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "create_answer",
        create_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "invalid_response",
        invalid_response,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "validate_input",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "validate_input",
        route_after_validation,
        {
            "create_answer": "create_answer",
            "invalid_response": "invalid_response",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "create_answer",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "invalid_response",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state = {
        "question": "What is START in LangGraph?",
        "normalized_question": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
        "error_message": "",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(initial_state)

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH START NODE")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result.get("question"))
    print(
        "Normalized question:",
        result.get("normalized_question"),
    )
    print("Status:", result.get("status"))
    print("Answer:", result.get("answer"))
    print(
        "Error:",
        result.get("error_message") or "None",
    )


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f. Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is START in LangGraph?

START is LangGraph’s reserved virtual entry point. An edge from START identifies which node or nodes should begin execution.

Q2. Is START a normal executable node?

No. START does not contain application logic and is not registered with add_node(). It marks the graph’s entry boundary.

Q3. Can START connect to multiple nodes?

Yes. Several edges from START can activate independent branches. If those branches update the same field, an appropriate reducer is required.

Q4. What should the first real node do?

The first node commonly validates, authenticates, normalizes, initializes, or classifies the request before more expensive or sensitive operations execute.

Q5. How is START connected to the first node?

Use add_edge():

builder.add_edge(START, "first_node",)

Topic 3: END Node

a. Definition

END marks the termination of a graph execution path. When execution reaches END, no additional application node is activated along that path.

END is a virtual node rather than a normal processing node. It does not invoke a model, update state, validate output, or execute business logic.

A real application node normally performs the final operation before transitioning to END. That node might format an answer, create a controlled failure response, record a final status, or validate output.

A graph can contain multiple edges to END. Successful, rejected, unsupported, and not-found paths can all terminate separately after placing the appropriate result in state.

b. Purpose

END provides an explicit termination boundary. Developers can inspect the graph and identify where each execution path completes.

It prevents the graph from continuing after the final operation. Once the selected path reaches END, the compiled graph returns the completed output state.

It supports multiple controlled outcomes. A successful response and a controlled error response can reach `END through different terminal nodes.

It provides a destination for conditional termination. A router can return END when no additional processing is required.

c. Architecture Diagram

                         END NODE
                         ========

                        User Input
                             |
                             v
                           START
                             |
                             v
                      validate_input
                             |
                    +--------+--------+
                    |                 |
                  Valid             Invalid
                    |                 |
                    v                 v
               process_request   failure_response
                    |                 |
                    v                 v
               success_response      END
                    |
                    v
                   END
                    |
                    v
              Final State Returned

d. Core Components

1. Final Processing Node

The final processing node performs the last application operation before termination. It commonly creates an answer, formats output, or produces a safe failure response. It should populate all fields required by the graph’s output schema. END does not fill missing answer or status fields.

2. Terminal Edge

A terminal edge connects a real application node to END. It indicates that no further processing is required along that path.

3. Final State

Final state is the completed graph state available after the execution path terminates. When an output schema is configured, only the approved output fields are returned to the caller.

4. Terminal Condition

A terminal condition determines when processing should stop. It may represent success, rejection, missing evidence, a limit, or an unrecoverable condition. Every branch and cycle should have a deliberate terminal condition.


e. Code Example

from typing import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class EndState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    answer: str
    status: str
    error_message: str


def validate_input(state):
    question = state.get("question", "").strip()

    if not question:
        return {
            "status": "invalid",
            "error_message": "The question cannot be empty.",
        }

    return {
        "status": "valid",
        "error_message": "",
    }


def route_after_validation(state):
    if state.get("status") == "valid":
        return "success"

    return "failure"


def create_success_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "END is the virtual termination point "
            "of a LangGraph execution path."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def create_failure_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": state.get(
            "error_message",
            "The request could not be completed.",
        ),
        "status": "failed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(EndState)

    builder.add_node(
        "validate_input",
        validate_input,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "success_response",
        create_success_response,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "failure_response",
        create_failure_response,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "validate_input",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "validate_input",
        route_after_validation,
        {
            "success": "success_response",
            "failure": "failure_response",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "success_response",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "failure_response",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state = {
        "question": "What is END in LangGraph?",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
        "error_message": "",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(initial_state)

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH END NODE")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result.get("question"))
    print("Status:", result.get("status"))
    print("Answer:", result.get("answer"))
    print(
        "Error:",
        result.get("error_message") or "None",
    )


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f. Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is END in LangGraph?

END is LangGraph’s reserved virtual termination point. It represents the final node of a graph execution path.

Q2. Is END a normal processing node?

No. It does not execute application logic or modify state. A real node must produce the required final update before transitioning to END.

Q3. Can multiple nodes connect to END?

Yes. Successful, failed, rejected, unsupported, and not-found paths can each connect to END.

Q4. Can a conditional edge route directly to END?

Yes. However, the source node must already have produced all required output fields because no additional response node will execute.

Q5. Why must loops have a path to END?

A reachable terminal path prevents retries, retrieval cycles, or reflection loops from continuing indefinitely.


Topic 4: Sequential Edges

a. Definition

A Sequential Edge is an unconditional connection between two graph nodes. It ensures that one node executes after another in a predefined order.
Sequential edges create deterministic workflow paths. Every request following that path executes the same nodes in the same sequence.
The destination node receives state after the source node’s update has been applied. This allows each node to consume values produced by the previous node.
Sequential edges are created with add_edge(). They are appropriate when the next node does not depend on a runtime decision.

b. Purpose

Sequential edges define processing dependencies. A generator should execute only after retrieval has produced evidence.

They separate individual workflow responsibilities. Validation, normalization, retrieval, generation, and formatting can remain separate while executing in a controlled order.

They make execution easier to trace and test. The workflow follows one visible path without hidden function calls.

They support state handoff between nodes. One node writes fields into state, and the next node reads those updated values.

c. Architecture Diagram

                       SEQUENTIAL EDGES
                       ================

                          Initial State
                               |
                               v
                             START
                               |
                               v
                       validate_input
                               |
                               v
                      normalize_question
                               |
                               v
                         create_answer
                               |
                               v
                        format_response
                               |
                               v
                              END

d. Core Components

1. Source Node
The source node executes before the transition occurs. Its state update becomes available before the destination node runs.

2. Destination Node
The destination node executes after the source node completes.
It can read state values written by the source node.

3. Directed Edge
The edge defines one-way execution from source to destination It does not automatically create a route back to the source node.

4. Updated State
Updated state is the merged state after the source node returns. The destination receives this updated snapshot rather than the earlier state.

5. Terminal Edge
A terminal edge connects the final processing node to END. It marks completion of the sequential path.


e. Code Example

from typing import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class SequentialState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    normalized_question: str
    draft_answer: str
    final_answer: str
    status: str


def validate_input(state):
    question = state.get("question", "").strip()

    if not question:
        return {
            "draft_answer": (
                "The question cannot be empty."
            ),
            "status": "invalid",
        }

    return {
        "status": "valid",
    }


def normalize_question(state):
    if state.get("status") != "valid":
        return {
            "normalized_question": "",
        }

    normalized = " ".join(
        state.get("question", "").strip().split()
    )

    return {
        "normalized_question": normalized,
        "status": "normalized",
    }


def create_draft_answer(state):
    if state.get("status") != "normalized":
        return {
            "draft_answer": (
                state.get("draft_answer")
                or "The request could not be processed."
            ),
        }

    return {
        "draft_answer": (
            "Sequential edges execute nodes "
            "in a fixed order."
        ),
        "status": "draft_created",
    }


def format_final_answer(state):
    draft = state.get(
        "draft_answer",
        "No answer was created.",
    )

    return {
        "final_answer": (
            "Final response: " + draft
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
