    builder = StateGraph(
        SequentialState
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "validate_input",
        validate_input,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "normalize_question",
        normalize_question,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "create_draft_answer",
        create_draft_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "format_final_answer",
        format_final_answer,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "validate_input",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "validate_input",
        "normalize_question",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "normalize_question",
        "create_draft_answer",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "create_draft_answer",
        "format_final_answer",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "format_final_answer",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state = {
        "question": (
            "What are sequential edges?"
        ),
        "normalized_question": "",
        "draft_answer": "",
        "final_answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH SEQUENTIAL EDGES")
    print("=" * 60)

    print(
        "Question:",
        result.get("question"),
    )

    print(
        "Normalized question:",
        result.get("normalized_question"),
    )

    print(
        "Draft answer:",
        result.get("draft_answer"),
    )

    print(
        "Status:",
        result.get("status"),
    )

    print(
        "Final answer:",
        result.get("final_answer"),
    )


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f. Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is a Sequential Edge?

A Sequential Edge is an unconditional directed connection from one node to another. The destination node executes after the source node completes.

Q2. Does the destination receive the source node’s updated state?

Yes. The source node’s update is applied before the destination node executes.

Q3. When should a conditional edge replace a sequential edge?

Use a conditional edge when the next node depends on status, category, authorization, evidence availability, retry count, or another runtime value.

Q4. What happens when multiple source nodes are passed to add_edge()?

LangGraph waits for all listed source nodes to complete before executing the destination node.


Topic 5: State Transitions

a. Definition

A State Transition is the movement from one workflow state snapshot to another. A node reads the current state, performs an operation, and returns fields that should change.

A transition includes both state modification and execution movement. The node changes workflow data, while an outgoing edge activates the next node.

The destination node receives the updated state. It sees the values returned by the previous node after LangGraph applies the relevant reducers.

Transitions may be deterministic or conditional. A fixed edge always moves to the same destination, while a conditional edge selects a route according to the updated state.

b. Purpose

State transitions allow the workflow to evolve over time. Values such as status, evidence, retry count, approval, and answer change as different nodes execute.

They connect processing outcomes with execution decisions. A retrieval node can set status to evidence_ready, allowing the graph to continue to generation.

State transitions make workflow behavior inspectable. Developers can compare state before and after each node and determine why the graph followed a particular route.

They support branching, loops, recovery, and termination. The graph can continue, retry, use a fallback, stop safely, or request human input according to the current state.

c. Architecture Diagram

                    STATE TRANSITION
                    ================

                       Current State
          Question | Attempts | Evidence | Status
                            |
                            v
                       Active Node
                            |
                            v
                    Perform Operation
                            |
                            v
                  Return Partial Update
                 Attempts | Evidence | Status
                            |
                            v
                     Apply Reducers
                            |
                            v
                       Updated State
                            |
                            v
                    Evaluate Outgoing Edge
                       /             \
                      v               v
                   Continue          Retry
                      |               |
                      v               v
                  Next Node      Previous Node

Architecture Explanation
1. The active node receives the current state snapshot.
2. The node performs its assigned operation.
3. It returns a partial state update.
4. LangGraph merges the update into existing state.
5. The resulting state becomes the new workflow snapshot.
6. A fixed or conditional edge determines the next node.
7. The destination receives the updated state.


d. Core Components

1. Current State
Current state is the workflow snapshot before the active node executes.
It includes values produced by initial input and previously completed nodes.

2. State Update
A state update is the partial dictionary returned by the node.
It contains only the fields created or modified during that operation.

3. Reducer
A reducer defines how the incoming update is combined with the existing field value.
The resulting value becomes part of the new state snapshot.

4. Transition Edge
The transition edge activates the next node after the update has been applied.
It may be fixed, conditional, looping, parallel, or terminal.

5. Destination Node
The destination node receives the updated state.
It should depend only on values guaranteed to exist at that execution stage.


d. Code Example

from typing import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class TransitionState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    attempts: int
    evidence: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def validate_input(state):
    question = state.get("question", "").strip()

    if not question:
        return {
            "status": "invalid",
        }

    return {
        "status": "valid",
    }


def route_after_validation(state):
    if state.get("status") == "valid":
        return "retrieve"

    return "stop"


def retrieve_evidence(state):
    next_attempt = state.get("attempts", 0) + 1

    if next_attempt == 1:
        return {
            "attempts": next_attempt,
            "evidence": "",
            "status": "retry_required",
        }

    return {
        "attempts": next_attempt,
        "evidence": (
            "A state transition updates graph data "
            "and moves execution to another node."
        ),
        "status": "evidence_ready",
    }


def route_after_retrieval(state):
    if state.get("status") == "evidence_ready":
        return "generate"

    if state.get("attempts", 0) >= 2:
        return "stop"

    return "retry"


def generate_answer(state):
    return {
        "answer": state.get(
            "evidence",
            "No evidence was available.",
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def stop_workflow(state):
    if state.get("status") == "invalid":
        message = "The question cannot be empty."
    else:
        message = (
            "The workflow stopped because sufficient "
            "evidence was not found."
        )

    return {
        "answer": message,
        "status": "stopped",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(TransitionState)

    builder.add_node(
        "validate_input",
        validate_input,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "retrieve_evidence",
        retrieve_evidence,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "generate_answer",
        generate_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "stop_workflow",
        stop_workflow,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "validate_input",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "validate_input",
        route_after_validation,
        {
            "retrieve": "retrieve_evidence",
            "stop": "stop_workflow",
        },
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "retrieve_evidence",
        route_after_retrieval,
        {
            "retry": "retrieve_evidence",
            "generate": "generate_answer",
            "stop": "stop_workflow",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "generate_answer",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "stop_workflow",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    initial_state = {
        "question": "What is a state transition?",
        "attempts": 0,
        "evidence": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(initial_state)

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH STATE TRANSITIONS")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result.get("question"))
    print("Attempts:", result.get("attempts"))
    print("Evidence:", result.get("evidence"))
    print("Status:", result.get("status"))
    print("Answer:", result.get("answer"))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

e. Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is a State Transition in LangGraph?

A State Transition occurs when a node returns an update, LangGraph applies that update, and execution moves to the next node through an edge.

Q2. Does the destination node receive updated state?

Yes. The source node’s state update is applied before the destination node executes.

Q3. What is the difference between a state update and a state transition?

A state update changes workflow data. A state transition includes the data change and movement of execution to another workflow stage.

Q4. How are conditional state transitions implemented?

A node updates state, then a routing function inspects the updated values and returns a route mapped to the next node.

Q5. How do you prevent infinite transitions?

Use maximum attempts, execution timeouts, cost limits, duplicate-result detection, no-progress checks, and explicit terminal paths.


Topic 6: Execution Flow

Execution Flow describes how LangGraph processes input from START to END. During execution, the runtime activates nodes, applies their state updates, evaluates outgoing edges, and continues until no additional nodes remain active.

LangGraph uses a message-passing runtime inspired by Pregel. Sequential nodes run in separate super-steps, while independent nodes can run in parallel during the same super-step.

a. Definition

Execution Flow is the ordered movement of state and control through a compiled LangGraph workflow. It begins when the graph receives input and ends when the selected path reaches END, pauses, or fails.

Nodes perform processing during the execution flow. Every active node receives the current state and returns a partial state update.

Edges control movement between nodes. Fixed edges create predictable sequences, while conditional edges dynamically select the next node using updated state.

The runtime manages node activation and state visibility. Updates produced during one execution step become available to dependent nodes in the next step.

b. Purpose

Execution Flow makes multi-step application behavior predictable. Developers can identify which nodes run, in what order, and under which conditions.

It connects state changes with workflow control. A node can update category, status, or retry_count, allowing the graph to select the appropriate next step.

It enables observable execution. Applications can stream node updates, display progress, and inspect the output produced by every operation.

It supports advanced workflows. Sequential processing, conditional branches, parallel execution, retries, persistence, and human intervention all build on the execution-flow model.

c. Execution Flow Diagram

                     LANGGRAPH EXECUTION FLOW
                     ========================

                         Initial Input
                              |
                              v
                            START
                              |
                              v
                     normalize_question
                              |
                     State update applied
                              |
                              v
                     classify_question
                              |
                     State update applied
                              |
                    +---------+---------+
                    |                   |
              Technical             General
                    |                   |
                    v                   v
           technical_answer      general_answer
                    |                   |
                    +---------+---------+
                              |
                              v
                             END
                              |
                              v
                       Final State

d. Execution Flow Diagram

                     LANGGRAPH EXECUTION FLOW
                     ========================

                         Initial Input
                              |
                              v
                            START
                              |
                              v
                     normalize_question
                              |
                     State update applied
                              |
                              v
                     classify_question
                              |
                     State update applied
                              |
                    +---------+---------+
                    |                   |
              Technical             General
                    |                   |
                    v                   v
           technical_answer      general_answer
                    |                   |
                    +---------+---------+
                              |
                              v
                             END
                              |
                              v
                       Final State

e. Core Components

1. Initial State

The initial state contains the values supplied when execution begins. It must provide the input required by the first executable node.

2. Active Node

An active node is selected to execute during the current graph step. It reads state, performs work, and returns a partial update.

3. State Update

A state update contains fields created or changed by a node. LangGraph merges it into shared state before dependent nodes execute.

4. Outgoing Edge

An outgoing edge determines which node becomes active next. It may be fixed, conditional, looping, parallel, or terminal.

5. Terminal State

Terminal state is the completed graph snapshot returned after execution reaches END. It should contain every field required by the graph’s output interface.


f. Code Example

from typing import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class FlowState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    normalized_question: str
    category: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def normalize_question(state):
    question = state.get(
        "question",
        "",
    )

    normalized = " ".join(
        question.strip().split()
    )

    return {
        "normalized_question": normalized,
        "status": "normalized",
    }


def classify_question(state):
    question = state.get(
        "normalized_question",
        "",
    ).lower()

    if "langgraph" in question:
        category = "technical"
    else:
        category = "general"

    return {
        "category": category,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_question(state):
    if state.get("category") == "technical":
        return "technical"

    return "general"


def technical_answer(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "LangGraph execution starts at START, "
            "runs active nodes, applies state updates, "
            "follows edges, and ends at END."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def general_answer(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request followed the general "
            "execution path."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(FlowState)

    builder.add_node(
        "normalize_question",
        normalize_question,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "classify_question",
        classify_question,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "technical_answer",
        technical_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "general_answer",
        general_answer,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "normalize_question",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "normalize_question",
        "classify_question",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify_question",
        route_question,
        {
            "technical": "technical_answer",
            "general": "general_answer",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "technical_answer",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "general_answer",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def create_initial_state():
    return {
        "question": (
            "How does LangGraph execution flow work?"
        ),
        "normalized_question": "",
        "category": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    print("=" * 60)
    print("STREAMED EXECUTION UPDATES")
    print("=" * 60)

    for update in graph.stream(
        create_initial_state(),
        stream_mode="updates",
    ):
        print("Update:", update)

    result = graph.invoke(
        create_initial_state()
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 60)
    print("FINAL EXECUTION RESULT")
    print("=" * 60)
    print("Question:", result.get("question"))
    print(
        "Normalized question:",
        result.get("normalized_question"),
    )
    print("Category:", result.get("category"))
    print("Status:", result.get("status"))
    print("Answer:", result.get("answer"))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


g. Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Execution Flow in LangGraph?

Execution Flow is the movement of state and control from START, through active nodes and edges, until execution reaches END or another stopping condition.

Q2. When does the next node receive a source node’s update?

LangGraph applies the source node’s state update before the dependent destination node executes.

Q3. What is a super-step?

A super-step is one runtime execution stage. Independent nodes may execute in the same super-step, while sequentially dependent nodes run in later super-steps.

Q4. What is the difference between invoke() and stream()?

invoke() returns the completed graph result. stream() exposes intermediate node updates or events while execution is occurring.

Q5. When does graph execution stop?

Execution normally stops when the selected path reaches END. At the runtime level, processing finishes when no nodes remain active and no messages remain in transit.


Chapter 5: Conditional Routing

Topic 1: Conditional Edges

Conditional Edges allow a LangGraph workflow to choose its next node at runtime. Unlike a sequential edge with one fixed destination, a conditional edge evaluates the updated graph state and selects one or more approved execution paths.

StateGraph.add_conditional_edges() accepts a source node, a routing function, and an optional path map. The routing function can return a single route, multiple routes, a destination node, or END to terminate execution.

a. Definition

A Conditional Edge is a state-dependent transition between nodes. After the source node completes and its update is merged, LangGraph calls a routing function to choose the next destination.

The routing function reads the latest graph state. It may inspect fields such as category, status, authorized, retry_count, evidence_available, or approval_result.

A path map connects logical route labels to registered nodes. For example, the label technical can map to technical_response, while billing maps to billing_response.

Conditional Edges support branching, retries, fallback, escalation, and termination. They are essential when requests should not all follow the same fixed path.

b. Purpose

Conditional Edges create adaptive workflows. One compiled graph can process technical, billing, unsupported, or restricted requests through different branches.

They separate decisions from processing. A classifier node determines the request category, a router maps that category to an approved destination, and branch nodes perform the actual work.

They prevent unnecessary execution. A technical request does not need to execute the billing workflow, and an invalid request can stop before expensive model or tool calls.

They support safe failure handling. Unknown categories, unavailable evidence, authorization failures, and exhausted retries can be routed to controlled fallback or termination nodes.

c. Architecture Diagram

                    CONDITIONAL EDGES
                    =================

                         User Request
                              |
                              v
                            START
                              |
                              v
                     classify_request
                              |
                    Writes category to state
                              |
                              v
                       route_request
              +---------------+---------------+
              |               |               |
              v               v               v
          technical         billing       unsupported
              |               |               |
              v               v               v
       technical_response billing_response reject_response
              |               |               |
              +---------------+---------------+
                              |
                              v
                             END

Architecture Explanation

1. The caller invokes the graph with the initial request. The initial state may contain the user’s question, an empty category, an empty answer, and a 
   pending status.

2. The classification node analyzes the request. It assigns a controlled category such as technical, billing, or unsupported and writes that value into 
   graph state.

3. LangGraph applies the state update before routing. The router therefore receives the new category rather than the earlier empty value.

4. The router inspects the updated state. It returns a stable route label representing the branch that should execute.

5. The path map resolves the route label to a graph node. Only the selected destination node executes. The other branches remain inactive.

6. The branch node creates the appropriate response. After updating the answer and status, it transitions to END.

d. Core Components

1. Source Node

The source node creates the decision data used by the conditional edge. It may classify intent, verify evidence, check authorization, evaluate a tool result, or calculate whether another retry is allowed. Its state update is applied before the router executes. Therefore, routing decisions can use the newest category, validation result, evidence status, or retry count.

2. Router Function

The router reads current state and returns the logical route that should be followed. It should be short, deterministic, and based on clearly defined state fields. The router should avoid expensive or side-effecting operations. Model invocation, retrieval, database updates, and tool execution should remain inside dedicated nodes.

3. Route Label

A route label is a stable value representing one workflow decision. Common examples include technical, billing, retry, review, fallback, and stop. Labels should be concise and documented. Free-form sentences and dynamically generated route names make mapping, testing, and graph visualization difficult.

4. Path Map

The path map converts router labels into registered graph destinations. It ensures that a logical decision such as technical activates the correct processing node. Every expected route must have a valid mapping. Missing mappings can cause runtime failures or incomplete execution paths.

5. Destination Node

The destination node performs the branch-specific operation. It may retrieve documents, calculate a result, call a tool, generate an answer, or return a controlled failure response. Only the selected destination executes for that decision. Other branch nodes remain inactive unless another graph path activates them later.

e. Code Example

from typing import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class RoutingState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    category: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def classify_request(state):
    question = state.get(
        "question",
        "",
    ).lower()

    technical_terms = (
        "langgraph",
        "python",
        "api",
    )

    billing_terms = (
        "invoice",
        "payment",
        "billing",
    )

    if any(
        term in question
        for term in technical_terms
    ):
        category = "technical"

    elif any(
        term in question
        for term in billing_terms
    ):
        category = "billing"

    else:
        category = "unsupported"

    return {
        "category": category,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_request(state):
    category = state.get(
        "category",
        "unsupported",
    )

    if category == "technical":
        return "technical"

    if category == "billing":
        return "billing"

    return "unsupported"


def technical_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "Your request was routed to the "
            "technical support workflow."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def billing_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "Your request was routed to the "
            "billing support workflow."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def unsupported_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request does not match a "
            "supported workflow category."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(RoutingState)

    builder.add_node(
        "classify_request",
        classify_request,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "technical_response",
        technical_response,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "billing_response",
        billing_response,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "unsupported_response",
        unsupported_response,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify_request",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify_request",
        route_request,
        {
            "technical": "technical_response",
            "billing": "billing_response",
            "unsupported": "unsupported_response",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "technical_response",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "billing_response",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "unsupported_response",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def run_question(graph, question):
    initial_state = {
        "question": question,
        "category": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(initial_state)

    print("-" * 60)
    print("Question:", result.get("question"))
    print("Category:", result.get("category"))
    print("Status:", result.get("status"))
    print("Answer:", result.get("answer"))


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH CONDITIONAL EDGES")
    print("=" * 60)

    run_question(
        graph,
        "How do conditional edges work in LangGraph?",
    )

    run_question(
        graph,
        "Where can I find my billing invoice?",
    )

    run_question(
        graph,
        "What food is available today?",
    )


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

f. Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is a Conditional Edge in LangGraph?

A Conditional Edge is a graph transition whose destination is selected at runtime by examining the current shared state. It is created through add_conditional_edges() using a source node, a routing function, and an optional path map.

Q2. When does the router function execute?

The router executes after the source node finishes and its returned state update has been merged. Therefore, the router can use the most recent category, status, retry count, or decision value.

Q3. What is the purpose of a path map?

A path map converts logical route labels into actual destination-node names. It separates business-level decisions such as technical from graph implementation names such as technical_response.

Q4. Can a conditional router terminate the graph?

Yes. A routing function can return END when no additional node should execute. Before doing so, the source node must already have produced the necessary output state.

Q5. Why should a router include a safe default?

A safe default ensures that missing, malformed, or unsupported decisions lead to rejection, fallback, review, or controlled termination rather than an unintended or sensitive execution path.

Topic 2: Dynamic Routing

Dynamic Routing allows a LangGraph workflow to choose its next operation while the graph is running. The path is not fixed for every request. Instead, the graph uses the current state, intermediate results, validation outcomes, or execution limits to determine which node should execute next.

LangGraph commonly implements dynamic routing through conditional edges. It can also use Command when a node needs to update state and select the next destination in one operation.

a) Definition

Dynamic Routing is the runtime selection of the next graph node based on the current application state. The workflow may choose different paths for different questions, users, tool results, errors, or evidence conditions, even though each request uses the same compiled graph.

The routing decision occurs after a node completes and its state update has been applied. For example, a classifier first writes a request category into state. A router then reads that category and selects the appropriate technical, billing, calculation, approval, or fallback node.

Dynamic Routing is commonly implemented with add_conditional_edges(). The routing function can return a logical route label, a destination-node name, multiple destinations, or END when the workflow should terminate without executing another node.

Dynamic Routing can also be implemented with Command. A node may return a Command containing an update for state changes and a goto destination, allowing the same node to update workflow data and control the next execution step.

b) Purpose

Dynamic Routing enables one graph to support several business workflows. A knowledge question can enter a RAG pipeline, a calculation request can use a deterministic calculator, a restricted operation can require human approval, and an unsupported request can terminate safely.

It avoids unnecessary work by executing only relevant nodes. A calculation request should not pass through document retrieval, reranking, and answer synthesis. Similarly, an invalid request can stop before consuming model tokens or calling external services.

Dynamic Routing provides explicit fallback and recovery paths. A tool timeout can route to a retry node, unavailable evidence can lead to abstention, a primary-model failure can select a fallback model, and exhausted limits can terminate the workflow safely.

It keeps flexible decision-making under application control. An LLM may classify the request, but deterministic routing code should map the validated classification to approved graph destinations. This prevents unrestricted model text from directly selecting sensitive operations.

c) Architecture Diagram

                       DYNAMIC ROUTING
                       ===============

                          User Request
                               |
                               v
                             START
                               |
                               v
                        validate_input
                               |
                               v
                       classify_request
                               |
                    Writes route into state
                               |
                               v
                         route_request
            +------------------+------------------+
            |                  |                  |
            v                  v                  v
        knowledge          calculation          action
            |                  |                  |
            v                  v                  v
     retrieve_documents  calculate_result  authorize_action
            |                  |                  |
            v                  v                  v
      generate_answer     format_result     execute_action
            |                  |                  |
            +------------------+------------------+
                               |
                               v
                              END

Architecture Explanation

1. The initial request enters the graph through START. A validation node confirms that the input contains the required values and follows the expected format.

2. A classification node determines the request type. It writes a controlled value such as knowledge, calculation, action, or unsupported into shared state.

3. The routing function examines the updated state. It maps the request type to one approved route label.

4. Only the selected branch becomes active. A knowledge request uses retrieval, while a calculation request uses deterministic computation.

5. Each branch performs its own specialized operations. Branch nodes remain independent and return their results through shared state.

6. All branches eventually reach an explicit terminal path. The workflow returns either a completed answer or a controlled fallback response.

d) Core Components

1. Decision State

Decision State contains the values used to select the next route. These may include request type, status, confidence, authorization, evidence quality, or retry count. Decision values should be controlled and predictable. Stable categories are easier to test than unrestricted free-form text generated by a model.

2. Dynamic Router

The Dynamic Router reads current state and returns the route that should execute next. The same router may produce different results for different graph invocations. The router should remain deterministic whenever practical. Expensive reasoning, retrieval, and side effects belong in separate nodes.

3. Path Map

The path map translates logical route labels into registered node names. This separates business decisions from implementation details. Every expected route must map to a valid destination. Unknown values should use a safe fallback rather than continuing unpredictably.

4. Branch Node

A Branch Node performs the specialized work associated with one route. Examples include retrieval, calculation, tool authorization, or fallback response generation. Branch nodes should remain independently testable. They should not assume that unrelated branches have executed.

5. Termination Policy

The Termination Policy defines when a route should stop rather than continue. Completion, retry exhaustion, budget limits, rejection, and missing evidence are common terminal conditions. Every dynamic path must eventually terminate or pause through a controlled interrupt. Otherwise, the workflow may cycle indefinitely.

e) Code Example

from typing import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class DynamicState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    request_type: str
    context: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def classify_request(state):
    question = state.get(
        "question",
        "",
    ).lower()

    knowledge_terms = (
        "langgraph",
        "policy",
        "documentation",
    )

    calculation_terms = (
        "calculate",
        "total",
        "multiply",
    )

    if any(
        term in question
        for term in knowledge_terms
    ):
        request_type = "knowledge"

    elif any(
        term in question
        for term in calculation_terms
    ):
        request_type = "calculation"

    else:
        request_type = "unsupported"

    return {
        "request_type": request_type,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_request(state):
    request_type = state.get(
        "request_type",
        "unsupported",
    )

    if request_type == "knowledge":
        return "knowledge"

    if request_type == "calculation":
        return "calculation"

    return "unsupported"


def retrieve_knowledge(state):
    return {
        "context": (
            "Dynamic Routing selects the next "
            "LangGraph node at runtime."
        ),
        "status": "context_ready",
    }


def generate_knowledge_answer(state):
    context = state.get(
        "context",
        "",
    )

    if not context:
        return {
            "answer": (
                "No knowledge context was available."
            ),
            "status": "completed",
        }

    return {
        "answer": context,
        "status": "completed",
    }


def calculate_result(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The calculated total is 100."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def unsupported_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request does not match a "
            "supported workflow."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(
        DynamicState
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "classify_request",
        classify_request,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "retrieve_knowledge",
        retrieve_knowledge,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "generate_knowledge_answer",
        generate_knowledge_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "calculate_result",
        calculate_result,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "unsupported_response",
        unsupported_response,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify_request",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify_request",
        route_request,
        {
            "knowledge": "retrieve_knowledge",
            "calculation": "calculate_result",
            "unsupported": "unsupported_response",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "retrieve_knowledge",
        "generate_knowledge_answer",
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "generate_knowledge_answer",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "calculate_result",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "unsupported_response",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def run_request(graph, question):
    initial_state = {
        "question": question,
        "request_type": "",
        "context": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("-" * 60)
    print(
        "Question:",
        result.get("question"),
    )
    print(
        "Request type:",
        result.get("request_type"),
    )
    print(
        "Context:",
        result.get("context") or "None",
    )
    print(
        "Status:",
        result.get("status"),
    )
    print(
        "Answer:",
        result.get("answer"),
    )


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH DYNAMIC ROUTING")
    print("=" * 60)

    run_request(
        graph,
        "What is Dynamic Routing in LangGraph?",
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Calculate the total for 5 items at 20 each.",
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Recommend a restaurant.",
    )


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Dynamic Routing in LangGraph?

Dynamic Routing is the runtime selection of the next graph node according to current state, intermediate results, or workflow conditions. It enables the same graph to follow different paths for different executions.

Q2. How is Dynamic Routing implemented?

It is commonly implemented with add_conditional_edges(), where a routing function reads state and returns a route label or destination. It can also use Command when a node must update state and choose the next node together.

Q3. What is the difference between static and dynamic routing?

Static routing always moves from a source node to the same destination. Dynamic routing chooses the destination at runtime according to state.

Q4. Should an LLM directly select sensitive graph nodes?

No. An LLM may produce a constrained classification, but deterministic application code should validate that classification and map it to approved destinations. Authorization must be enforced separately.

Q5. When should Command be used instead of conditional edges?

Use Command when one node naturally owns both the state update and the next-hop decision, such as agent handoffs or navigation across graph boundaries. Prefer conditional edges when separating the decision node, router, and path mapping produces a clearer workflow.


Topic 3: Router Functions

A Router Function examines the latest graph state and decides which node should execute next. It does not normally perform the branch’s business operation. Instead, it returns a stable route label that LangGraph resolves to a destination through add_conditional_edges().

LangGraph routing functions can be synchronous or asynchronous and may return a single route or multiple routes. A path map can translate the router’s logical output, such as knowledge, into an actual graph node such as retrieve_documents.

a) Definition

A Router Function is a callable that selects the next execution path by examining the current graph state. It runs after the source node completes and after that node’s state updates have been merged, so it always works with the latest available decision data.

A router normally returns a stable route label or destination-node name. For example, it may return knowledge, calculation, approval, or unsupported. LangGraph then uses the configured path map to resolve that value to a registered destination node.

Router Functions control execution but should not perform the destination operation. A router can decide that retrieval is required, but the actual vector search, database call, model invocation, or tool execution should remain inside a dedicated node.

A router can select one destination, multiple destinations, or graph termination. The add_conditional_edges() API accepts routing functions that return a hashable route value or a sequence of route values, and returning END can terminate the workflow.

b) Purpose

Router Functions separate decision-making from business processing. A classification node can produce the request type, the router can map that type to an approved destination, and branch-specific nodes can perform retrieval, calculation, generation, or controlled rejection.

They make dynamic workflows easier to understand and test. Because routing is placed inside a small function, developers can test every category, missing value, retry condition, authorization outcome, and fallback without executing the complete workflow.

Router Functions provide a deterministic control boundary around model-generated decisions. An LLM may classify a question as knowledge, but application code should validate that value and map it to an approved node rather than permitting unrestricted model output to choose arbitrary graph operations.

They make safe fallback behavior explicit. Unexpected categories, missing fields, exhausted retries, low-confidence decisions, and permanent failures can be routed to abstention, human review, fallback, or termination paths.


c) Architecture Diagram

                      ROUTER FUNCTION
                      ===============

                         User Request
                              |
                              v
                            START
                              |
                              v
                     classify_request
                              |
                 Writes request_type to state
                              |
                              v
                        route_request
              +---------------+---------------+
              |               |               |
              v               v               v
          knowledge       calculation      unsupported
              |               |               |
              v               v               v
       retrieve_context calculate_result fallback_response
              |               |               |
              v               |               |
       generate_answer        |               |
              |               |               |
              +---------------+---------------+
                              |
                              v
                             END

Architecture Explanation

1. The graph receives an initial request. At this point, no specialized processing path has been selected.

2. The classification node determines the request type. It stores a controlled value such as knowledge, calculation, or unsupported in shared state.

3. LangGraph applies the classification update. The Router Function receives the updated state rather than the original state.

4. The router checks the decision field. It returns one stable route label and includes a safe default for missing or unexpected values.

5. The path map resolves the returned label. Only the selected destination becomes active.

6. The selected branch performs its own work. Each branch eventually reaches END or another controlled decision point.

d) Core Components

1. Decision Field

The Decision Field contains the state value used by the router. Examples include request_type, status, authorized, verification_result, approval_status, and retry_count. Decision fields should use controlled values rather than unrestricted text. A small documented value set makes routing behavior easier to validate and prevents accidental destinations.

2. Router Function

The Router Function evaluates decision fields and returns one or more route values. It acts as the control layer between a source node and its possible destination nodes. The router should not mutate state or perform the destination’s work. Its primary responsibility is deciding where execution should continue.

3. Route Label

A Route Label represents the router’s logical decision. It provides a stable abstraction such as knowledge, retry, approved, or stop. Logical labels reduce coupling between decision logic and graph node names. The destination node can be renamed without changing the classifier’s business categories.

4. Path Map

The Path Map connects logical labels to registered node names. LangGraph uses it to resolve the router’s output into a graph destination. Every expected label should have a corresponding destination. An invalid or missing mapping can prevent the graph from continuing successfully.

5. Default Route

The Default Route handles missing, malformed, unsupported, or unexpected decisions. It should lead to a safe outcome. Appropriate defaults include rejection, abstention, fallback, human review, or controlled termination.

e) Code Example

from typing import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class RouterState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    request_type: str
    attempts: int
    context: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def classify_request(state):
    question = state.get(
        "question",
        "",
    ).lower()

    if any(
        term in question
        for term in (
            "langgraph",
            "documentation",
            "policy",
        )
    ):
        request_type = "knowledge"

    elif any(
        term in question
        for term in (
            "calculate",
            "total",
            "multiply",
        )
    ):
        request_type = "calculation"

    else:
        request_type = "unsupported"

    return {
        "request_type": request_type,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_request(state):
    request_type = state.get(
        "request_type",
        "unsupported",
    )

    if request_type == "knowledge":
        return "knowledge"

    if request_type == "calculation":
        return "calculation"

    return "unsupported"


def retrieve_context(state):
    attempts = state.get(
        "attempts",
        0,
    ) + 1

    if attempts == 1:
        return {
            "attempts": attempts,
            "context": "",
            "status": "retry_required",
        }

    return {
        "attempts": attempts,
        "context": (
            "Router Functions inspect graph state "
            "and return the next route."
        ),
        "status": "context_ready",
    }


def route_after_retrieval(state):
    status = state.get(
        "status",
        "",
    )

    attempts = state.get(
        "attempts",
        0,
    )

    if status == "context_ready":
        return "generate"

    if attempts >= 2:
        return "stop"

    return "retry"


def generate_answer(state):
    return {
        "answer": state.get(
            "context",
            "No context was available.",
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def calculate_result(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The calculated total is 100."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def unsupported_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request is not supported "
            "by this workflow."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def stop_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The workflow stopped because context "
            "was not found within the retry limit."
        ),
        "status": "stopped",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(
        RouterState
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "classify_request",
        classify_request,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "retrieve_context",
        retrieve_context,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "generate_answer",
        generate_answer,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "calculate_result",
        calculate_result,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "unsupported_response",
        unsupported_response,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "stop_response",
        stop_response,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify_request",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify_request",
        route_request,
        {
            "knowledge": "retrieve_context",
            "calculation": "calculate_result",
            "unsupported": "unsupported_response",
        },
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "retrieve_context",
        route_after_retrieval,
        {
            "retry": "retrieve_context",
            "generate": "generate_answer",
            "stop": "stop_response",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "generate_answer",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "calculate_result",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "unsupported_response",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "stop_response",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def create_initial_state(question):
    return {
        "question": question,
        "request_type": "",
        "attempts": 0,
        "context": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }


def run_request(graph, question):
    result = graph.invoke(
        create_initial_state(question)
    )

    print("-" * 60)
    print(
        "Question:",
        result.get("question"),
    )
    print(
        "Request type:",
        result.get("request_type"),
    )
    print(
        "Attempts:",
        result.get("attempts"),
    )
    print(
        "Status:",
        result.get("status"),
    )
    print(
        "Answer:",
        result.get("answer"),
    )


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH ROUTER FUNCTIONS")
    print("=" * 60)

    run_request(
        graph,
        "What is a Router Function in LangGraph?",
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Calculate the total for five items.",
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Recommend a movie.",
    )


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is a Router Function in LangGraph?

A Router Function is a callable that reads current graph state and returns the route or destination that should execute next. It is commonly used with add_conditional_edges().

Q2. When does a Router Function execute?

It executes after the source node completes and after the node’s state update has been applied. Therefore, it reads the latest graph state.

Q3. Should a Router Function update state?

A standard Router Function should normally only return a route. If state update and routing must happen together, a node can return a LangGraph Command containing both update and goto.

Q4. What is the difference between a route label and a node name?

A route label represents a logical business decision such as knowledge. A node name identifies the graph implementation, such as retrieve_context. A path map connects the two.

Q5. What makes a good Router Function?

A good router is short, deterministic, side-effect free, based on stable state values, fully mapped to valid destinations, and equipped with a safe default and bounded loop behavior.


Topic 4: Branching Workflows

A Branching Workflow allows one LangGraph application to split into several possible execution paths. The selected branch depends on the graph’s current state, such as request category, validation result, authorization status, retrieved evidence, or tool outcome.

LangGraph implements branching through conditional edges. The routing function evaluates the latest state and returns one or more route values, which are then resolved to destination nodes through a path map.

a) Definition

A Branching Workflow is a graph structure in which one processing step can lead to multiple possible execution paths. Instead of forcing every request through the same sequence, the graph selects the branch that matches the current request type or intermediate outcome.

Each branch can contain a different number and type of nodes. A knowledge branch might perform retrieval and generation, while a calculation branch might use one deterministic node. A restricted-action branch could require approval before any external operation is executed.

Only the selected branch normally executes for a particular request. Nodes in unrelated branches remain inactive, reducing unnecessary model calls, tool operations, retrieval requests, latency, and infrastructure cost.

Branches can terminate independently or converge into a common downstream node. For example, several specialized branches may eventually connect to one formatting, verification, auditing, or final-response node.


b) Purpose

Branching Workflows allow one compiled graph to support several business scenarios. A customer-support graph can handle technical questions, billing issues, account actions, restricted requests, and unsupported topics without creating a separate graph for each category.

They allow each request to use the most suitable processing strategy. Knowledge questions can use RAG, mathematical requests can use deterministic calculations, and high-impact actions can require human approval. This keeps each branch aligned with its reliability and security requirements.

Branching avoids processing every request through unnecessary components. A simple calculation does not need document retrieval or an LLM, while an unsupported request can terminate before expensive services are called.

Explicit branches improve observability and governance. Traces can show which branch was selected, which nodes ran, why the route was chosen, and whether the request completed, failed, or required escalation.

c) Architecture Diagram

                    BRANCHING WORKFLOW
                    ==================

                       User Request
                            |
                            v
                          START
                            |
                            v
                    classify_request
                            |
                    Updates branch field
                            |
                            v
                      route_request
          +-----------------+-----------------+
          |                 |                 |
          v                 v                 v
      Knowledge         Calculation        Approval
          |                 |                 |
          v                 v                 v
   retrieve_context   calculate_result  approval_response
          |
          v
   generate_answer
          |
          +-----------------+-----------------+
                            |
                            v
                           END

Unsupported decisions:
route_request -> unsupported_response -> END

Architecture Explanation

1. The caller submits an initial request. The state initially contains the question, empty branch information, an empty answer, and a pending status.

2. The classification node examines the request. It assigns a controlled branch label such as knowledge, calculation, approval, or unsupported.

3. LangGraph merges the classification result into state. The routing function receives the latest state containing the selected branch.

4. The router returns one logical route label. A path map resolves that label to the corresponding graph node.

5. The selected branch executes its specialized workflow. The knowledge branch performs two operations, while the calculation and approval branches contain one terminal operation each.

6. Every branch reaches a controlled ending. Successful, unsupported, and approval-required outcomes all terminate with an explicit final state.


d) Core Components

1. Branching Node

The branching node produces the state value required to choose a path. It may classify intent, evaluate evidence, determine risk, or interpret the result of a previous operation. It should return a controlled decision field rather than start branch processing itself. This keeps decision generation separate from route execution.

2. Router Function

The Router Function translates the current decision state into a route label. It determines which branch becomes active. The router should use a safe fallback when state is missing or unexpected. Unknown decisions should lead to controlled handling rather than privileged operations.

3. Path Map

The Path Map connects logical branch labels to registered destination nodes. It defines the approved routes available from the branching point. Every returned label must have a valid destination. Route labels and node names should remain stable and well documented.

4. Branch Nodes

Branch Nodes perform the specialized work required by one path. One branch may contain retrieval and generation, while another uses deterministic calculation. Each branch should be independently testable. It should not depend on unrelated branches having executed.

5. Terminal or Convergence Node

A branch may terminate directly or join a common downstream node. Direct termination is suitable when the branch already produces the complete output. A convergence node is appropriate when several branches need common formatting, verification, or auditing. The graph should clearly define what state fields each branch must provide before convergence.

e) Code Example

from typing import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class BranchState(TypedDict):
    question: str
    branch: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def classify_request(state):
    question = state.get("question", "").lower()

    if "langgraph" in question:
        branch = "knowledge"
    elif "calculate" in question:
        branch = "calculation"
    elif "deploy" in question:
        branch = "approval"
    else:
        branch = "unsupported"

    return {
        "branch": branch,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def route_request(state):
    branch = state.get("branch", "unsupported")

    if branch == "knowledge":
        return "knowledge"

    if branch == "calculation":
        return "calculation"

    if branch == "approval":
        return "approval"

    return "unsupported"


def knowledge_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "A branching workflow selects one "
            "execution path at runtime."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def calculation_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": "The calculated total is 100.",
        "status": "completed",
    }


def approval_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "This operation requires human approval."
        ),
        "status": "approval_required",
    }


def unsupported_response(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request is not supported "
            "by this workflow."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(BranchState)

    builder.add_node(
        "classify_request",
        classify_request,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "knowledge_response",
        knowledge_response,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "calculation_response",
        calculation_response,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "approval_response",
        approval_response,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "unsupported_response",
        unsupported_response,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify_request",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify_request",
        route_request,
        {
            "knowledge": "knowledge_response",
            "calculation": "calculation_response",
            "approval": "approval_response",
            "unsupported": "unsupported_response",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "knowledge_response",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "calculation_response",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "approval_response",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "unsupported_response",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def run_request(graph, question):
    initial_state = {
        "question": question,
        "branch": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(initial_state)

    print("-" * 60)
    print("Question:", result.get("question"))
    print("Branch:", result.get("branch"))
    print("Status:", result.get("status"))
    print("Answer:", result.get("answer"))


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH BRANCHING WORKFLOWS")
    print("=" * 60)

    run_request(
        graph,
        "What is a branching workflow in LangGraph?",
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Calculate the total.",
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Deploy the application.",
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Recommend a movie.",
    )


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

f) Interview Questions And Answers

Q1. What is a Branching Workflow in LangGraph?

A Branching Workflow is a graph in which runtime state determines which of several possible execution paths becomes active. It is commonly implemented with a decision node, Router Function, path map, and branch-specific nodes.

Q2. Does every branch execute for each request?

No. In ordinary conditional branching, only the branch selected by the Router Function executes. Multiple branches execute only when the router intentionally returns multiple destinations or the graph uses fan-out.

Q3. Can different branches contain different numbers of nodes?

Yes. One branch may contain a complete RAG workflow, while another may contain one deterministic calculation node. Every branch should still produce the fields required by its terminal or convergence point.

Q4. What is the difference between branching and parallel execution?

Branching typically selects one path from several alternatives. Parallel execution activates several independent paths during the same graph execution.

Q5. When should a branch become a subgraph?

A branch should become a subgraph when it contains several nodes, has its own state contract, requires independent testing, or makes the parent graph difficult to understand.


Topic 5: Decision-Based Execution

Decision-Based Execution means that a LangGraph workflow selects its next operation from a decision stored in the current graph state. The decision may represent approval, rejection, retry, fallback, escalation, tool selection, or successful completion.

A decision node first evaluates the available information and writes a controlled decision value into state. A Router Function then maps that decision to an approved destination through conditional edges. LangGraph also supports Command when a node must combine its state update and navigation decision in one return value.

a) Definition

Decision-Based Execution is a control-flow pattern in which a workflow’s next step depends on an explicit decision recorded in graph state. The decision is normally produced by a validation, classification, authorization, evaluation, verification, or risk-assessment node.

The decision node and routing logic serve different responsibilities. The decision node analyzes input and writes a value such as approve, review, or reject. The Router Function reads that value and selects the corresponding graph destination.

The decision can be produced using deterministic rules or model-based reasoning. Strict business rules, authorization, financial limits, and security policies should use deterministic code. LLMs may help with semantic classification, but their outputs should be constrained and validated before routing.

Decision-Based Execution should use a controlled set of possible outcomes. Stable values make routing predictable and prevent free-form output from choosing arbitrary or sensitive graph nodes.

b) Purpose

Decision-Based Execution allows the workflow to respond differently to changing runtime conditions. A low-risk request can continue automatically, a high-risk request can require human review, and an invalid request can be rejected before any external operation occurs.

It separates evaluation from action. One node applies the rules and records the decision, while separate branch nodes approve, reject, retry, escalate, or execute the appropriate workflow. This improves testing, observability, and governance.

It creates a deterministic safety boundary around model-driven applications. A model may recommend an action, but application code can verify permissions, limits, confidence, and business rules before mapping that recommendation to an executable graph path.

It supports controlled failure and escalation. Missing information, low confidence, conflicting evidence, exhausted retry limits, and high-risk operations can be sent to review or rejection rather than allowing the workflow to continue unpredictably.

c) Architecture Diagram

                 DECISION-BASED EXECUTION
                 ========================

                       User Request
                            |
                            v
                          START
                            |
                            v
                    evaluate_request
                            |
                Writes risk and decision
                            |
                            v
                     route_decision
             +--------------+--------------+
             |              |              |
             v              v              v
          approve         review         reject
             |              |              |
             v              v              v
      approve_request request_review reject_request
             |              |              |
             +--------------+--------------+
                            |
                            v
                           END

Architecture Explanation

1. The caller invokes the graph with initial request data. The state may contain a request description, amount, empty risk level, empty decision, and pending status.

2. The evaluation node applies deterministic rules. It evaluates request validity, amount, risk, operation type, or other relevant information.

3. The evaluation result is stored in shared state. The node records values such as risk_level and decision.

4. The Router Function reads the updated decision. It returns one stable route label such as approve, review, or reject.

5. The path map activates the matching destination node. Only the selected action branch executes.

6. The selected branch writes the final answer and status. The workflow then reaches END.

d) Core Components

1. Evaluation Node

The Evaluation Node analyzes state and produces the workflow decision. It can inspect validity, risk, evidence, permissions, confidence, cost, or an operation result. It should return controlled decision values. The output should be easy to validate and map to approved graph paths.

2. Decision Field

The Decision Field stores the evaluation result in shared state. Examples include approve, review, reject, retry, fallback, and complete. It acts as the contract between evaluation and routing. A documented value set prevents inconsistent or ambiguous control flow.

3. Router Function

The Router Function reads the Decision Field and selects the next path. It should include a safe default. Missing or unknown decisions should route to rejection, fallback, review, or another harmless outcome.

4. Decision Branch

A Decision Branch performs the action associated with one approved outcome. Approval, review, rejection, retry, or fallback should have separate focused nodes. Branches should not rely on unrelated branches having executed. Each path must receive the state required for its own operation.

5. Decision Audit

Decision Audit records why a route was selected and which rules or model version were used. Sensitive inputs should be protected. Logs should record controlled metadata rather than unrestricted private data or credentials.


e) Code Example

from typing import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class DecisionState(TypedDict):
    request: str
    amount: float
    risk_level: str
    decision: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def evaluate_request(state):
    request = state.get(
        "request",
        "",
    ).lower()

    amount = state.get(
        "amount",
        0.0,
    )

    if not request:
        return {
            "risk_level": "unknown",
            "decision": "reject",
            "status": "evaluated",
        }

    if amount <= 0:
        return {
            "risk_level": "invalid",
            "decision": "reject",
            "status": "evaluated",
        }

    if "deploy" in request or amount > 1000:
        return {
            "risk_level": "high",
            "decision": "review",
            "status": "evaluated",
        }

    return {
        "risk_level": "low",
        "decision": "approve",
        "status": "evaluated",
    }


def route_decision(state):
    decision = state.get(
        "decision",
        "reject",
    )

    if decision == "approve":
        return "approve"

    if decision == "review":
        return "review"

    return "reject"


def approve_request(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request passed the automatic "
            "decision rules."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def request_review(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request requires human review "
            "before execution."
        ),
        "status": "review_required",
    }


def reject_request(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request was rejected by "
            "the validation rules."
        ),
        "status": "rejected",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(DecisionState)

    builder.add_node(
        "evaluate_request",
        evaluate_request,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "approve_request",
        approve_request,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "request_review",
        request_review,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "reject_request",
        reject_request,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "evaluate_request",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "evaluate_request",
        route_decision,
        {
            "approve": "approve_request",
            "review": "request_review",
            "reject": "reject_request",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "approve_request",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "request_review",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "reject_request",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def run_request(graph, request, amount):
    initial_state = {
        "request": request,
        "amount": amount,
        "risk_level": "",
        "decision": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(initial_state)

    print("-" * 60)
    print(
        "Request:",
        result.get("request"),
    )
    print(
        "Amount:",
        result.get("amount"),
    )
    print(
        "Risk level:",
        result.get("risk_level"),
    )
    print(
        "Decision:",
        result.get("decision"),
    )
    print(
        "Status:",
        result.get("status"),
    )
    print(
        "Answer:",
        result.get("answer"),
    )


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH DECISION-BASED EXECUTION")
    print("=" * 60)

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Approve routine reimbursement",
        250.0,
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Deploy application to production",
        250.0,
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Approve request",
        0.0,
    )


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Decision-Based Execution in LangGraph?

Decision-Based Execution is a routing pattern in which a node evaluates current state, stores a controlled decision, and the graph uses that decision to choose the next execution branch.

Q2. What is the difference between a Decision Node and Router Function?

A Decision Node evaluates data and writes the decision into state. A Router Function reads that decision and maps it to the appropriate graph destination.

Q3. Should an LLM make authorization decisions?

No. Authentication, authorization, financial limits, security policies, and resource ownership must be enforced through deterministic application logic.

Q4. When should a request be routed to human review?

Human review is appropriate for high-risk, consequential, ambiguous, conflicting, or low-confidence operations that should not execute automatically.

Q5. How can state update and routing be combined?

A node can return LangGraph Command with an update for state changes and a goto destination. This is useful when one node naturally owns both actions.


Topic 6: Multi-Path Routing

Multi-Path Routing allows a LangGraph workflow to choose among three or more execution paths at runtime. Unlike simple binary routing, which usually selects between success and failure, multi-path routing supports several specialized workflows such as knowledge retrieval, calculation, technical support, human approval, and fallback handling.

LangGraph implements this pattern using add_conditional_edges(). A Router Function reads the latest shared state and returns a route value, while a path map connects that value to the appropriate destination node. The API can route to one destination or a sequence of destinations when several paths must be activated.

a) Definition

Multi-Path Routing is a conditional-routing pattern that selects one or more destinations from several available graph paths. The path is selected during execution rather than being fixed when the graph is designed, allowing the same compiled workflow to process several request types.
