The routing decision is based on the current graph state. A classifier, validator, risk evaluator, tool executor, or evidence checker writes a decision into state, and the Router Function uses that decision to select the next path.

Each route can represent a completely different processing workflow. A knowledge route might invoke retrieval and generation, a calculation route might use deterministic logic, and an approval route might pause or produce a human-review request.

Multi-Path Routing must include a controlled fallback path. If state contains a missing, malformed, or unsupported route, the workflow should abstain, reject, escalate, or finish safely rather than executing an arbitrary destination.

b) Purpose

Multi-Path Routing lets one graph support several business capabilities without duplicating the entire application. Common validation, authentication, tracing, and output handling can remain shared while specialized branches handle different request categories.

It ensures that each request uses the most appropriate processing method. Exact calculations can use deterministic functions, knowledge questions can use RAG, incidents can enter technical-support workflows, and consequential actions can require human approval.

It avoids executing irrelevant nodes. A calculation request does not need retrieval, an unsupported request does not need an LLM, and a high-risk action should not execute automatically. This reduces latency, model usage, tool calls, and operational cost.

It creates explicit governance boundaries. Sensitive branches can apply stronger authorization, approval, auditing, and validation requirements than low-risk informational branches.

c) Architecture Diagram

                      MULTI-PATH ROUTING
                      ==================

                         User Request
                              |
                              v
                            START
                              |
                              v
                     classify_request
                              |
                     Writes route to state
                              |
                              v
                        select_path
       +----------+-----------+-----------+----------+
       |          |           |           |          |
       v          v           v           v          v
   Knowledge  Calculation   Support    Approval   Fallback
       |          |           |           |          |
       v          v           v           v          v
   Knowledge  Calculator   Incident     Human      Safe
   Workflow      Node       Handler      Review    Response
       |          |           |           |          |
       +----------+-----------+-----------+----------+
                              |
                              v
                             END

d) Architecture Diagram

                      MULTI-PATH ROUTING
                      ==================

                         User Request
                              |
                              v
                            START
                              |
                              v
                     classify_request
                              |
                     Writes route to state
                              |
                              v
                        select_path
       +----------+-----------+-----------+----------+
       |          |           |           |          |
       v          v           v           v          v
   Knowledge  Calculation   Support    Approval   Fallback
       |          |           |           |          |
       v          v           v           v          v
   Knowledge  Calculator   Incident     Human      Safe
   Workflow      Node       Handler      Review    Response
       |          |           |           |          |
       +----------+-----------+-----------+----------+
                              |
                              v
                             END

Architecture Explanation

1. The request enters the graph through START. Its initial state includes the request text, an empty route, an empty answer, and a pending status.

2. The classification node evaluates the request. It assigns one controlled route such as knowledge, calculation, support, approval, or fallback.

3. LangGraph merges the route into state. The Router Function therefore reads the latest classification result.

4. The router validates the route against an approved set. An unknown value is converted into the safe fallback route.

5. The path map activates the corresponding destination. Only the selected branch executes for that request.

6. The chosen branch produces its final state. The workflow terminates at END or continues to additional branch-specific nodes.

e) Core Components

1. Classification Node

The Classification Node analyzes the request and creates a controlled route value. It may use keyword rules, structured LLM output, metadata, policy evaluation, or another deterministic classifier. It should not execute the selected workflow itself. Its responsibility is producing decision data for the routing layer.

2. Multi-Path Router

The Multi-Path Router reads the route field and selects among several approved paths. It provides the graph’s central dispatching logic. It should remain short, deterministic, and side-effect free. Complex operations belong in destination nodes.

3. Route Allowlist

The allowlist defines valid routing outcomes. It prevents missing or malformed decisions from reaching arbitrary graph nodes. Sensitive destinations should require further authorization. Being classified as an action request does not mean that action is permitted.

4. Path Map

The Path Map translates logical route labels into registered destination names. Every route must map to an existing node or termination path. A safe fallback should cover unknown values.

5. Branch Contract

Each branch should return state compatible with the graph’s expected output. For example, every terminal branch should produce answer and status. Consistent branch contracts simplify convergence and public output. Without them, downstream code must handle many incompatible state shapes.


f) Code Example

from typing import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import END, START, StateGraph


class MultiPathState(TypedDict):
    request: str
    route: str
    answer: str
    status: str


def classify_request(state):
    request = state.get(
        "request",
        "",
    ).lower()

    knowledge_terms = (
        "langgraph",
        "documentation",
        "policy",
    )

    calculation_terms = (
        "calculate",
        "total",
        "multiply",
    )

    support_terms = (
        "incident",
        "error",
        "failure",
    )

    approval_terms = (
        "deploy",
        "delete",
        "approve",
    )

    if any(
        term in request
        for term in knowledge_terms
    ):
        route = "knowledge"

    elif any(
        term in request
        for term in calculation_terms
    ):
        route = "calculation"

    elif any(
        term in request
        for term in support_terms
    ):
        route = "support"

    elif any(
        term in request
        for term in approval_terms
    ):
        route = "approval"

    else:
        route = "fallback"

    return {
        "route": route,
        "status": "classified",
    }


def select_path(state):
    route = state.get(
        "route",
        "fallback",
    )

    allowed_routes = {
        "knowledge",
        "calculation",
        "support",
        "approval",
    }

    if route in allowed_routes:
        return route

    return "fallback"


def knowledge_path(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request was handled by "
            "the knowledge workflow."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def calculation_path(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The calculated result is 100."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def support_path(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request was routed to "
            "technical support."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def approval_path(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "The request requires "
            "human approval."
        ),
        "status": "approval_required",
    }


def fallback_path(state):
    return {
        "answer": (
            "No supported route matched "
            "the request."
        ),
        "status": "completed",
    }


def build_graph():
    builder = StateGraph(
        MultiPathState
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "classify_request",
        classify_request,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "knowledge_path",
        knowledge_path,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "calculation_path",
        calculation_path,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "support_path",
        support_path,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "approval_path",
        approval_path,
    )

    builder.add_node(
        "fallback_path",
        fallback_path,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        START,
        "classify_request",
    )

    builder.add_conditional_edges(
        "classify_request",
        select_path,
        {
            "knowledge": "knowledge_path",
            "calculation": "calculation_path",
            "support": "support_path",
            "approval": "approval_path",
            "fallback": "fallback_path",
        },
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "knowledge_path",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "calculation_path",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "support_path",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "approval_path",
        END,
    )

    builder.add_edge(
        "fallback_path",
        END,
    )

    return builder.compile()


def run_request(graph, request):
    initial_state = {
        "request": request,
        "route": "",
        "answer": "",
        "status": "pending",
    }

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state
    )

    print("-" * 60)
    print(
        "Request:",
        result.get("request"),
    )
    print(
        "Route:",
        result.get("route"),
    )
    print(
        "Status:",
        result.get("status"),
    )
    print(
        "Answer:",
        result.get("answer"),
    )


def main():
    graph = build_graph()

    print("=" * 60)
    print("LANGGRAPH MULTI-PATH ROUTING")
    print("=" * 60)

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Explain LangGraph documentation.",
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Calculate the total for five items.",
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "The API returned an error.",
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Deploy the application.",
    )

    run_request(
        graph,
        "Recommend a movie.",
    )


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


g) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Multi-Path Routing?

Multi-Path Routing is a conditional-routing pattern that selects among three or more possible execution paths according to current graph state.

Q2. How is Multi-Path Routing implemented?

A decision node writes a route value into state, and a Router Function used with add_conditional_edges() maps that value to a destination node.

Q3. Can a router select multiple paths at once?

Yes. A conditional routing function may return a sequence of route values, allowing several destinations to become active. Shared fields updated by those branches require compatible reducers.

Q4. Why is a fallback route important?

It ensures that missing, malformed, or unsupported route values terminate safely rather than reaching unintended nodes.

Q5. When should a path become a subgraph?

Convert a path into a subgraph when it contains several nodes, owns a distinct state contract, requires independent testing, or makes the parent graph difficult to understand.


Chapter 6: State Management

a. Definition

The state lifecycle in LangGraph describes how state is created, read, updated, merged, optionally checkpointed, and returned while a graph executes.

In a StateGraph, every node receives the current state and returns a partial state update. LangGraph combines that update with the existing state before passing the new state to the next node. A node therefore should not directly manage the entire workflow state unless it genuinely needs to replace every field.


b. Purpose of the State Lifecycle

1. Maintain workflow context

The state carries information from one node to another throughout graph execution. It can contain user input, intermediate results, validation status, messages, tool outputs, retry counts, errors, and final responses.

Without state, each node would execute independently and would not know what previous nodes had produced.

2. Support controlled state updates

Nodes usually return only the fields they want to change. LangGraph merges those partial updates into the current state.

For Example: return {"status": "validated"}

The node updates only status. Other values already present in the state, such as request, attempts, and events, remain available.

3. Support accumulation with reducers

By default, a new value generally replaces the previous value for the same state key. Reducers can define alternative behavior, such as appending items to a list instead of replacing the complete list.

A state key can be annotated with a reducer whose conceptual signature is: (CurrentValue, NewValue) -> MergedValue

Reducers are particularly important when multiple nodes update the same field or when the graph must preserve a history of events or messages.

4. Enable resumable workflows

When a graph is compiled with a checkpointer, LangGraph can store state snapshots associated with a thread. This allows an application to continue a conversation, resume after an interruption, inspect earlier states, and implement fault-tolerant or human-in-the-loop workflows.


c. State Lifecycle Phases

Phase 1: State Schema Definition

Before the workflow runs, we define the structure of its state.

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    request: str
    normalized_request: str
    status: str
    attempts: int
    events: list[str]

The schema documents: Which fields can exist in the graph state, The expected type of each field, Which keys require reducers, What information nodes can read and update.

LangGraph supports dictionary-style state definitions such as TypedDict. A StateGraph is created using the state schema and must be compiled before methods such as invoke() and stream() can execute it.

Phase 2: State Initialization

The lifecycle begins when the application invokes the compiled graph with input.

initial_state = {
    "request": "  Generate Monthly Project Report  ",
    "normalized_request": "",
    "status": "received",
    "attempts": 0,
    "events": ["Request submitted"],
}

This input becomes the initial state available to the first node. Initial values should be provided for fields that nodes expect to access directly.

result = graph.invoke(initial_state)

If separate input and internal-state schemas are not defined, the graph normally uses its main state schema to represent the data moving through the workflow.

Phase 3: State Reading

Every node receives the current state as its argument.

	def normalize_request(state: WorkflowState):
    		original_request = state["request"]

The node should treat this state as the current snapshot of the workflow. It reads whichever values it requires to perform its responsibility.

A node does not need to read every field. Keeping each node focused on only the relevant fields improves maintainability and reduces accidental coupling between nodes.

Phase 4: Partial State Update

After processing, a node returns a dictionary containing the fields it wants to update.

def normalize_request(state: WorkflowState):
    cleaned_request = state["request"].strip().lower()

    return {
        "normalized_request": cleaned_request,
        "status": "normalized",
    }

The returned dictionary is a partial state update, not necessarily the complete state. LangGraph applies the returned values to the graph’s current state before executing the next node.

Conceptually:

Current state:
{
    request: "  Generate Monthly Project Report  ",
    normalized_request: "",
    status: "received",
    attempts: 0
}

Node update:
{
    normalized_request: "generate monthly project report",
    status: "normalized"
}

Updated state:
{
    request: "  Generate Monthly Project Report  ",
    normalized_request: "generate monthly project report",
    status: "normalized",
    attempts

A StateGraph node follows the general pattern State -> Partial<State>.


Phase 5: State Merging

When the node returns an update, LangGraph determines how each updated field should be merged.

Default replacement

Without a reducer, a new value replaces the previous value.

	return {"status": "validated"}

If the previous value was "normalized", it becomes "validated".

Reducer-based merging

A reducer controls how old and new values are combined.

from operator import add
from typing import Annotated

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    events: Annotated[list[str], add]

Now a node can return:

return {"events": ["Request normalized"]}

Instead of replacing the previous list, operator.add combines the lists.


Existing value: ["Request submitted"]
New update:     ["Request normalized"]

Merged value:
[
    "Request submitted",
    "Request normalized"
]

Each state key can have its own reducer, while fields without reducers use normal replacement behavior.


Phase 6: State Checkpointing

If the graph is compiled with a checkpointer, LangGraph saves state snapshots during execution.

from langgraph.checkpoint.memory import InMemorySaver

checkpointer = InMemorySaver()
graph = builder.compile(checkpointer=checkpointer)

The application supplies a thread_id to identify the execution thread:

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "project-workflow-001"
    }
}

Checkpoints are associated with that thread. Reusing the same thread ID allows LangGraph to load the thread’s existing state and continue working with it.

InMemorySaver stores checkpoints in application memory, so its data disappears when the process stops. For production durability, a database-backed checkpointer such as PostgreSQL should be used.


Phase 7: Final State

When execution reaches END, LangGraph returns the latest state.

result = graph.invoke(initial_state, config=config)
print(result)

The result contains all final state fields permitted by the graph’s output schema.

{
    "request": "  Generate Monthly Project Report  ",
    "normalized_request": "generate monthly project report",
    "status": "completed",
    "attempts": 1,
    "events": [
        "Request submitted",
        "Request normalized",
        "Request validated",
        "Workflow completed",
    ],
}


Reaching END completes that particular graph invocation. If a checkpointer is configured, the state can still remain stored for future invocations using the same thread.


d. StateLife Cycle Architecture 

                 ┌───────────────────────────────┐
                 │       Application Input                 │
                 │ request, status, attempts               │
                 └──────────────┬────────────────┘
                                     │
                                     ▼
                 ┌───────────────────────────────┐
                 │    Initialize Graph State               │
                 └──────────────┬────────────────┘
                                     │
                                     ▼
┌───────────┐    ┌───────────────────────────────┐
│ Previous      │── │        Node Reads Current State          │
│Checkpoint     │    │      performs its assigned task         │
└───────────┘    └──────────────┬────────────────┘
                                       │
                                       ▼
                 ┌───────────────────────────────┐
                 │ Node Returns Partial Update             │
                 └──────────────┬────────────────┘
                                     │
                                     ▼
                 ┌───────────────────────────────┐
                 │ Apply Replacement/Reducers   			   │
                 └──────────────┬────────────────┘
                                     │
                                     ▼
                 ┌───────────────────────────────┐
                 │ Save Updated Checkpoint                 │
                 └──────────────┬────────────────┘
                                     │
                      ┌─────────┴─────────┐
                      │                          │
                 More nodes?               Reached END?
                      │                         │
                      ▼                         ▼
               Next node reads          Return final state
               updated state



e) Code Example

from operator import add
from typing import Annotated
from typing_extensions import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import StateGraph, START, END
from langgraph.checkpoint.memory import InMemorySaver


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 1. Define the state schema
# ---------------------------------------------------------
class WorkflowState(TypedDict, total=False):
    request: str
    normalized_request: str
    status: str
    attempts: int

    # The add reducer appends new list values to the existing list.
    events: Annotated[list[str], add]


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 2. Define graph nodes
# ---------------------------------------------------------
def normalize_request(state: WorkflowState) -> dict:
    """
    Reads the original request and returns a partial state update.
    """
    request = state.get("request", "")
    normalized = " ".join(request.strip().lower().split())

    print("\n[NODE: normalize_request]")
    print(f"Current request: {request!r}")
    print(f"Normalized request: {normalized!r}")

    return {
        "normalized_request": normalized,
        "status": "normalized",
        "events": ["Request normalized"],
    }


def validate_request(state: WorkflowState) -> dict:
    """
    Validates the normalized request.
    It replaces status and attempts, while events are appended.
    """
    normalized = state.get("normalized_request", "")
    current_attempts = state.get("attempts", 0)
    new_attempts = current_attempts + 1

    print("\n[NODE: validate_request]")
    print(f"Request being validated: {normalized!r}")
    print(f"Validation attempt: {new_attempts}")

    if not normalized:
        return {
            "status": "validation_failed",
            "attempts": new_attempts,
            "events": ["Validation failed: request was empty"],
        }

    if len(normalized) < 5:
        return {
            "status": "validation_failed",
            "attempts": new_attempts,
            "events": ["Validation failed: request was too short"],
        }

    return {
        "status": "validated",
        "attempts": new_attempts,
        "events": ["Request validated"],
    }


def process_request(state: WorkflowState) -> dict:
    """
    Processes the validated request and completes the workflow.
    """
    current_status = state.get("status", "unknown")
    request = state.get("normalized_request", "")

    print("\n[NODE: process_request]")
    print(f"Current status: {current_status!r}")
    print(f"Request being processed: {request!r}")

    if current_status != "validated":
        return {
            "status": "stopped",
            "events": [
                "Processing stopped because validation was unsuccessful"
            ],
        }

    return {
        "status": "completed",
        "events": [
            f"Completed workflow for request: {request}"
        ],
    }


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 3. Build the StateGraph
# ---------------------------------------------------------
builder = StateGraph(WorkflowState)

builder.add_node("normalize_request", normalize_request)
builder.add_node("validate_request", validate_request)
builder.add_node("process_request", process_request)

builder.add_edge(START, "normalize_request")
builder.add_edge("normalize_request", "validate_request")
builder.add_edge("validate_request", "process_request")
builder.add_edge("process_request", END)


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 4. Configure checkpointing
# ---------------------------------------------------------
checkpointer = InMemorySaver()

graph = builder.compile(checkpointer=checkpointer)


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 5. Define a thread configuration
# ---------------------------------------------------------
config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "project-workflow-001"
    }
}


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 6. First invocation: initialize and run the state
# ---------------------------------------------------------
initial_state: WorkflowState = {
    "request": "  Generate   Monthly Project Report  ",
    "normalized_request": "",
    "status": "received",
    "attempts": 0,
    "events": ["First request submitted"],
}

print("=" * 70)
print("FIRST GRAPH INVOCATION")
print("=" * 70)

first_result = graph.invoke(
    initial_state,
    config=config,
)

print("\nFirst invocation final state:")

for key, value in first_result.items():
    print(f"{key}: {value}")


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 7. Inspect the latest checkpoint
# ---------------------------------------------------------
latest_snapshot = graph.get_state(config)

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("LATEST CHECKPOINT AFTER FIRST INVOCATION")
print("=" * 70)

print("State values:")
for key, value in latest_snapshot.values.items():
    print(f"{key}: {value}")

print(f"\nNext nodes: {latest_snapshot.next}")


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 8. Continue the same thread with another request
# ---------------------------------------------------------
# Because the same thread_id is used, LangGraph loads the
# existing checkpoint and merges this new input into it.
second_input: WorkflowState = {
    "request": "Prepare Risk Summary",
    "status": "received",
    "attempts": 0,
    "events": ["Second request submitted"],
}

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("SECOND GRAPH INVOCATION USING THE SAME THREAD")
print("=" * 70)

second_result = graph.invoke(
    second_input,
    config=config,
)

print("\nSecond invocation final state:")

for key, value in second_result.items():
    print(f"{key}: {value}")


# ---------------------------------------------------------
# 9. Display checkpoint history
# ---------------------------------------------------------
print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("CHECKPOINT HISTORY")
print("=" * 70)

history = list(graph.get_state_history(config))

for checkpoint_number, snapshot in enumerate(history, start=1):
    print(f"\nCheckpoint {checkpoint_number}")
    print(f"Next nodes: {snapshot.next}")
    print(f"Status: {snapshot.values.get('status')}")
    print(f"Attempts: {snapshot.values.get('attempts')}")
    print(f"Events: {snapshot.values.get('events')}")


print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("STATE LIFECYCLE DEMONSTRATION COMPLETED")
print("=" * 70)


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is the state lifecycle in LangGraph?

The state lifecycle in LangGraph is the sequence through which workflow state is initialized, read by nodes, updated through partial state changes, merged using default replacement behavior or reducers, optionally persisted as checkpoints, and finally returned when execution reaches END. Each node receives the current state and returns only the fields it wants to update. LangGraph applies those updates before executing the next node. This model makes data flow explicit and supports stateful, resumable, and debuggable agent workflows.


Q4. What is the difference between state and a checkpoint?

State is the current data snapshot used by graph nodes during execution. A checkpoint is a persisted snapshot of that state associated with an execution thread. State exists conceptually throughout graph execution, while checkpoints make selected state snapshots available for continuation, inspection, recovery, time travel, or human-in-the-loop execution.

Q5. What is the role of thread_id?

A thread_id identifies a logical execution thread when checkpointing is enabled. LangGraph uses it to find and store the correct checkpoints. Invocations using the same thread_id can continue from the same thread state. Invocations using different thread IDs maintain independent state histories.

Q6. Why should nodes return partial updates instead of mutating state?

Partial updates make every state transition explicit and allow LangGraph to apply reducer logic correctly. They also reduce accidental overwriting because each node changes only the fields for which it is responsible. This approach improves testability, observability, and maintainability, especially in workflows containing branching, parallel execution, retries, and multiple agents.


Topic 2: State Persistence

a. Definition

State persistence is the process of saving a LangGraph workflow’s state outside the immediate node execution so that it can be retrieved, inspected, continued, or recovered later.

LangGraph implements thread-level state persistence through checkpointers. A checkpointer saves graph-state snapshots as checkpoints, generally at each completed super-step, and organizes those checkpoints using a unique thread_id.


b. Purpose of State Persistence

1. Continue workflows across interactions

A workflow may require multiple user interactions instead of completing during one invocation. Persistence lets the application retain the thread’s previous state and use it when the same thread_id is invoked again.

For example, a project-assistant workflow can remember the project name, generated reports, approval status, and previous user requests.

2. Recover from failures

If a node fails after earlier nodes have completed successfully, checkpointing can allow execution to continue from a saved point instead of restarting the entire workflow.

LangGraph can also retain pending writes from successful nodes in a super-step where another node failed. When execution resumes, successfully completed work does not necessarily need to run again.


3. Support human-in-the-loop workflows

Some workflows must stop and wait for human approval.

Generate proposal
       ↓
Save checkpoint
       ↓
Wait for approval
       ↓
Resume graph
       ↓
Submit proposal

Because the graph state is saved before the interruption, the workflow can resume after a human reviews or modifies the request. Checkpointers are therefore a core requirement for durable interrupts and human-in-the-loop workflows.


4. Provide state history and debugging

Persistence creates a history of state snapshots. Developers can inspect these snapshots to understand: Which nodes executed, How values changed, Where an incorrect value entered the state, Which node should execute next, What state existed before an error.

Checkpoint history also enables time-travel debugging and forking execution from an earlier checkpoint.


c. Core Persistence Concepts

1. Checkpoint

A checkpoint is a snapshot of the graph state at a particular execution point. It can include: Current state values, Channel versions, Checkpoint metadata, Pending writes, Next nodes to execute, Configuration identifying the thread and checkpoint.

Every checkpoint has an identifier. A specific previous state can be addressed using both thread_id and checkpoint_id.


2. Checkpointer

A checkpointer is the component responsible for saving and loading checkpoints.  It is supplied when the graph is compiled: graph = builder.compile(checkpointer=checkpointer)

Once configured, LangGraph integrates checkpoint creation and retrieval into graph execution.

3. Thread

A thread is a sequence of related checkpoints identified by a thread_id.

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id

Invocations using the same thread ID operate within the same state history. Different thread IDs maintain independent state histories, which is essential for separating users, conversations, workflows, or business transactions.


4. State snapshot

When the application calls: snapshot = graph.get_state(config)

LangGraph returns a state snapshot containing information such as: snapshot.values, snapshot.next, snapshot.config, snapshot.metadata, snapshot.created_at
snapshot.tasks.


5. Checkpoint history

The historical checkpoints for a thread can be retrieved using: history = graph.get_state_history(config)
The returned snapshots allow developers to inspect changes to the graph state across the workflow’s execution.


d. State Persistence Architecture

┌──────────────────────────────┐
│ Application                  │
│ graph.invoke(input, config)  │
└──────────────┬───────────────┘
               │
               ▼
┌──────────────────────────────┐
│ thread_id identifies thread  │
└──────────────┬───────────────┘
               │
               ▼
┌──────────────────────────────┐
│ Checkpointer loads previous  │
│ checkpoint for the thread    │
└──────────────┬───────────────┘
               │
               ▼
┌──────────────────────────────┐
│ Node reads current state     │
│ and returns partial update   │
└──────────────┬───────────────┘
               │
               ▼
┌──────────────────────────────┐
│ LangGraph merges the update  │
└──────────────┬───────────────┘
               │
               ▼
┌──────────────────────────────┐
│ Checkpointer saves snapshot  │
│ Memory / SQLite / PostgreSQL │
└──────────────┬───────────────┘
                    │
        ┌──────┴──────┐
        │                 │
        ▼                 ▼
    Next node         Graph reaches END
        │                 │
        └──────┬──────┘
               ▼
       Final state returned

e. Code Example

import argparse
import sqlite3
from operator import add
from pathlib import Path
from typing import Annotated

from typing_extensions import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import StateGraph, START, END
from langgraph.checkpoint.sqlite import SqliteSaver


# =========================================================
# 1. Application configuration
# =========================================================

DATABASE_FILE = Path("langgraph_state.db")
THREAD_ID = "project-workflow-001"


# =========================================================
# 2. Define the graph state
# =========================================================

class ProjectState(TypedDict, total=False):
    project_name: str
    current_request: str
    normalized_request: str
    request_count: int
    status: str

    # New event lists will be appended to existing events.
    events: Annotated[list[str], add]


# =========================================================
# 3. Define graph nodes
# =========================================================

def normalize_request(state: ProjectState) -> dict:
    """
    Normalize the latest request.

    The node reads current_request from the persisted state
    and returns only the fields it wants to update.
    """

    current_request = state.get("current_request", "")
    normalized_request = " ".join(
        current_request.strip().lower().split()
    )

    print("\n[NODE: normalize_request]")
    print(f"Original request  : {current_request!r}")
    print(f"Normalized request: {normalized_request!r}")

    return {
        "normalized_request": normalized_request,
        "status": "normalized",
        "events": [
            f"Normalized request: {normalized_request}"
        ],
    }


def process_request(state: ProjectState) -> dict:
    """
    Process the normalized request and update the persisted
    request counter.
    """

    normalized_request = state.get("normalized_request", "")
    previous_count = state.get("request_count", 0)
    updated_count = previous_count + 1

    print("\n[NODE: process_request]")
    print(f"Project        : {state.get('project_name', 'Unknown')}")
    print(f"Previous count : {previous_count}")
    print(f"Updated count  : {updated_count}")

    if not normalized_request:
        return {
            "status": "failed",
            "events": ["Processing failed because the request was empty"],
        }

    return {
        "request_count": updated_count,
        "status": "completed",
        "events": [
            f"Completed request number {updated_count}"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 4. Build and compile the graph
# =========================================================

def create_graph(connection: sqlite3.Connection):
    """
    Create a new graph object connected to the same SQLite
    checkpoint database.
    """

    builder = StateGraph(ProjectState)

    builder.add_node("normalize_request", normalize_request)
    builder.add_node("process_request", process_request)

    builder.add_edge(START, "normalize_request")
    builder.add_edge("normalize_request", "process_request")
    builder.add_edge("process_request", END)

    checkpointer = SqliteSaver(connection)

    return builder.compile(checkpointer=checkpointer)


# =========================================================
# 5. Create the thread configuration
# =========================================================

def get_thread_config() -> dict:
    return {
        "configurable": {
            "thread_id": THREAD_ID
        }
    }


# =========================================================
# 6. Display a state snapshot
# =========================================================

def display_snapshot(snapshot, heading: str) -> None:
    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print(heading)
    print("=" * 70)

    if not snapshot.values:
        print("No persisted state was found for this thread.")
        return

    print("\nPersisted values:")

    for key, value in snapshot.values.items():
        print(f"{key}: {value}")

    print(f"\nNext nodes: {snapshot.next}")
    print(f"Created at: {snapshot.created_at}")
    print(f"Metadata: {snapshot.metadata}")


# =========================================================
# 7. Start a new persistent workflow
# =========================================================

def start_workflow(graph, config: dict) -> None:
    initial_state: ProjectState = {
        "project_name": "Enterprise RAG Platform",
        "current_request": "  Generate   Weekly Status Report  ",
        "normalized_request": "",
        "request_count": 0,
        "status": "received",
        "events": ["Persistent workflow started"],
    }

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("STARTING PERSISTENT WORKFLOW")
    print("=" * 70)

    result = graph.invoke(
        initial_state,
        config=config,
    )

    print("\nFinal state after start command:")

    for key, value in result.items():
        print(f"{key}: {value}")

    print(
        "\nState was saved in:",
        DATABASE_FILE.resolve(),
    )


# =========================================================
# 8. Inspect persisted state
# =========================================================

def inspect_workflow(graph, config: dict) -> None:
    snapshot = graph.get_state(config)

    display_snapshot(
        snapshot,
        "LATEST PERSISTED STATE",
    )


# =========================================================
# 9. Continue the same persisted thread
# =========================================================

def continue_workflow(graph, config: dict) -> None:
    """
    Reuse the same thread_id.

    LangGraph loads the previous checkpoint and merges this
    new input with the existing thread state.
    """

    additional_input: ProjectState = {
        "current_request": "Prepare Project Risk Summary",
        "status": "received",
        "events": ["A follow-up request was submitted"],
    }

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("CONTINUING PERSISTED WORKFLOW")
    print("=" * 70)

    result = graph.invoke(
        additional_input,
        config=config,
    )

    print("\nFinal state after continue command:")

    for key, value in result.items():
        print(f"{key}: {value}")


# =========================================================
# 10. Display checkpoint history
# =========================================================

def display_history(graph, config: dict) -> None:
    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("CHECKPOINT HISTORY")
    print("=" * 70)

    snapshots = list(graph.get_state_history(config))

    if not snapshots:
        print("No checkpoint history was found.")
        return

    for position, snapshot in enumerate(snapshots, start=1):
        values = snapshot.values

        print(f"\nCheckpoint position: {position}")
        print(f"Checkpoint config  : {snapshot.config}")
        print(f"Created at         : {snapshot.created_at}")
        print(f"Next nodes         : {snapshot.next}")
        print(f"Status             : {values.get('status')}")
        print(f"Request count      : {values.get('request_count')}")
        print(
            "Normalized request :",
            values.get("normalized_request"),
        )

        print("-" * 70)


# =========================================================
# 11. Parse command-line arguments
# =========================================================

def parse_arguments():
    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(
        description="LangGraph SQLite state persistence example"
    )

    parser.add_argument(
        "command",
        choices=[
            "start",
            "inspect",
            "continue",
            "history",
        ],
        help=(
            "start: create state, "
            "inspect: read saved state, "
            "continue: continue the same thread, "
            "history: display checkpoints"
        ),
    )

    return parser.parse_args()


# =========================================================
# 12. Main application
# =========================================================

def main() -> None:
    args = parse_arguments()

    # check_same_thread=False permits the SQLite connection
    # to be used by the LangGraph checkpoint implementation.
    connection = sqlite3.connect(
        DATABASE_FILE,
        check_same_thread=False,
    )

    try:
        graph = create_graph(connection)
        config = get_thread_config()

        if args.command == "start":
            start_workflow(graph, config)

        elif args.command == "inspect":
            inspect_workflow(graph, config)

        elif args.command == "continue":
            continue_workflow(graph, config)

        elif args.command == "history":
            display_history(graph, config)

    finally:
        connection.close()


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


f) Interview-Ready Questions

Q1. What is state persistence in LangGraph?

State persistence is the process of saving graph-state snapshots so that a workflow can retain its context across node executions and multiple graph invocations. LangGraph implements thread-level persistence through checkpointers. Each sequence of checkpoints is associated with a thread_id. By invoking the graph again with the same thread ID, an application can continue with the previously saved state.

Q2. What is the difference between InMemorySaver and SqliteSaver?

InMemorySaver stores checkpoints in application memory. It is fast and convenient for testing, but its state disappears when the process terminates. SqliteSaver stores checkpoints in an SQLite database. When configured with a database file, its state survives application restarts. It is useful for local development and smaller persistent applications, while PostgreSQL is generally more appropriate for scalable production systems.

Q3. What is the purpose of thread_id?

The thread_id identifies a logical sequence of graph executions and checkpoints. LangGraph uses it to determine which state history should be loaded and updated. Using the same thread ID continues the existing state. Using a different thread ID creates or accesses an independent state history.

Q4. What is saved in a checkpoint?

A checkpoint represents the graph state at a specific execution point. It can include state-channel values, version information, metadata, pending writes, checkpoint configuration, and information about which nodes should execute next. This information allows LangGraph to inspect, resume, replay, or recover a workflow.

Q5. What happens when the application invokes a persisted graph again?

When the graph is invoked with an existing thread_id, the checkpointer retrieves the thread’s latest checkpoint. LangGraph then combines the new input with the existing state according to the state schema and reducers. The graph executes from the appropriate state, produces additional updates, and saves new checkpoints to the same thread.

Q6. What is the difference between a checkpointer and a store?

A checkpointer saves graph-execution state within a particular thread. It supports workflow continuation, conversational memory, fault recovery, interrupts, and time-travel debugging. A store saves application-defined information independently of one execution thread. It is used for cross-thread or long-term information such as user preferences, reusable facts, or shared knowledge.

Topic 3: Shared State

a) Definition

Shared state is a common data structure through which nodes in a LangGraph workflow exchange information. Every node receives the current state, reads the fields it needs, and returns a dictionary containing only the fields it wants to update.

A LangGraph StateGraph follows the general node contract: Current State → Node Processing → Partial State Update

LangGraph applies the returned update to the shared state before downstream nodes execute. State fields can also use reducers to accumulate or combine updates instead of simply replacing their previous values.

def process_request(state: WorkflowState) -> dict:
    request = state["request"]

    return {
        "status": "processed"
    }

In this example, the node reads request from the shared state and updates only status. It does not need to return the entire state.


b) Purpose of Shared State

1. Communication between nodes

Shared state is the primary communication mechanism between LangGraph nodes. One node can save an intermediate result in the state, and downstream nodes can read that result without directly calling the earlier node.

For example, an input node can store a project request, three specialist nodes can generate separate analyses, and a finalizer node can read all analyses to produce one consolidated report.

Input Node
    │
    ▼
Shared State
    │
    ├── Cost Node
    ├── Schedule Node
    └── Risk Node
            │
            ▼
      Finalizer Node

2. Maintaining workflow context

Shared state preserves the information required throughout workflow execution. It may contain the original input, intermediate results, workflow status, validation errors, tool results, retry counters, audit events, and the final answer.

Every node does not need to use every state field. A well-designed node reads only the fields necessary for its responsibility and returns only the values it changes.

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    user_request: str
    normalized_request: str
    validation_status: str
    retry_count: int
    final_response: str

3. Coordinating parallel execution

Multiple nodes can execute in parallel and contribute their results to the shared state. If parallel nodes update different fields, their updates can be combined naturally.

# Cost node
return {"cost_analysis": "Budget is sufficient"}

# Schedule node
return {"schedule_analysis": "Timeline is achievable"}

# Risk node
return {"risk_analysis": "Integration risk is moderate"}

If parallel nodes update the same state key, the field needs a reducer that explains how the values should be combined. State fields can be annotated with reducer functions whose conceptual form is: (Current Value, New Value) → Merged Value

Reducers are especially important when accumulating lists, messages, dictionaries, numeric totals, or parallel results.

4. Supporting multi-agent systems

Shared state enables multiple agents to collaborate within the same workflow. Each agent can specialize in one responsibility while contributing results to a shared workflow context.

Supervisor Agent
        │
        ├── Research Agent
        ├── Analysis Agent
        └── Review Agent
                    │
                    ▼
              Shared State
                    │
                    ▼
              Final Response

Subgraphs can also participate in shared-state communication. If a parent graph and subgraph use shared state keys, the compiled subgraph can be added directly as a node. If their schemas differ, a wrapper node must transform the parent state into the subgraph’s expected input and map its output back into the parent state.

c) Shared State Architecture

┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│            Initial Shared State                            │
│                                                            │
│ project_name, budget, duration, events                     │
└─────────────────────┬───────────────────────┘
                             │
                             ▼
           ┌──────────────────────┐
           │     Planning Node           │
           │                             │
           │ Creates workflow plan       │
           │ Updates shared state        │
           └──────────┬───────────┘
                          │
         ┌────────────┼────────────┐
         │               │                 │
         ▼               ▼                 ▼
┌────────────────┐ ┌────────────────┐ ┌────────────────┐
│   Cost Node         │ │ Schedule Node        │ │   Risk Node         │
│                     │ │                      │ │                     │
│ Reads budget        │ │ Reads duration       │ │ Reads project       │
│ Writes cost         │ │ Writes schedule      │ │ Writes risk         │
│ analysis            │ │ analysis             │ │ analysis            │
└───────┬────────┘ └───────┬────────┘ └───────┬────────┘
           │                        │                        │
           └──────────────────┼──────────────────┘
                                    │
                                   ▼
              ┌────────────────────────┐
              │     State Reducers             │
              │                                │
              │ Merge parallel updates         │
              │ into the shared state          │
              └────────────┬───────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
              ┌────────────────────────┐
              │    Finalizer Node              │
              │                                │
              │ Reads cost, schedule,          │
              │ and risk analyses              │
              └────────────┬───────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
              ┌────────────────────────┐
              │   Final Shared State           │
              │                                │
              │ final_report                   │
              │ status = completed             │
              │ accumulated events             │
              └────────────┬───────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
                              END

e) Interview Questions And Answers

Q1. How do parallel nodes update shared state?

Parallel nodes can return updates during the same graph stage. If they update different state fields, those updates can be applied independently. If they update the same state field, the field should use a reducer that defines how multiple values are combined. For example, a dictionary reducer can merge cost, schedule, and risk results into one shared analysis dictionary.

Q2. What happens if parallel nodes update the same field without a reducer?

The updates can conflict because LangGraph has not been given a strategy for combining multiple values for that field. The correct solution is to configure a reducer that reflects the intended behavior, such as list concatenation, dictionary merging, numeric addition, set union, or a custom domain-specific operation.

Q3. What is the difference between shared state and persistence?

Shared state is the mechanism through which nodes communicate during graph execution. Persistence is the mechanism through which snapshots of that state are stored and recovered across interruptions or separate graph invocations. A graph can use shared state without a checkpointer. In that situation, nodes can still exchange data during one graph execution, but the workflow state will not be durably retained after the execution ends.

Q4. What is the purpose of a reducer?

A reducer defines how the current value of a state field should be combined with a new update. Without a reducer, a new value generally replaces the previous value. With a reducer, values can be accumulated or merged. Reducers are especially important when several parallel nodes update the same field.

Q5. How do parent graphs and subgraphs share state?

If the parent graph and subgraph share compatible state fields, the compiled subgraph can be added directly as a node in the parent graph. If they use different schemas, a wrapper node must transform the parent state into the subgraph input and convert the subgraph result back into a parent-state update. This isolates the subgraph’s internal structure while maintaining controlled communication.


Q6. What is shared state in LangGraph?

Shared state is the common data structure through which nodes in a LangGraph workflow communicate. Every node receives the current state, reads the fields it requires, and returns a partial state update. LangGraph applies the returned updates before downstream nodes execute. This enables nodes to exchange inputs, intermediate results, tool outputs, errors, status values, and final responses without directly calling one another.

Q7. Do LangGraph nodes directly modify shared state?

Nodes should not directly mutate the state object. They should treat the received state as the current workflow snapshot and return a dictionary containing only the fields they want to update.

For Example: return { "status": "validated" }

LangGraph applies the update according to the state schema and any reducer associated with the field.


Topic 4: Complex State Structures

a) Definition

A complex state structure is a LangGraph state schema containing nested objects, collections, optional values, constrained values, accumulated histories, and domain-specific models rather than only simple string or integer fields.

LangGraph state can be defined using a TypedDict, dataclass, or Pydantic model. The state schema determines the shape of the graph’s shared data, while reducers determine how updates to individual state fields are combined.

A basic state might contain only simple values:

class BasicState(TypedDict):
    request: str
    status: str

A complex state can contain multiple related structures:

class ComplexState(TypedDict):
    project: ProjectDetails
    tasks: list[Task]
    analyses: dict[str, AnalysisResult]
    errors: list[str]
    metrics: WorkflowMetrics
    status: Literal["pending", "processing", "completed", "failed"]

Complex state structures are useful when a workflow must preserve detailed information across multiple nodes without placing every value in one flat and difficult-to-maintain dictionary.


b) Purpose of Complex State Structures

1. Represent real workflow data

Production workflows rarely manage only one input and one output. They often need user information, task collections, documents, intermediate analyses, tool results, validation errors, execution metrics, and final responses.

Complex state structures group related values together. For example, project information can remain under project, workflow measurements under metrics, and specialist results under analyses.

{
    "project": {
        "name": "Enterprise RAG Platform",
        "budget": 750000.0,
        "duration_weeks": 16
    },
    "metrics": {
        "completed_tasks": 2,
        "failed_tasks": 0
    }
}

2. Improve type safety

A structured schema clearly identifies which values are expected and what type each value should have. This helps static type checkers, IDEs, and developers detect incorrect field usage before runtime.

A TypedDict provides static type information while preserving dictionary-style access. Pydantic models are more appropriate when runtime validation and richer field constraints are required. LangGraph’s Graph API supports TypedDict, dataclass, and Pydantic-based state definitions.

3. Separate workflow concerns

Complex state structures allow related data to be organized by responsibility.

project       → Project configuration
tasks         → Work items
analyses      → Specialist outputs
errors        → Validation or execution failures
metrics       → Workflow measurements
audit_events  → Execution history
final_report  → Final workflow output

This organization is easier to maintain than a large flat state containing many loosely related keys such as task_1, task_2, cost_result, risk_result, error_1, and error_2.

4. Support sophisticated reducers

Different state fields may require different update behaviors: A status field should be replaced, An event list should be appended, A dictionary should be merged, A set should use union behavior, Metrics may require custom aggregation, Message histories may require a message-specific reducer.

Reducers allow complex structures to evolve safely when multiple nodes contribute updates. LangGraph treats state as a schema plus reducer functions that control how node updates are applied.

c) Complex State Architecture


┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                 Initial Complex State                 │
│                                                       │
│ project, tasks, analyses, metrics, errors, events     │
└──────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┘
                           │
                           ▼
              ┌────────────────────────┐
              │   Initialization Node  │
              │                        │
              │ Validates project data │
              │ Creates task structure │
              └────────────┬───────────┘
                           │
              ┌────────────┼─────────────┐
              │            │             │
              ▼            ▼             ▼
     ┌────────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐
     │ Financial Node      │ │Schedule Node      │ │  Risk Node   │
     │                     │ │                   │ │              │
     │ Reads project       │ │ Reads tasks       │ │ Reads nested │
     │ Writes analysis     │ │Writes result      │ │ project data │
     └───────┬────────┘ └──────┬───────┘ └──────┬───────┘
             │                 │                │
             └─────────────────┼────────────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
                  ┌────────────────────────┐
                  │     State Reducers     │
                  │                        │
                  │ Merge analyses         │
                  │ Append events          │
                  │ Accumulate errors      │
                  └────────────┬───────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
                  ┌────────────────────────┐
                  │    Aggregation Node    │
                  │                        │
                  │ Reads nested structures│
                  │ Calculates metrics     │
                  │ Creates final report   │
                  └────────────┬───────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
                  ┌────────────────────────┐
                  │  Final Complex State   │
                  │                        │
                  │ status = completed     │
                  │ combined analyses      │
                  │ workflow metrics       │
                  │ final report           │
                  └────────────┬───────────┘
                               │
                               ▼
                              END


d) Code Example

from operator import add
from typing import Annotated, Literal

from typing_extensions import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import StateGraph, START, END


# =========================================================
# 1. Define nested state structures
# =========================================================

class ProjectDetails(TypedDict):
    project_id: str
    name: str
    budget: float
    duration_weeks: int
    team_size: int


class TaskDetails(TypedDict):
    task_id: str
    description: str
    priority: Literal["low", "medium", "high"]
    completed: bool


class AnalysisResult(TypedDict):
    category: str
    summary: str
    score: float
    recommendations: list[str]


class WorkflowMetrics(TypedDict):
    total_tasks: int
    completed_tasks: int
    analysis_count: int
    average_score: float


# =========================================================
# 2. Define custom reducers
# =========================================================

def merge_analyses(
    current: dict[str, AnalysisResult],
    new: dict[str, AnalysisResult],
) -> dict[str, AnalysisResult\]:
    """
    Merge analysis results returned by multiple nodes.

    Each specialist node should use a unique dictionary key,
    such as financial, schedule, or risk.
    """
    return {
        **current,
        **new,
    }


def merge_metrics(
    current: WorkflowMetrics,
    new: WorkflowMetrics,
) -> WorkflowMetrics:
    """
    Merge a metrics update with the current metrics.

    Only values supplied in the new update are replaced.
    """
    return {
        **current,
        **new,
    }


# =========================================================
# 3. Define the complete graph state
# =========================================================

class ComplexWorkflowState(TypedDict, total=False):
    project: ProjectDetails
    tasks: list[TaskDetails]

    analyses: Annotated[
        dict[str, AnalysisResult],
        merge_analyses,
    ]

    metrics: Annotated[
        WorkflowMetrics,
        merge_metrics,
    ]

    events: Annotated[
        list[str],
        add,
    ]

    errors: Annotated[
        list[str],
        add,
    ]

    status: Literal[
        "pending",
        "processing",
        "completed",
        "failed",
    ]

    final_report: str


# =========================================================
# 4. Define the initialization node
# =========================================================

def initialize_workflow(
    state: ComplexWorkflowState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Read the nested project and task structures, then initialize
    workflow status and metrics.
    """

    project = state["project"]
    tasks = state.get("tasks", [])

    total_tasks = len(tasks)

    completed_tasks = sum(
        1
        for task in tasks
        if task["completed"]
    )

    print("\n[NODE: initialize_workflow]")
    print(f"Project: {project['name']}")
    print(f"Total tasks: {total_tasks}")
    print(f"Completed tasks: {completed_tasks}")

    errors: list[str] = []

    if project["budget"] <= 0:
        errors.append(
            "Project budget must be greater than zero."
        )

    if project["duration_weeks"] <= 0:
        errors.append(
            "Project duration must be greater than zero."
        )

    if project["team_size"] <= 0:
        errors.append(
            "Project team size must be greater than zero."
        )

    if errors:
        return {
            "status": "failed",
            "errors": errors,
            "events": [
                "Workflow initialization failed"
            ],
        }

    initial_metrics: WorkflowMetrics = {
        "total_tasks": total_tasks,
        "completed_tasks": completed_tasks,
        "analysis_count": 0,
        "average_score": 0.0,
    }

    return {
        "status": "processing",
        "metrics": initial_metrics,
        "events": [
            "Complex workflow initialized"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 5. Define parallel analysis nodes
# =========================================================

def analyze_financials(
    state: ComplexWorkflowState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Read the nested project structure and generate a financial
    analysis result.
    """

    project = state["project"]
    budget = project["budget"]
    team_size = project["team_size"]

    budget_per_member = budget / team_size

    print("\n[NODE: analyze_financials]")
    print(f"Budget: {budget}")
    print(f"Team size: {team_size}")
    print(f"Budget per member: {budget_per_member:.2f}")

    if budget_per_member >= 100_000:
        score = 0.90
        summary = (
            "The project has strong financial capacity relative "
            "to its current team size."
        )
        recommendations = [
            "Track cloud and licensing costs monthly.",
            "Reserve a contingency budget for integration work.",
        ]
    elif budget_per_member >= 50_000:
        score = 0.75
        summary = (
            "The project budget is workable but requires active "
            "cost monitoring."
        )
        recommendations = [
            "Prioritize essential platform capabilities.",
            "Review external service costs at every milestone.",
        ]
    else:
        score = 0.55
        summary = (
            "The available budget may be constrained for the "
            "current team and delivery scope."
        )
        recommendations = [
            "Reduce nonessential scope.",
            "Consider phased implementation.",
        ]

    result: AnalysisResult = {
        "category": "financial",
        "summary": summary,
        "score": score,
        "recommendations": recommendations,
    }

    return {
        "analyses": {
            "financial": result
        },
        "events": [
            "Financial analysis completed"
        ],
    }


def analyze_schedule(
    state: ComplexWorkflowState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Analyze nested project duration and structured task data.
    """

    project = state["project"]
    tasks = state.get("tasks", [])

    duration = project["duration_weeks"]

    high_priority_tasks = [
        task
        for task in tasks
        if task["priority"] == "high"
        and not task["completed"]
    ]

    print("\n[NODE: analyze_schedule]")
    print(f"Duration: {duration} weeks")
    print(
        "Open high-priority tasks:",
        len(high_priority_tasks),
    )

    if duration >= 20:
        score = 0.88
        summary = (
            "The delivery schedule provides sufficient time for "
            "implementation, testing, and release preparation."
        )
    elif duration >= 12:
        score = 0.72
        summary = (
            "The schedule is achievable, but high-priority tasks "
            "must be monitored closely."
        )
    else:
        score = 0.50
        summary = (
            "The schedule is aggressive and may require reduced "
            "scope or additional delivery capacity."
        )

    recommendations = [
        "Review high-priority tasks every week.",
        "Start integration testing early.",
    ]

    if high_priority_tasks:
        recommendations.append(
            "Assign owners to all open high-priority tasks."
        )

    result: AnalysisResult = {
        "category": "schedule",
        "summary": summary,
        "score": score,
        "recommendations": recommendations,
    }

    return {
        "analyses": {
            "schedule": result
        },
        "events": [
            "Schedule analysis completed"
        ],
    }


def analyze_risks(
    state: ComplexWorkflowState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Read multiple nested structures and calculate overall risk.
    """

    project = state["project"]
    tasks = state.get("tasks", [])

    risk_factors: list[str] = []

    if project["duration_weeks"] < 12:
        risk_factors.append(
            "aggressive schedule"
        )

    if project["budget"] < 500_000:
        risk_factors.append(
            "limited budget"
        )

    incomplete_high_priority_tasks = [
        task
        for task in tasks
        if task["priority"] == "high"
        and not task["completed"]
    ]

    if len(incomplete_high_priority_tasks) >= 2:
        risk_factors.append(
            "multiple open high-priority tasks"
        )

    print("\n[NODE: analyze_risks]")
    print(f"Risk factors: {risk_factors}")

    if not risk_factors:
        score = 0.90
        summary = (
            "No critical budget, schedule, or task-related risk "
            "was detected."
        )
    elif len(risk_factors) == 1:
        score = 0.70
        summary = (
            "The project has one significant risk factor that "
            "requires active monitoring."
        )
    else:
        score = 0.45
        summary = (
            "The project has multiple risk factors requiring "
            "formal mitigation actions."
        )

    recommendations = [
        "Maintain a project risk register.",
        "Assign an owner and review date to every major risk.",
    ]

    if risk_factors:
        recommendations.append(
            "Create mitigation plans for: "
            + ", ".join(risk_factors)
            + "."
        )

    result: AnalysisResult = {
        "category": "risk",
        "summary": summary,
        "score": score,
        "recommendations": recommendations,
    }

    return {
        "analyses": {
            "risk": result
        },
        "events": [
            "Risk analysis completed"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 6. Define the final aggregation node
# =========================================================

def aggregate_results(
    state: ComplexWorkflowState,
) -> dict:
    """
    Read the merged complex state, calculate final metrics,
    and create a consolidated report.
    """

    project = state["project"]
    tasks = state.get("tasks", [])
    analyses = state.get("analyses", {})

    completed_tasks = sum(
        1
        for task in tasks
        if task["completed"]
    )

    scores = [
        result["score"]
        for result in analyses.values()
    ]

    average_score = (
        sum(scores) / len(scores)
        if scores
        else 0.0
    )

    updated_metrics: WorkflowMetrics = {
        "total_tasks": len(tasks),
        "completed_tasks": completed_tasks,
        "analysis_count": len(analyses),
        "average_score": round(
            average_score,
            2,
        ),
    }

    report_sections: list[str] = []

    for analysis_name, result in analyses.items():
        recommendations = "\n".join(
            f"  - {item}"
            for item in result["recommendations"]
        )

        section = (
            f"{analysis_name.upper()} ANALYSIS\n"
            f"Score: {result['score'\]:.2f}\n"
            f"Summary: {result['summary']}\n"
            f"Recommendations:\n"
            f"{recommendations}"
        )

        report_sections.append(section)

    combined_sections = "\n\n".join(
        report_sections
    )

    final_report = f"""
PROJECT ASSESSMENT
==================

Project ID: {project['project_id']}
Project Name: {project['name']}
Budget: {project['budget'\]:.2f}
Duration: {project['duration_weeks']} weeks
Team Size: {project['team_size']}

TASK INFORMATION
================
Total Tasks: {updated_metrics['total_tasks']}
Completed Tasks: {updated_metrics['completed_tasks']}

ANALYSIS RESULTS
================
{combined_sections}

FINAL METRICS
=============
Analysis Count: {updated_metrics['analysis_count']}
Average Score: {updated_metrics['average_score'\]:.2f}

FINAL STATUS
============
Completed
""".strip()

    print("\n[NODE: aggregate_results]")
    print(
        "Analysis fields:",
        sorted(analyses.keys()),
    )
    print(
        "Average score:",
        updated_metrics["average_score"],
    )

    return {
        "metrics": updated_metrics,
        "final_report": final_report,
        "status": "completed",
        "events": [
            "Final project assessment created"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 7. Define routing after initialization
# =========================================================

def route_after_initialization(
    state: ComplexWorkflowState,
) -> Literal["continue", "stop"\]:
    """
    Stop the workflow if initialization failed.
    Otherwise, continue to the parallel analysis nodes.
    """

    if state.get("status") == "failed":
        return "stop"

    return "continue"


# =========================================================
# 8. Build the graph
# =========================================================

builder = StateGraph(ComplexWorkflowState)

builder.add_node(
    "initialize_workflow",
    initialize_workflow,
)

builder.add_node(
    "analyze_financials",
    analyze_financials,
)

builder.add_node(
    "analyze_schedule",
    analyze_schedule,
)

builder.add_node(
    "analyze_risks",
    analyze_risks,
)

builder.add_node(
    "aggregate_results",
    aggregate_results,
)


# =========================================================
# 9. Add edges
# =========================================================

builder.add_edge(
    START,
    "initialize_workflow",
)

builder.add_conditional_edges(
    "initialize_workflow",
    route_after_initialization,
    {
        "continue": "analyze_financials",
        "stop": END,
    },
)

# Fan out from the same initialization node.
# The financial node is already targeted by conditional routing.
builder.add_edge(
    "alize_workflow",
    "analyze_schedule",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "initialize_workflow",
    "analyze_risks",
)

# Join all three parallel analysis branches.
builder.add_edge(
    [
        "analyze_financials",
        "analyze_schedule",
        "analyze_risks",
    ],
    "aggregate_results",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "aggregate_results",
    END,
)


# =========================================================
# 10. Compile the graph
# =========================================================

graph = builder.compile()


# =========================================================
# 11. Define complex initial state
# =========================================================

initial_state: ComplexWorkflowState = {
    "project": {
        "project_id": "PRJ-2026-001",
        "name": "Enterprise RAG Platform",
        "budget": 750_000.0,
        "duration_weeks": 16,
        "team_size": 8,
    },
    "tasks": [
        {
            "task_id": "TASK-001",
            "description": "Finalize data-source inventory",
            "priority": "high",
            "completed": True,
        },
        {
            "task_id": "TASK-002",
            "description": "Design retrieval architecture",
            "priority": "high",
            "completed": False,
        },
        {
            "task_id": "TASK-003",
            "description": "Implement evaluation pipeline",
            "priority": "medium",
            "completed": False,
        },
        {
            "task_id": "TASK-004",
            "description": "Prepare production monitoring",
            "priority": "medium",
            "completed": False,
        },
    ],
    "analyses": {},
    "metrics": {
        "total_tasks": 0,
        "completed_tasks": 0,
        "analysis_count": 0,
        "average_score": 0.0,
    },
    "events": [
        "Project assessment requested"
    ],
    "errors": [],
    "status": "pending",
    "final_report": "",
}


# =========================================================
# 12. Execute the graph
# =========================================================

def main() -> None:
    print("=" * 70)
    print("LANGGRAPH COMPLEX STATE STRUCTURES")
    print("=" * 70)

    final_state = graph.invoke(initial_state)

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("FINAL REPORT")
    print("=" * 70)

    print(
        final_state.get(
            "final_report",
            "No report was generated.",
        )
    )

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("FINAL METRICS")
    print("=" * 70)

    for key, value in final_state.get(
        "metrics",
        {},
    ).items():
        print(f"{key}: {value}")

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("EVENT HISTORY")
    print("=" * 70)

    for number, event in enumerate(
