        final_state.get("events", []),
        start=1,
    ):
        print(f"{number}. {event}")

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("ERRORS")
    print("=" * 70)

    errors = final_state.get("errors", [])

    if errors:
        for error in errors:
            print(f"- {error}")
    else:
        print("No workflow errors were recorded.")

    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print("FINAL STATUS")
    print("=" * 70)

    print(final_state.get("status"))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


e) Inteview Questions And Answers

Q1. Why are reducers important for complex state?

Reducers define how new updates are combined with existing values. They are especially important when parallel nodes update the same complex field. For example, a dictionary reducer can merge structured analysis results, while a list reducer can append workflow events or errors.

Q2. What is the difference between TypedDict and Pydantic state?

TypedDict provides static type information but does not perform runtime validation. It is lightweight and works well for internal graph state. Pydantic validates data at runtime and can enforce constraints such as minimum values, valid formats, and custom validation rules. It is suitable when state enters the graph from untrusted or external sources.

Q3. Should every workflow use a complex state?

No. The state should be only as complex as the workflow requires. A small graph should use a simple schema. Complex nested state is justified when it improves organization, establishes clear domain models, supports multiple specialists, or represents structured intermediate results.

Q4. What are complex state structures in LangGraph?

Complex state structures are schemas containing nested objects, lists of structured records, dictionaries of named results, constrained status values, accumulated histories, metrics, and optional fields. They are used when a workflow must manage realistic domain data across multiple nodes rather than a few basic string or numeric values.

Q5. How can complex state be defined?

LangGraph state can be defined using a TypedDict, dataclass, or Pydantic model. TypedDict is commonly used because it provides lightweight dictionary-style access and static type hints. Dataclasses provide object-oriented access, while Pydantic models provide runtime validation and richer field constraints.


Q6. How should a node update a nested object?

A node should avoid directly mutating the nested object. Instead, it should create a new object by copying the current values and replacing the required fields.

updated_project = {
    **state["project"],
    "budget": 900000,
}

return {
    "project": updated_project
}

This makes the update explicit and reduces unexpected mutation problems.


Topic 5: State Validation

a) Definition

State validation is the process of checking whether the data stored in a LangGraph state follows the expected structure, data types, permitted values, and business rules before downstream nodes use it.

A LangGraph state schema can be defined using TypedDict, a dataclass, or a Pydantic model. Nodes read the current state and return partial updates, while validation ensures that those updates contain reliable and usable data.

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    project_name: str
    budget: float
    duration_weeks: int
    validation_status: str

The schema describes the expected shape of the state. However, when runtime validation is required, the workflow should use explicit validation logic or a validation library such as Pydantic.

b) Purpose

1. Prevent invalid state from reaching processing nodes

Invalid values can cause downstream calculations, tool calls, or decisions to fail. For example, calculating a weekly project cost is unsafe when duration_weeks is zero.

A validation node detects such problems before the processing node executes. This creates a controlled failure path instead of allowing an unexpected runtime exception.

2. Maintain data consistency

Individual values may be valid while their combination is logically inconsistent. For example, a two-week, high-priority project may be unrealistic when only one team member is available.

Cross-field validation examines relationships between multiple state values. It ensures that the entire state is meaningful, not merely that each field has the correct Python type.

3. Control graph routing

Validation results can determine which path the graph follows. A valid state can continue to business processing, while an invalid state can move to an error-handling or human-review node.

Valid State   → Processing Node
Invalid State → Error Handler

This makes validation an active part of workflow control rather than only a passive data check.

4. Produce understandable errors

Validation should store structured information explaining which field failed, why it failed, and what type of error occurred.

{
    "field": "budget",
    "message": "Budget must be greater than zero.",
    "error_type": "greater_than"
}

Structured errors are easier to display, log, test, persist, or send to another node for correction.

c) Core Components

1. State schema

The state schema defines all values required by validation, processing, routing, and error handling.

class WorkflowState(TypedDict, total=False):
    project_name: str
    budget: float
    validation_status: str
    validation_errors: list[ValidationIssue]

TypedDict provides lightweight dictionary-style state and static type information. LangGraph supports TypedDict, dataclass, and Pydantic-based state schemas.

2. Validation model

The Pydantic model applies runtime validation.

class ProjectInput(BaseModel):
    project_name: str = Field(min_length=3)
    budget: float = Field(gt=0)
    duration_weeks: int = Field(ge=1)

It can validate individual fields and relationships between multiple fields.

3. Validation node

The validation node converts raw state values into validated data. 

def validate_state(state: WorkflowState) -> dict:
    validated = ProjectInput.model_validate(...)
    return {"validation_status": "valid"}

If validation fails, the node catches ValidationError and stores structured error details in the state.


4. Conditional router

The routing function selects the next node according to the validation result.

def route_after_validation(state: WorkflowState) -> str:
    return (
        "process"
        if state["validation_status"] == "valid"
        else "reject"
    )

The router does not perform business processing. Its only responsibility is selecting the correct graph path.

d) Code Example

from operator import add
from typing import Annotated, Literal

from langgraph.graph import StateGraph, START, END
from pydantic import (
    BaseModel,
    ConfigDict,
    Field,
    ValidationError,
    field_validator,
    model_validator,
)
from typing_extensions import TypedDict


# =========================================================
# 1. Define structured validation errors
# =========================================================

class ValidationIssue(TypedDict):
    field: str
    message: str
    error_type: str


# =========================================================
# 2. Define the LangGraph shared state
# =========================================================

class WorkflowState(TypedDict, total=False):
    project_name: str
    project_owner: str
    budget: float
    duration_weeks: int
    team_size: int
    priority: str

    validation_status: Literal[
        "not_validated",
        "valid",
        "invalid",
    ]

    validation_errors: list[ValidationIssue]

    cost_per_week: float
    assessment: str

    status: Literal[
        "received",
        "validating",
        "completed",
        "rejected",
    ]

    events: Annotated[list[str], add]


# =========================================================
# 3. Define the Pydantic validation model
# =========================================================

class ProjectInput(BaseModel):
    model_config = ConfigDict(
        strict=True,
        extra="forbid",
    )

    project_name: str = Field(
        min_length=3,
        max_length=100,
    )

    project_owner: str = Field(
        min_length=2,
        max_length=100,
    )

    budget: float = Field(
        gt=0,
        le=100_000_000,
    )

    duration_weeks: int = Field(
        ge=1,
        le=104,
    )

    team_size: int = Field(
        ge=1,
        le=500,
    )

    priority: Literal[
        "low",
        "medium",
        "high",
    ]

    @field_validator(
        "project_name",
        "project_owner",
    )
    @classmethod
    def validate_text(cls, value: str) -> str:
        cleaned_value = value.strip()

        if not cleaned_value:
            raise ValueError(
                "The value cannot be blank."
            )

        return cleaned_value

    @model_validator(mode="after")
    def validate_delivery_capacity(
        self,
    ) -> "ProjectInput":
        if (
            self.priority == "high"
            and self.duration_weeks < 4
            and self.team_size < 3
        ):
            raise ValueError(
                "A high-priority project shorter than four "
                "weeks requires at least three team members."
            )

        return self


# =========================================================
# 4. Convert Pydantic errors into graph state
# =========================================================

def format_validation_errors(
    error: ValidationError,
) -> list[ValidationIssue\]:
    issues: list[ValidationIssue] = []

    for error_item in error.errors():
        location = error_item.get("loc", ())

        field_name = (
            ".".join(str(part) for part in location)
            if location
            else "state"
        )

        issue: ValidationIssue = {
            "field": field_name,
            "message": error_item.get(
                "msg",
                "Invalid value",
            ),
            "error_type": error_item.get(
                "type",
                "validation_error",
            ),
        }

        issues.append(issue)

    return issues


# =========================================================
# 5. Prepare the state for validation
# =========================================================

def prepare_input(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:
    print("\n[NODE: prepare_input]")
    print(
        "Project received:",
        state.get("project_name"),
    )

    return {
        "status": "validating",
        "validation_status": "not_validated",
        "validation_errors": [],
        "events": [
            "Project state prepared for validation"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 6. Validate the state
# =========================================================

def validate_state(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:
    print("\n[NODE: validate_state]")

    validation_input = {
        "project_name": state.get("project_name"),
        "project_owner": state.get("project_owner"),
        "budget": state.get("budget"),
        "duration_weeks": state.get(
            "duration_weeks"
        ),
        "team_size": state.get("team_size"),
        "priority": state.get("priority"),
    }

    try:
        validated = ProjectInput.model_validate(
            validation_input
        )

        print("Validation result: VALID")

        return {
            "project_name": validated.project_name,
            "project_owner": validated.project_owner,
            "budget": validated.budget,
            "duration_weeks": validated.duration_weeks,
            "team_size": validated.team_size,
            "priority": validated.priority,
            "validation_status": "valid",
            "validation_errors": [],
            "events": [
                "State validation completed successfully"
            ],
        }

    except ValidationError as error:
        validation_issues = format_validation_errors(
            error
        )

        print("Validation result: INVALID")

        for issue in validation_issues:
            print(
                f"- {issue['field']}: "
                f"{issue['message']}"
            )

        return {
            "validation_status": "invalid",
            "validation_errors": validation_issues,
            "events": [
                "State validation failed"
            ],
        }


# =========================================================
# 7. Route according to validation result
# =========================================================

def route_after_validation(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> Literal["process", "reject"\]:
    if state.get("validation_status") == "valid":
        return "process"

    return "reject"


# =========================================================
# 8. Process valid state
# =========================================================

def process_valid_state(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:
    print("\n[NODE: process_valid_state]")

    budget = state["budget"]
    duration_weeks = state["duration_weeks"]
    team_size = state["team_size"]
    priority = state["priority"]

    cost_per_week = budget / duration_weeks

    assessment = (
        f"Project '{state['project_name']}' is valid and ready "
        f"for execution. The project owner is "
        f"{state['project_owner']}. It has priority "
        f"'{priority}', a team size of {team_size}, and an "
        f"estimated weekly cost of {cost_per_week:.2f}."
    )

    print(
        f"Weekly cost: {cost_per_week:.2f}"
    )

    return {
        "cost_per_week": round(
            cost_per_week,
            2,
        ),
        "assessment": assessment,
        "status": "completed",
        "events": [
            "Validated state processed successfully"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 9. Handle invalid state
# =========================================================

def reject_invalid_state(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:
    print("\n[NODE: reject_invalid_state]")

    validation_errors = state.get(
        "validation_errors",
        [],
    )

    if validation_errors:
        error_message = "; ".join(
            f"{issue['field']}: {issue['message']}"
            for issue in validation_errors
        )
    else:
        error_message = (
            "The state failed validation for an "
            "unspecified reason."
        )

    assessment = (
        "The project request was rejected because its state "
        f"is invalid. Errors: {error_message}"
    )

    return {
        "assessment": assessment,
        "status": "rejected",
        "events": [
            "Invalid state rejected"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 10. Build the graph
# =========================================================

builder = StateGraph(WorkflowState)

builder.add_node(
    "prepare_input",
    prepare_input,
)

builder.add_node(
    "validate_state",
    validate_state,
)

builder.add_node(
    "process_valid_state",
    process_valid_state,
)

builder.add_node(
    "reject_invalid_state",
    reject_invalid_state,
)


# =========================================================
# 11. Add graph edges
# =========================================================

builder.add_edge(
    START,
    "prepare_input",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "prepare_input",
    "validate_state",
)

builder.add_conditional_edges(
    "validate_state",
    route_after_validation,
    {
        "process": "process_valid_state",
        "reject": "reject_invalid_state",
    },
)

builder.add_edge(
    "process_valid_state",
    END,
)

builder.add_edge(
    "reject_invalid_state",
    END,
)


# =========================================================
# 12. Compile the graph
# =========================================================

graph = builder.compile()


# =========================================================
# 13. Define test states
# =========================================================

valid_state: WorkflowState = {
    "project_name": "  Enterprise RAG Platform  ",
    "project_owner": "Kartik",
    "budget": 750_000.0,
    "duration_weeks": 16,
    "team_size": 8,
    "priority": "high",
    "validation_status": "not_validated",
    "validation_errors": [],
    "cost_per_week": 0.0,
    "assessment": "",
    "status": "received",
    "events": [
        "Valid project request received"
    ],
}


invalid_state: WorkflowState = {
    "project_name": " ",
    "project_owner": "K",
    "budget": -25_000.0,
    "duration_weeks": 0,
    "team_size": 0,
    "priority": "critical",
    "validation_status": "not_validated",
    "validation_errors": [],
    "cost_per_week": 0.0,
    "assessment": "",
    "status": "received",
    "events": [
        "Invalid project request received"
    ],
}


cross_field_invalid_state: WorkflowState = {
    "project_name": "Emergency Migration",
    "project_owner": "Kartik",
    "budget": 500_000.0,
    "duration_weeks": 2,
    "team_size": 2,
    "priority": "high",
    "validation_status": "not_validated",
    "validation_errors": [],
    "cost_per_week": 0.0,
    "assessment": "",
    "status": "received",
    "events": [
        "Cross-field validation request received"
    ],
}


# =========================================================
# 14. Display graph results
# ================================

def display_result(
    title: str,
    result: WorkflowState,
) -> None:
    print("\n" + "=" * 70)
    print(title)
    print("=" * 70)

    print(
        "Workflow status:",
        result.get("status"),
    )

    print(
        "Validation status:",
        result.get("validation_status"),
    )

    print(
        "Assessment:",
        result.get("assessment"),
    )

    validation_errors = result.get(
        "validation_errors",
        [],
    )

    if validation_errors:
        print("\nValidation errors:")

        for issue in validation_errors:
            print(
                f"- Field: {issue['field']}"
            )
            print(
                f"  Message: {issue['message']}"
            )
            print(
                f"  Type: {issue['error_type']}"
            )

    print("\nEvent history:")

    for number, event in enumerate(
        result.get("events", []),
        start=1,
    ):
        print(f"{number}. {event}")


# =========================================================
# 15. Run all scenarios
# =========================================================

def main() -> None:
    print("=" * 70)
    print("LANGGRAPH STATE VALIDATION")
    print("=" * 70)

    valid_result = graph.invoke(
        valid_state
    )

    display_result(
        "SCENARIO 1: VALID STATE",
        valid_result,
    )

    invalid_result = graph.invoke(
        invalid_state
    )

    display_result(
        "SCENARIO 2: INVALID FIELD VALUES",
        invalid_result,
    )

    cross_field_result = graph.invoke(
        cross_field_invalid_state
    )

    display_result(
        "SCENARIO 3: INVALID FIELD COMBINATION",
        cross_field_result,
    )


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()


e) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is state validation in LangGraph?

State validation is the process of checking whether graph-state values follow the expected structure, types, permitted values, ranges, and business rules before downstream nodes use them. It prevents incorrect data from reaching processing nodes and allows the graph to route invalid state to an error-handling, retry, repair, or human-review path.

Q2. Does TypedDict perform runtime validation?

No. TypedDict mainly provides static type information for developers, IDEs, and type-checking tools. A normal Python dictionary can still contain invalid runtime values. Explicit validation or a library such as Pydantic is required when the data must be checked during execution.

Q3. Why should validation use a separate node?

A dedicated validation node separates data checking from business processing. It can store validation errors in the shared state and use conditional routing to select the next path. This improves graph clarity, testability, observability, and error handling.

Q4. What is cross-field validation?

Cross-field validation checks the relationship between two or more fields. For example, a duration of two weeks and a team size of two may each be valid independently, but their combination may be invalid for a high-priority project.

Q5. When should state be revalidated?

State should be revalidated whenever uncertain data modifies the workflow and before critical operations. Common examples include LLM-generated output, tool responses, API results, subgraph output, database updates, financial operations, deployments, and irreversible actions.

Q6. How does state validation control routing?

The validation node stores a result such as:

{
    "validation_status": "valid"
}

A conditional routing function reads that value and returns a route name such as "process" or "reject". LangGraph then executes the node mapped to that route.



Chapter 7: Checkpointing & Durable Execution

a) Definition 

Checkpointing is the mechanism used in LangGraph to save the execution state of a workflow at different stages so that execution can be resumed later if the workflow is interrupted, fails, or requires human intervention.

In a LangGraph workflow, every node reads the current state, performs some work, and returns state updates. As the workflow progresses, the state continuously changes. Without checkpointing, if the system crashes after several nodes have already executed, the workflow must restart from the beginning. Checkpointing prevents this by storing snapshots of the workflow state.

A checkpoint contains information such as the current state, execution progress, node outputs, metadata, and thread information. When recovery is required, LangGraph loads the most recent checkpoint and continues execution from the saved point instead of re-running completed nodes.

Checkpointing is a foundational capability for building durable AI agents, long-running multi-step workflows, human-in-the-loop systems, and enterprise-grade automation where failures and interruptions are expected.

b) Purpose

1. Recover workflow execution after failures

Production AI systems can experience failures due to API outages, network issues, process crashes, infrastructure problems, or unexpected exceptions. Without checkpointing, all completed work would be lost.

By periodically saving state snapshots, the workflow can continue from the last successful checkpoint. This reduces downtime and improves system reliability.

2. Support long-running workflows

Some workflows execute for several minutes, hours, or even days. Examples include document processing pipelines, research agents, approval workflows, and enterprise automation systems.

Checkpointing ensures progress is preserved throughout execution. If interruption occurs during a long-running operation, only the unfinished portion must be executed again.

3. Enable human-in-the-loop workflows

Many enterprise AI applications require human review before continuing to the next step. A workflow may pause while waiting for days until a user approves or modifies information. Checkpointing allows the workflow state to remain available during the pause. Once a decision is received, execution can resume using the stored checkpoint.

4. Reduce execution cost

Modern AI workflows may involve multiple LLM calls, vector searches, tool executions, and database operations. Repeating completed work after every failure can significantly increase cost.

Checkpointing avoids recomputation by preserving completed execution results. The workflow continues from the saved checkpoint instead of repeating expensive operations.

c) Core Components

1. Workflow State

The workflow state contains all data required for execution. Every node reads and updates this shared state.

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    user_query: str
    documents: list[str]
    final_answer: str

The state becomes the primary object stored inside checkpoints.

2. Checkpoint Snapshot

A checkpoint snapshot represents the complete state of execution at a particular moment.

Example:

{
    "user_query": "What is RAG?",
    "documents": [
        "RAG combines retrieval and generation."
    ]
}

Snapshots allow the workflow to reconstruct execution exactly where it stopped.


3. Checkpoint Store

A checkpoint store is responsible for persisting checkpoints.

MemorySaver
    ↓
Stores checkpoints in RAM

SqliteSaver
    ↓
Stores checkpoints in SQLite

PostgreSQL
    ↓
Stores checkpoints in database

The store determines how checkpoints are saved, retrieved, and recovered.


4. Thread Identifier

Each workflow instance must be uniquely identified.

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "project-123"
    }
}

The thread identifier allows LangGraph to associate checkpoints with the correct workflow execution.


d) Architecture Diagram

                    START
                       |
                       v

             +------------------+
             |   Node A         |
             +------------------+
                       |
                Checkpoint #1
                       |
                       v

             +------------------+
             |   Node B         |
             +------------------+
                       |
                Checkpoint #2
                       |
                       v

             +------------------+
             |   Node C         |
             +------------------+
                       |
                      END


              Failure Occurs
                      |
                      v

         Load Checkpoint #2
                      |
                      v

           Resume From Node C


e) Code Example

from typing_extensions import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import (
    StateGraph,
    START,
    END,
)

from langgraph.checkpoint.memory import (
    MemorySaver,
)


# ====================================================
# 1. Define State
# ====================================================

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    project_name: str
    progress: int
    current_step: str
    execution_log: list[str]


# ====================================================
# 2. Define Nodes
# ====================================================

def planning_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Planning")

    return {
        "progress": 25,
        "current_step": "planning_completed",
        "execution_log": (
            state["execution_log"]
            + ["Planning completed"]
        ),
    }


def design_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Design")

    return {
        "progress": 50,
        "current_step": "design_completed",
        "execution_log": (
            state["execution_log"]
            + ["Design completed"]
        ),
    }


def development_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Development")

    return {
        "progress": 75,
        "current_step": "development_completed",
        "execution_log": (
            state["execution_log"]
            + ["Development completed"]
        ),
    }


def deployment_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Deployment")

    return {
        "progress": 100,
        "current_step": "deployment_completed",
        "execution_log": (
            state["execution_log"]
            + ["Deployment completed"]
        ),
    }


# ====================================================
# 3. Build Graph
# ====================================================

builder = StateGraph(
    WorkflowState
)

builder.add_node(
    "planning",
    planning_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "design",
    design_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "development",
    development_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "deployment",
    deployment_node,
)

builder.add_edge(
    START,
    "planning",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "planning",
    "design",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "design",
    "development",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "development",
    "deployment",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "deployment",
    END,
)

# ====================================================
# 4. Configure Checkpointing
# ====================================================

memory = MemorySaver()

graph = builder.compile(
    checkpointer=memory
)

# ====================================================
# 5. Thread Configuration
# ====================================================

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "project-001"
    }
}

# ====================================================
# 6. Execute Workflow
# ====================================================

initial_state = {
    "project_name": "Enterprise RAG Platform",
    "progress": 0,
    "current_step": "started",
    "execution_log": [],
}

result = graph.invoke(
    initial_state,
    config=config,
)

# ====================================================
# 7. Display Results
# ====================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 60)
print("FINAL RESULT")
print("=" * 60)

print(
    "Project:",
    result["project_name"]
)

print(
    "Progress:",
    result["progress"]
)

print(
    "Current Step:",
    result["current_step"]
)

print("\nExecution Log:")

for item in result["execution_log"\]:
    print("-", item)

# ====================================================
# 8. Inspect Checkpoint
# ====================================================

saved_state = graph.get_state(
    config
)

print("\n" + "=" * 60)
print("CHECKPOINT INFORMATION")
print("=" * 60)

print(saved_state)

f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is checkpointing in LangGraph?

Checkpointing is the process of saving workflow execution state during graph execution. If the workflow is interrupted due to a crash, failure, restart, or human intervention, LangGraph can load the saved checkpoint and continue execution from the last stored state instead of restarting from the beginning.

Q2. Why is checkpointing important in production systems?

Production workflows often involve long-running tasks, LLM calls, API integrations, and business-critical operations. Losing progress after a failure can be costly and disruptive. Checkpointing improves reliability by preserving workflow progress and enabling fast recovery.

Q3. What information is typically stored in a checkpoint?

A checkpoint usually contains workflow state, execution metadata, thread information, node outputs, progress indicators, and other data required to reconstruct execution. The goal is to restore the workflow exactly as it existed when the checkpoint was created.

Q4. What is the role of thread_id in checkpointing?

The thread_id uniquely identifies a workflow instance. LangGraph uses this identifier to associate checkpoints with the correct execution history. Multiple workflow executions can therefore maintain independent checkpoint records.

Q5. Can checkpointing reduce AI infrastructure costs?

Yes. Without checkpointing, failures require re-executing completed nodes and repeating expensive LLM calls. Checkpointing preserves completed work and allows execution to continue from the most recent saved state, reducing compute costs and API usage.

Q6. What types of workflows benefit most from checkpointing?

Checkpointing is especially useful for long-running workflows, multi-agent systems, document processing pipelines, research agents, human-in-the-loop applications, approval workflows, and enterprise automation systems where interruptions and failures are expected.


Topic 2: MemorySaver


a) Definition

MemorySaver is LangGraph's built-in in-memory checkpointing mechanism used to store workflow checkpoints during graph execution. It automatically captures and saves state snapshots as nodes execute, allowing developers to inspect, recover, and resume workflow execution within the current application runtime.

Unlike persistent checkpoint stores such as SqliteSaver or PostgreSQL-based checkpoint systems, MemorySaver keeps all checkpoints in the application's RAM. This makes it extremely fast because no database or disk operations are required. However, all saved checkpoints are lost when the application stops, crashes, or restarts.

MemorySaver is primarily intended for development, testing, debugging, proof-of-concepts, and local experimentation. It provides the same checkpointing interface as persistent checkpoint stores, making it easy to switch to a production-grade solution later without changing workflow logic.

b) Purpose

1. Simplify checkpointing during development

When learning LangGraph or building an initial workflow, developers often want checkpointing without configuring a database. MemorySaver works immediately after creating an instance and attaching it to the graph. This helps developers focus on workflow design instead of database setup, migrations, credentials, and infrastructure.

2. Enable debugging and state inspection

Debugging complex workflows often requires examining state transitions after every node execution. Since MemorySaver automatically stores checkpoints, developers can inspect execution history and understand how state changes over time. This visibility significantly improves troubleshooting and workflow testing.

3. Support workflow interruption and recovery

During a single application session, a workflow may pause because of user approval, human review, or temporary failure. MemorySaver allows state recovery and continuation without losing progress. As long as the application process remains active, checkpoints remain available.

4. Provide high-performance checkpoint storage

Memory access is significantly faster than disk or database access. MemorySaver therefore provides extremely fast checkpoint creation and retrieval. For development environments where long-term persistence is unnecessary, MemorySaver offers excellent performance with minimal complexity.


c) Core Components


1. In-Memory Checkpoint Store

MemorySaver stores all checkpoints directly in RAM. memory = MemorySaver(). Every workflow state snapshot remains available only while the application process is running.


2.State Snapshots

As graph execution progresses, MemorySaver captures workflow state after node execution. 

Example: 

{
    "task": "Build AI Chatbot",
    "progress": 50,
    "current_step": "design"
}

Each snapshot represents a recovery point.

3. Thread Management

MemorySaver organizes checkpoints using thread identifiers.

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "project-101"
    }
}

Each thread maintains an independent checkpoint history, allowing multiple workflows to execute simultaneously.

4. Checkpoint Retrieval

Stored checkpoints can be retrieved later to inspect workflow progress.

state = graph.get_state(config)

This capability is useful when debugging execution paths, validating state transitions, and investigating failures.

d) Architecture Diagram

                Graph Execution
                        |
                        v

              +------------------+
              |   MemorySaver    |
              +------------------+
                        |
                        v

                  RAM Storage

      ----------------------------------

      Checkpoint #1
      Checkpoint #2
      Checkpoint #3
      Checkpoint #4

      ----------------------------------

             Application Running
                        |
                        v
              Checkpoints Available

             Application Stops
                        |
                        v
               Checkpoints Lost

e)  Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is MemorySaver in LangGraph?

MemorySaver is LangGraph's built-in in-memory checkpoint store. It automatically saves workflow state snapshots during execution and allows workflows to recover or inspect execution state while the application is still running.

Q2. Where are checkpoints stored when using MemorySaver?

Checkpoints are stored in the application's RAM. Because the data remains only in memory, checkpoints disappear when the application process stops, crashes, or restarts.

Q3. When should MemorySaver be used?

MemorySaver is best suited for development, local testing, debugging, educational examples, proof-of-concept projects, and short-lived workflows where permanent persistence is not required.

Q4. What are the limitations of MemorySaver?

The primary limitation is lack of persistence. Since checkpoints are stored only in memory, system restarts, container recreation, server crashes, or application shutdowns will remove all saved checkpoint data.

Q5. How does MemorySaver use thread_id?

MemorySaver organizes checkpoints by thread_id. Each workflow instance has its own execution history and checkpoint chain, allowing multiple workflow executions to run independently within the same application.

Q6. Can a production system use MemorySaver?

While technically possible, it is generally not recommended. Production systems usually require checkpoints to survive failures and restarts. Persistent checkpoint stores such as SqliteSaver, PostgreSQL, or other durable storage solutions are better choices.

Topic 3: SQLiteSaver

a) Definition

SqliteSaver is a persistent checkpointing implementation in LangGraph that stores workflow checkpoints inside a SQLite database. Unlike MemorySaver, which keeps checkpoints only in application memory, SqliteSaver writes checkpoint data to disk, allowing workflow state to survive application restarts, crashes, and system failures.

In production workflows, execution may span multiple requests, processes, servers, or even days. If a workflow stops unexpectedly, the saved checkpoint can be loaded from the SQLite database and execution can resume from the last successful state. This capability is a key building block for durable execution.

SqliteSaver follows the same checkpointing interface as other LangGraph checkpoint stores. Developers can switch from MemorySaver to SqliteSaver with minimal changes while gaining persistent storage and recovery capabilities.

A typical SqliteSaver configuration looks like:

from langgraph.checkpoint.sqlite import SqliteSaver

checkpointer = SqliteSaver.from_conn_string(
    "workflow.db"
)

graph = builder.compile(
    checkpointer=checkpointer
)

Whenever nodes execute, LangGraph automatically stores state snapshots into the SQLite database.

b) Purpose

1. Preserve workflow state across application restarts

Many business workflows must continue operating even after deployments, maintenance windows, container recreation, or application restarts. SqliteSaver stores checkpoints on disk, ensuring previously completed work remains available after the system comes back online.

2. Support fault-tolerant execution

Infrastructure failures can occur at any stage of a workflow. Without persistence, all progress would be lost and expensive operations would need to be repeated. SqliteSaver enables workflows to recover from failures by reloading the latest checkpoint and continuing execution from the saved state.

3. Enable workflow auditing and debugging

Since checkpoints are stored permanently, developers can inspect historical workflow states and understand how execution progressed. This is valuable for troubleshooting, compliance requirements, testing, and operational monitoring.

4. Provide a lightweight production-ready solution

SQLite requires no separate database server and stores data in a single file. This makes SqliteSaver an excellent choice for small to medium-sized production deployments. It delivers persistence benefits while maintaining simple configuration and operational overhead.

c) Core Components

1. SQLite Database

The SQLite database is the physical storage location where checkpoints are persisted. The database file remains available even after the application terminates.

Example: checkpointer = SqliteSaver.from_conn_string( "checkpoints.db" )

2. Persistent Checkpoints

Every state snapshot is saved permanently inside the database.

Example Checkpoint

{
    "project_name": "Enterprise RAG",
    "progress": 75,
    "current_step": "development"
}


3. Thread-Based Storage

Each workflow execution is associated with a unique thread identifier.

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "project-1001"
    }
}

This allows multiple workflow instances to store independent checkpoint histories in the same database.

4. Recovery Mechanism

When a workflow resumes, LangGraph loads the latest checkpoint associated with the thread.

state = graph.get_state(config)

The recovered state becomes the starting point for continued execution.

d) Architecture Diagram

                   LangGraph Workflow
                            |
                            v

                    +---------------+
                    |  SqliteSaver  |
                    +---------------+
                            |
                            v

                 +----------------------+
                 |  SQLite Database     |
                 |    workflow.db       |
                 +----------------------+
                            |
         -----------------------------------------
         |                  |                    |
         v                  v                    v

   Checkpoint 1      Checkpoint 2       Checkpoint 3
   Progress=25%      Progress=50%       Progress=75%

         -----------------------------------------
                            |
                            v

                   Application Crash
                            |
                            v

                 Load Latest Checkpoint
                            |
                            v

                    Resume Execution

e) Code Example

import sqlite3
from operator import add
from typing import Annotated

from typing_extensions import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import (
    StateGraph,
    START,
    END,
)

from langgraph.checkpoint.sqlite import (
    SqliteSaver,
)


# =========================================================
# 1. Define Shared State
# =========================================================

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    project_name: str
    progress: int
    current_step: str

    execution_history: Annotated[
        list[str],
        add,
    ]


# =========================================================
# 2. Define Workflow Nodes
# =========================================================

def planning_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Planning")

    return {
        "progress": 25,
        "current_step": "planning_completed",
        "execution_history": [
            "Planning completed"
        ],
    }


def design_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Design")

    return {
        "progress": 50,
        "current_step": "design_completed",
        "execution_history": [
            "Design completed"
        ],
    }


def development_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Development")

    return {
        "progress": 75,
        "current_step": "development_completed",
        "execution_history": [
            "Development completed"
        ],
    }


def deployment_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Deployment")

    return {
        "progress": 100,
        "current_step": "deployment_completed",
        "execution_history": [
            "Deployment completed"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 3. Create SQLite Database Connection
# =========================================================

connection = sqlite3.connect(
    "workflow.db",
    check_same_thread=False,
)

checkpointer = SqliteSaver(connection)


# =========================================================
# 4. Build Graph
# =========================================================

builder = StateGraph(
    WorkflowState
)

builder.add_node(
    "planning",
    planning_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "design",
    design_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "development",
    development_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "deployment",
    deployment_node,
)

builder.add_edge(
    START,
    "planning",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "planning",
    "design",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "design",
    "development",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "development",
    "deployment",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "deployment",
    END,
)

graph = builder.compile(
    checkpointer=checkpointer
)


# =========================================================
# 5. Thread Configuration
# =========================================================

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "project-001"
    }
}


# =========================================================
# 6. Initial State
# =========================================================

initial_state: WorkflowState = {
    "project_name": "Enterprise RAG Platform",
    "progress": 0,
    "current_step": "started",
    "execution_history": [],
}


# =========================================================
# 7. Execute Workflow
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("FIRST EXECUTION")
print("=" * 70)

result = graph.invoke(
    initial_state,
    config=config,
)


# =========================================================
# 8. Display Final Result
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("FINAL WORKFLOW RESULT")
print("=" * 70)

print(
    "Project:",
    result["project_name"]
)

print(
    "Progress:",
    result["progress"]
)

print(
    "Current Step:",
    result["current_step"]
)

print("\nExecution History:")

for event in result["execution_history"\]:
    print("-", event)


# =========================================================
# 9. Retrieve Latest Checkpoint
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("LATEST CHECKPOINT")
print("=" * 70)

latest_state = graph.get_state(
    config
)

print(
    "Checkpoint Values:"
)

print(
    latest_state.values
)

print(
    "\nNext Nodes:"
)

print(
    latest_state.next
)

print(
    "\nMetadata:"
)

print(
    latest_state.metadata
)


# =========================================================
# 10. View Checkpoint History
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("CHECKPOINT HISTORY")
print("=" * 70)

for count, snapshot in enumerate(
    graph.get_state_history(config),
    start=1,
):

    print(
        f"\nCheckpoint #{count}"
    )

    print(
        "Values:",
        snapshot.values
    )

    print(
        "Next:",
        snapshot.next
    )


# =========================================================
# 11. Simulate Recovery
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("SIMULATED RECOVERY")
print("=" * 70)

recovered_state = graph.get_state(
    config
)

print(
    "Recovered Workflow State:"
)

print(
    recovered_state.values
)


# =========================================================
# 12. Resume Execution Example
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("RESUME EXECUTION")
print("=" * 70)

result_after_resume = graph.invoke(
    None,
    config=config,
)

print(
    "Result After Resume:"
)

print(
    result_after_resume
)


# =========================================================
# 13. Close Database
# =========================================================

connection.close()

f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is SqliteSaver in LangGraph?

SqliteSaver is a persistent checkpoint storage implementation that saves workflow checkpoints into a SQLite database. Unlike MemorySaver, the checkpoints remain available after application restarts, making it suitable for durable workflow execution.

Q2. How is SqliteSaver different from MemorySaver?

MemorySaver stores checkpoints in RAM and loses them when the application terminates. SqliteSaver stores checkpoints in a SQLite database file, allowing checkpoints to survive server restarts, crashes, and deployments.

Q3. When should SqliteSaver be used?

SqliteSaver is recommended when workflows need durable execution, state recovery, auditing, or long-running processing. It is commonly used in small and medium-sized production deployments.

Q4. What information does SqliteSaver store?

SqliteSaver stores workflow checkpoints that include state values, execution metadata, thread information, node outputs, and graph progress. This information allows LangGraph to reconstruct execution during recovery.

Q5. Does SqliteSaver support workflow recovery?

Yes. When a workflow is restarted, LangGraph can read the saved checkpoint associated with a thread_id and continue execution from the latest saved state instead of restarting from the beginning.

Q6. Is SqliteSaver suitable for enterprise-scale systems?

SqliteSaver is suitable for many production scenarios, but large enterprise systems often prefer PostgreSQL or dedicated database solutions for higher scalability, concurrency, backup, and operational capabilities.

Topic 4: Resume Execution

a) Definition

Resume Execution is the ability of a LangGraph workflow to continue running from its most recently saved checkpoint instead of restarting from the beginning. This feature is one of the primary benefits of checkpointing and durable execution.

When a workflow is interrupted because of an application crash, server restart, network failure, deployment activity, or human approval step, LangGraph can reload the saved checkpoint associated with a specific thread_id. The workflow state is restored, and execution continues from the next pending node.

Without resume execution, long-running workflows would need to repeat all previously completed work whenever an interruption occurs. This can be expensive and inefficient, especially when previous nodes contain LLM calls, database operations, tool executions, or API requests.

Resume execution is commonly used in AI agents, multi-step workflows, human-in-the-loop systems, document processing pipelines, approval systems, and enterprise business processes where interruptions are expected.

b) Purpose

1. Prevent reprocessing completed work

LLM calls, vector searches, database queries, and API requests can be expensive. If a workflow restarts from the beginning after every interruption, costs increase significantly. Resume execution allows the workflow to continue from the last successful checkpoint and avoids unnecessary recomputation.

2. Support long-running workflows

Many enterprise workflows run for hours or days. Examples include document review pipelines, approval systems, research agents, and business automation. Resume execution ensures progress is preserved throughout the workflow lifecycle and can continue even after interruptions.

3. Enable human-in-the-loop processing

Some workflows intentionally pause while waiting for human review, approval, or feedback. Resume execution allows the workflow to continue processing after the human action is completed without restarting previous workflow stages.

4. Improve workflow reliability

Production systems must tolerate failures such as server restarts, deployments, and infrastructure outages. Resume execution provides fault tolerance by ensuring workflows can recover and continue with minimal loss of progress.

c) Core Components

1. Checkpoint Store

Resume execution depends on a checkpoint store that persists workflow state.

Examples:  MemorySaver(), SqliteSaver(), PostgresSaver(). The checkpoint store provides the saved state required for workflow recovery.

2. Thread ID

Every workflow execution is identified by a unique thread.

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "workflow-001"
    }
}

LangGraph uses this identifier to locate the correct checkpoint history.

3. Saved State

The checkpoint contains the latest workflow state.

Example:
 	
	{
    		"project_name": "Enterprise RAG",
    		"current_step": "design_completed",
    		"progress": 50
	}

This state becomes the starting point when execution resumes.

4. Recovery Process

Recovery loads the checkpoint and restores graph execution. 

	state = graph.get_state(config)

Once loaded, the workflow can continue from its most recent execution point.

d) Architecture Diagram

                    START
                       |
                       v

                Research Node
                       |
                Checkpoint #1
                       |
                       v

                Analysis Node
                       |
                Checkpoint #2
                       |
                 Application
                    Crash
                       |
      --------------------------------
                       |
                       v

               Application Restart
                       |
                       v

             Load Checkpoint #2
                       |
                       v

             Resume Execution
                       |
                       v

             Report Generation
                       |
                       v

                Email Sending
                       |
                       v

                      END

e) Complete Code Example

from typing import Annotated

    StateGraph,
    START,
    END,
)

    SqliteSaver,
)

# 1. Shared State
# =========================================================

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    task_name: str
    progress: int
    current_step: str

    execution_history: Annotated[
        list[str],
        add,
    ]


# =========================================================
# 2. Nodes
# =========================================================

def research_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Research")

    return {
        "progress": 25,
        "current_step": "research_completed",
        "execution_history": [
            "Research completed"
        ],
    }


def analysis_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Analysis")

    return {
        "progress": 50,
        "current_step": "analysis_completed",
        "execution_history": [
            "Analysis completed"
        ],
    }


def report_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Report Generation")

    return {
        "progress": 75,
        "current_step": "report_completed",
        "execution_history": [
            "Report generated"
        ],
    }


def email_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Email Sending")

    return {
        "progress": 100,
        "current_step": "workflow_completed",
        "execution_history": [
            "Email sent"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 3. Create Database
# =========================================================

connection = sqlite3.connect(
    "resume_execution.db",
    check_same_thread=False,
)

checkpointer = SqliteSaver(
    connection
)


# =========================================================
# 4. Build Graph
# =========================================================

builder = StateGraph(
    WorkflowState
)

builder.add_node(
    "research",
    research_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "analysis",
    analysis_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "report",
    report_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "email",
    email_node,
)

builder.add_edge(
    START,
    "research",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "research",
    "analysis",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "analysis",
    "report",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "report",
    "email",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "email",
    END,
)

graph = builder.compile(
    checkpointer=checkpointer
)


# =========================================================
# 5. Thread Configuration
# =========================================================

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "resume-demo"
    }
}


# =========================================================
# 6. Initial State
# =========================================================

initial_state = {
    "task_name": "Enterprise Research Project",
    "progress": 0,
    "current_step": "started",
    "execution_history": [],
}


# =========================================================
# 7. First Workflow Execution
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("INITIAL EXECUTION")
print("=" * 70)

result = graph.invoke(
    initial_state,
    config=config,
)

print(
    "\nWorkflow Completed:"
)

print(result)


# =========================================================
# 8. Simulate Application Restart
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("SIMULATED RESTART")
print("=" * 70)

saved_state = graph.get_state(
    config
)

print(
    "\nRecovered Checkpoint:"
)

print(
    saved_state.values
)


# =========================================================
# 9. Resume Execution
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("RESUME EXECUTION")
print("=" * 70)

resume_result = graph.invoke(
    None,
    config=config,
)

print(
    "\nResume Result:"
)

print(
    resume_result
)


# =========================================================
# 10. Checkpoint History
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("CHECKPOINT HISTORY")
print("=" * 70)

for number, checkpoint in enumerate(
    graph.get_state_history(config),
    start=1,
):

    print(
        f"\nCheckpoint #{number}"
    )

    print(
        checkpoint.values
    )


# =========================================================
# 11. Close Database
# =========================================================

connection.close()

f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Resume Execution in LangGraph?

Resume execution is the capability to continue workflow execution from a previously saved checkpoint. Instead of restarting the workflow, LangGraph restores the saved state and continues from the next pending node.

Q2. How does LangGraph know where to resume?

LangGraph uses the thread_id to locate the workflow's checkpoint history. It loads the latest saved checkpoint and determines the next node that should execute.

Q3. Why is Resume Execution important?

Resume execution prevents loss of workflow progress, reduces recomputation costs, improves fault tolerance, and enables long-running workflows to survive failures and interruptions.

Q4. What is required for Resume Execution?

A checkpointing mechanism such as MemorySaver, SqliteSaver, or PostgresSaver is required. Without checkpoints, there is no saved state available for recovery.

Q5. Can Resume Execution work after application restart?

Yes, if a persistent checkpoint store such as SqliteSaver or PostgresSaver is used. MemorySaver cannot resume after a restart because checkpoints exist only in RAM.

Q6. What is the difference between State Recovery and Resume Execution?

State Recovery focuses on restoring the saved workflow state from a checkpoint. Resume Execution uses that recovered state to continue workflow processing from the next node. Recovery happens first, and resuming happens afterward.
