import sqlite3
from operator import add
from typing import Annotated

from typing_extensions import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import (
    StateGraph,
    START,
    END,
)

from langgraph.checkpoint.sqlite import (
    SqliteSaver,
)


# =========================================================
# 1. Shared State
# =========================================================

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    task_name: str
    progress: int
    current_step: str

    execution_history: Annotated[
        list[str],
        add,
    ]


# =========================================================
# 2. Nodes
# =========================================================

def research_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Research")

    return {
        "progress": 25,
        "current_step": "research_completed",
        "execution_history": [
            "Research completed"
        ],
    }


def analysis_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Analysis")

    return {
        "progress": 50,
        "current_step": "analysis_completed",
        "execution_history": [
            "Analysis completed"
        ],
    }


def report_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Report Generation")

    return {
        "progress": 75,
        "current_step": "report_completed",
        "execution_history": [
            "Report generated"
        ],
    }


def email_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Email Sending")

    return {
        "progress": 100,
        "current_step": "workflow_completed",
        "execution_history": [
            "Email sent"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 3. Create Database
# =========================================================

connection = sqlite3.connect(
    "resume_execution.db",
    check_same_thread=False,
)

checkpointer = SqliteSaver(
    connection
)


# =========================================================
# 4. Build Graph
# =========================================================

builder = StateGraph(
    WorkflowState
)

builder.add_node(
    "research",
    research_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "analysis",
    analysis_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "report",
    report_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "email",
    email_node,
)

builder.add_edge(
    START,
    "research",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "research",
    "analysis",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "analysis",
    "report",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "report",
    "email",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "email",
    END,
)

graph = builder.compile(
    checkpointer=checkpointer
)


# =========================================================
# 5. Thread Configuration
# =========================================================

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "resume-demo"
    }
}


# =========================================================
# 6. Initial State
# =========================================================

initial_state = {
    "task_name": "Enterprise Research Project",
    "progress": 0,
    "current_step": "started",
    "execution_history": [],
}


# =========================================================
# 7. First Workflow Execution
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("INITIAL EXECUTION")
print("=" * 70)

result = graph.invoke(
    initial_state,
    config=config,
)

print(
    "\nWorkflow Completed:"
)

print(result)


# =========================================================
# 8. Simulate Application Restart
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("SIMULATED RESTART")
print("=" * 70)

saved_state = graph.get_state(
    config
)

print(
    "\nRecovered Checkpoint:"
)

print(
    saved_state.values
)


# =========================================================
# 9. Resume Execution
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("RESUME EXECUTION")
print("=" * 70)

resume_result = graph.invoke(
    None,
    config=config,
)

print(
    "\nResume Result:"
)

print(
    resume_result
)


# =========================================================
# 10. Checkpoint History
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("CHECKPOINT HISTORY")
print("=" * 70)

for number, checkpoint in enumerate(
    graph.get_state_history(config),
    start=1,
):

    print(
        f"\nCheckpoint #{number}"
    )

    print(
        checkpoint.values
    )


# =========================================================
# 11. Close Database
# =========================================================

connection.close()


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Resume Execution in LangGraph?

Resume execution is the capability to continue workflow execution from a previously saved checkpoint. Instead of restarting the workflow, LangGraph restores the saved state and continues from the next pending node.

Q2. How does LangGraph know where to resume?

LangGraph uses the thread_id to locate the workflow's checkpoint history. It loads the latest saved checkpoint and determines the next node that should execute.

Q3. Why is Resume Execution important?

Resume execution prevents loss of workflow progress, reduces recomputation costs, improves fault tolerance, and enables long-running workflows to survive failures and interruptions.

Q4. What is required for Resume Execution?

A checkpointing mechanism such as MemorySaver, SqliteSaver, or PostgresSaver is required. Without checkpoints, there is no saved state available for recovery.

Q5. Can Resume Execution work after application restart?

Yes, if a persistent checkpoint store such as SqliteSaver or PostgresSaver is used. MemorySaver cannot resume after a restart because checkpoints exist only in RAM.

Q6. What is the difference between State Recovery and Resume Execution?

State Recovery focuses on restoring the saved workflow state from a checkpoint. Resume Execution uses that recovered state to continue workflow processing from the next node. Recovery happens first, and resuming happens afterward.


Topic 7:- Durable Workflows

a) Definition

A Durable Workflow is a workflow that can reliably continue execution even when interruptions occur due to application crashes, server restarts, deployment activities, infrastructure failures, human approval delays, or network issues. Durable workflows are built on top of checkpointing, persistence, state recovery, and resume execution.

In a traditional workflow system, if a failure occurs midway through execution, all progress may be lost and the workflow must restart from the beginning. Durable workflows eliminate this problem by continuously saving workflow state and execution progress so that the workflow can recover and continue from the last successful checkpoint.

In LangGraph, durable workflows are achieved through the combination of:

Checkpointing
      +
Persistent Storage
      +
State Recovery
      +
Resume Execution
      =
Durable Workflow

Durable workflows are especially important for enterprise AI systems where workflows may run for long periods and involve expensive operations such as LLM calls, tool execution, database transactions, API integrations, document processing, and human approvals.

b) Purpose

1. Ensure workflow completion despite failures

Enterprise workflows often encounter unexpected failures caused by infrastructure issues, service outages, deployment activities, or application crashes. Durable workflows guarantee that progress is preserved and execution can continue from the latest checkpoint instead of starting over.

2. Support long-running AI agents

Modern AI agents may execute for hours or days while performing research, document analysis, planning, approval processes, or tool orchestration. Durable workflows allow the agent to maintain progress across multiple sessions and interruptions.

3. Reduce operational and LLM costs

Many workflow steps include expensive operations such as GPT calls, vector retrieval, API requests, and database processing. Durable workflows prevent repeated execution of completed tasks, reducing cost and improving overall efficiency.

4. Improve reliability and user experience

Users expect enterprise systems to continue functioning even during maintenance, deployments, or failures. Durable workflows provide a more reliable experience because work is preserved and automatically recoverable.

c) Core Components

1. Checkpointing

Checkpointing periodically saves workflow state during execution.

memory = MemorySaver()
graph = builder.compile( checkpointer=memory )

Each saved checkpoint serves as a recovery point.


2. Persistent Storage

Durable workflows require checkpoints to survive application restarts.

Example: SqliteSaver, PostgresSaver

Persistent storage ensures workflow history remains available after failures.

3. State Recovery

State recovery reloads the most recent checkpoint.

saved_state = graph.get_state( config )

The recovered state becomes the starting point for continued execution.

4. Resume Execution

After state recovery, execution continues from the next pending node.

graph.invoke( None, config=config )

This avoids repeating previously completed workflow steps.


d. Architecture Diagram

                    User Request
                           |
                           v

                 +------------------+
                 | Workflow Start   |
                 +------------------+
                           |
                           v

                    Node A Execute
                           |
                    Checkpoint #1
                           |
                           v

                    Node B Execute
                           |
                    Checkpoint #2
                           |
                           v

                    Node C Execute
                           |
                     System Crash
                           |
     ------------------------------------------------
                           |
                           v

                   Application Restart
                           |
                           v

                 Recover Last Checkpoint
                           |
                           v

                  Resume Execution
                           |
                           v

                     Node D Execute
                           |
                           v

                           END

e. Code Example

import sqlite3
from operator import add
from typing import Annotated, Literal
from typing_extensions import TypedDict
from langgraph.graph import (
    StateGraph,
    START,
    END,
)

from langgraph.checkpoint.sqlite import (
    SqliteSaver,
)


# =========================================================
# 1. Define Shared State
# =========================================================

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    project_name: str

    status: Literal[
        "started",
        "planning_done",
        "development_done",
        "testing_done",
        "completed",
    ]

    progress: int

    events: Annotated[
        list[str],
        add,
    ]


# =========================================================
# 2. Planning Node
# =========================================================

def planning_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Planning")

    return {
        "status": "planning_done",
        "progress": 25,
        "events": [
            "Planning completed"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 3. Development Node
# =========================================================

def development_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Development")

    return {
        "status": "development_done",
        "progress": 50,
        "events": [
            "Development completed"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 4. Testing Node
# =========================================================

def testing_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Testing")

    return {
        "status": "testing_done",
        "progress": 75,
        "events": [
            "Testing completed"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 5. Deployment Node
# =========================================================

def deployment_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Deployment")

    return {
        "status": "completed",
        "progress": 100,
        "events": [
            "Deployment completed"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 6. Configure Persistent Storage
# =========================================================

connection = sqlite3.connect(
    "durable_workflow.db",
    check_same_thread=False,
)

checkpointer = SqliteSaver(
    connection
)


# =========================================================
# 7. Build Graph
# =========================================================

builder = StateGraph(
    WorkflowState
)

builder.add_node(
    "planning",
    planning_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "development",
    development_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "testing",
    testing_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "deployment",
    deployment_node,
)

builder.add_edge(
    START,
    "planning",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "planning",
    "development",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "development",
    "testing",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "testing",
    "deployment",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "deployment",
    END,
)

graph = builder.compile(
    checkpointer=checkpointer
)


# =========================================================
# 8. Thread Configuration
# =========================================================

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "durable-project-001"
    }
}


# =========================================================
# 9. Initial State
# =========================================================

initial_state: WorkflowState = {
    "project_name": "Enterprise AI Assistant",
    "status": "started",
    "progress": 0,
    "events": [],
}


# =========================================================
# 10. Execute Workflow
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("DURABLE WORKFLOW EXECUTION")
print("=" * 70)

result = graph.invoke(
    initial_state,
    config=config,
)


# =========================================================
# 11. Display Final Result
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("FINAL RESULT")
print("=" * 70)

print(
    "Project:",
    result["project_name"]
)

print(
    "Status:",
    result["status"]
)

print(
    "Progress:",
    result["progress"]
)

print("\nEvents:")

for event in result["events"]:
    print("-", event)


# =========================================================
# 12. Recover Latest State
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("STATE RECOVERY")
print("=" * 70)

saved_state = graph.get_state(
    config
)

print(
    "Recovered State:"
)

print(
    saved_state.values
)


# =========================================================
# 13. Display Checkpoint History
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("CHECKPOINT HISTORY")
print("=" * 70)

for index, checkpoint in enumerate(
    graph.get_state_history(config),
    start=1,
):

    print(
        f"\nCheckpoint #{index}"
    )

    print(
        checkpoint.values
    )


# =========================================================
# 14. Resume Workflow
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("RESUME EXECUTION")
print("=" * 70)

resume_result = graph.invoke(
    None,
    config=config,
)

print(
    resume_result
)


# =========================================================
# 15. Close Connection
# =========================================================

connection.close()


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is a Durable Workflow in LangGraph?

A durable workflow is a workflow that can survive failures, crashes, restarts, and interruptions without losing progress. It achieves this through checkpointing, persistence, state recovery, and resume execution.

Q2. What makes a workflow durable?

A workflow becomes durable when its state is continuously checkpointed and stored in persistent storage. If execution is interrupted, the workflow can recover its state and continue processing from the last checkpoint.

Q3. Why are durable workflows important for AI agents?

AI agents often perform expensive and long-running operations such as LLM calls, tool usage, document processing, and human approval workflows. Durable execution prevents loss of progress and reduces recomputation costs.

Q4. What is the relationship between checkpointing and durable workflows?

Checkpointing is the foundation of durable workflows. Without checkpoints, there is no saved state to recover after a failure. Durable execution depends on checkpoints being created and persisted.

Q5. Can a workflow be durable using MemorySaver?

Only while the application remains running. If the application restarts, MemorySaver data is lost. For true durability, persistent checkpoint stores such as SqliteSaver or PostgresSaver should be used.

Q6. What are common use cases for durable workflows?

Common use cases include AI agents, RAG pipelines, document processing systems, approval workflows, customer support automation, research agents, multi-agent systems, and enterprise business process automation.


Chapter 8: Human-in-the-Loop

Topic 1: Interrupts

a) Definition

An Interrupt is a LangGraph mechanism that intentionally pauses workflow execution and waits for external input before continuing. Unlike failures or crashes, interrupts are planned pauses that allow humans to review, modify, approve, or provide information required by the workflow.

Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) systems often perform critical tasks where fully autonomous execution may be risky. For example, an AI agent may generate a financial recommendation, deployment plan, legal summary, or customer response. Before the workflow proceeds, a human may need to verify the result. Interrupts make this possible by stopping execution at a specific point and handing control to a human.

When an interrupt occurs, LangGraph saves the current workflow state using the configured checkpointer. The workflow then waits until a human provides a decision or updated information. Once the input is received, execution resumes from the interruption point instead of restarting the entire workflow.

Interrupts are one of the most important enterprise features in LangGraph because they combine AI automation with human judgment. They ensure that high-risk decisions remain under human control while still benefiting from AI-driven automation.

Example:

User Request
      │
      ▼
AI Generates Report
      │
      ▼
INTERRUPT
      │
      ▼
Human Reviews Report
      │
      ▼
Resume Workflow
      │
      ▼
Send Final Report


b) Purpose

1. Enable Human Decision Making

Certain business processes cannot be fully automated because they involve legal, financial, security, or compliance-related decisions. AI can assist in generating recommendations, but a human must make the final decision.

Interrupts create a safe point in the workflow where human review can occur before execution continues. This reduces business risk and increases trust in AI systems.

2. Improve Accuracy of AI Systems

LLMs can occasionally produce hallucinations, incorrect reasoning, or incomplete information. Automatically executing every AI output could lead to serious mistakes. Interrupts allow humans to validate AI-generated content before it is used by downstream nodes. This improves overall workflow accuracy and reliability.

3. Support Human-in-the-Loop Workflows

Many enterprise workflows require collaboration between humans and AI agents. Examples include document approvals, code reviews, policy checks, and deployment approvals. Interrupts make human participation a natural part of graph execution by allowing workflows to pause and wait for manual input.

4. Prevent Expensive Mistakes

AI-driven actions such as deleting data, approving transactions, sending customer communications, or deploying software should not always happen automatically. An interrupt creates a checkpoint before performing such actions, ensuring that humans can inspect and approve the operation before it proceeds.

c) Core Components

1. Interrupt Point

An interrupt point is the location where workflow execution pauses. It is intentionally inserted into the graph when human review or external input is required.

When the interrupt is reached, LangGraph stops executing subsequent nodes and returns control to the caller. The workflow remains paused until execution is resumed with additional information.

interrupt( "Awaiting human approval" )

2. Checkpoint Store

When an interrupt occurs, the current workflow state must be preserved. The checkpointer stores the complete state so that execution can be resumed later without losing progress.

Checkpoint stores such as MemorySaver and SqliteSaver are commonly used with interrupts. They ensure that all workflow data remains available while the workflow is paused.

memory = MemorySaver()

3. Human Input

The purpose of an interrupt is usually to collect human input. The input may be an approval decision, rejection, correction, feedback, or additional business information. After the human responds, the provided information becomes part of the workflow state and is used by downstream nodes.

{
    "approval": "approved"
}

4. Resume Mechanism

Once human input is received, LangGraph resumes execution from the interruption point rather than restarting the workflow. The saved checkpoint is restored, the new input is merged into the state, and the remaining nodes continue processing normally.

graph.invoke(
    Command(
        resume="approved"
    ),
    config=config
)


d) Architecture Diagram

│      START             │
└────────┬─────────┘
            │
            ▼
┌──────────────────┐
│  Generate Report       │
└────────┬─────────┘
            │
            ▼
┌──────────────────┐
│    INTERRUPT           │
│ Waiting For User       │
└────────┬─────────┘
            │
            ▼
┌──────────────────┐
│ Human Review           │
│ Approve/Reject         │
└────────┬─────────┘
            │
            ▼
┌──────────────────┐
│   State Recovery       │
└────────┬─────────┘
            │
            ▼
┌──────────────────┐
│ Resume Workflow        │
└────────┬─────────┘
            │
            ▼
┌──────────────────┐
│ Final Processing       │
└────────┬─────────┘
            │
            ▼
┌──────────────────┐
│         END            │
└──────────────────┘


e) Code Example


from operator import add
from typing import Annotated, Literal

from typing_extensions import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import (
    StateGraph,
    START,
    END,
)

from langgraph.checkpoint.memory import (
    MemorySaver,
)

from langgraph.types import (
    interrupt,
    Command,
)


# =========================================================
# 1. Define Shared State
# =========================================================

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    report_title: str

    report_content: str

    approval_status: Literal[
        "pending",
        "approved",
        "rejected",
    ]

    final_message: str

    events: Annotated[
        list[str],
        add,
    ]


# =========================================================
# 2. Generate Report Node
# =========================================================

def generate_report(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Generate Report")

    return {
        "report_content":
        (
            "Quarterly revenue increased "
            "by 18 percent."
        ),
        "events": [
            "Report generated"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 3. Interrupt Node
# =========================================================

def human_review(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Human Review")

    approval = interrupt(
        {
            "message":
            "Approve report?",
            "report":
            state["report_content"],
        }
    )

    return {
        "approval_status":
        approval
    }


# =========================================================
# 4. Final Processing Node
# =========================================================

def final_processing(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print(
        "\n[NODE] Final Processing"
    )

    if (
        state["approval_status"]
        == "approved"
    ):

        return {
            "final_message":
            "Report approved and sent.",
            "events": [
                "Report approved"
            ],
        }

    return {
        "final_message":
        "Report rejected.",
        "events": [
            "Report rejected"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 5. Build Graph
# =========================================================

builder = StateGraph(
    WorkflowState
)

builder.add_node(
    "generate_report",
    generate_report,
)

builder.add_node(
    "human_review",
    human_review,
)

builder.add_node(
    "final_processing",
    final_processing,
)

builder.add_edge(
    START,
    "generate_report",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "generate_report",
    "human_review",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "human_review",
    "final_processing",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "final_processing",
    END,
)

# =========================================================
# 6. Checkpointer
# =========================================================

memory = MemorySaver()

graph = builder.compile(
    checkpointer=memory
)

# =========================================================
# 7. Thread Configuration
# =========================================================

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "approval-001"
    }
}

# =========================================================
# 8. Initial Execution
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("WORKFLOW START")
print("=" * 70)

graph.invoke(
    {
        "report_title":
        "Quarterly Business Report",
        "report_content": "",
        "approval_status":
        "pending",
        "final_message": "",
        "events": [],
    },
    config=config,
)

# =========================================================
# 9. Resume After Human Approval
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("HUMAN APPROVES")
print("=" * 70)

result = graph.invoke(
    Command(
        resume="approved"
    ),
    config=config,
)

# =========================================================
# 10. Display Result
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("FINAL RESULT")
print("=" * 70)

print(
    "Approval Status:",
    result["approval_status"]
)

print(
    "Message:",
    result["final_message"]
)

print("\nEvents:")

for event in result["events"]:
    print("-", event)


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is an interrupt in LangGraph?

An interrupt is a mechanism that pauses workflow execution and waits for external input before continuing. It is commonly used for human review, approval processes, feedback collection, and other Human-in-the-Loop workflows where automation must temporarily stop.

Q2. Why are interrupts important in enterprise AI systems?

Enterprise workflows often involve sensitive decisions such as financial approvals, legal reviews, or production deployments. Interrupts allow humans to review AI-generated outputs before critical actions occur, reducing business risk and improving trust.

Q3. What happens when an interrupt is triggered?

When an interrupt occurs, LangGraph stops workflow execution and saves the current state using the configured checkpointer. The workflow remains paused until external input is provided, after which it can resume from the interruption point.

Q4. How does a workflow resume after an interrupt?

The workflow resumes using a Command(resume=...) object. LangGraph loads the saved checkpoint, injects the provided human input into the workflow, and continues execution from the node following the interrupt.

Q5. Do interrupts require checkpointing?

Yes. Interrupts are most useful when combined with checkpointing because the workflow state must be preserved while waiting for human input. Without checkpoints, execution progress could be lost.

Q6. What are common real-world use cases for interrupts?

Common use cases include document approval systems, deployment approval workflows, legal contract reviews, financial transaction verification, AI-generated content moderation, healthcare review systems, and customer-support escalation processes.


Topic 2: Human Approval

a) Definition

Human Approval is a Human-in-the-Loop pattern where a workflow pauses and waits for a person to explicitly approve or reject an action before execution continues. It is one of the most common enterprise workflow patterns because many business operations require human authorization even when AI can perform most of the work.

In LangGraph, human approval is typically implemented using interrupts, checkpointing, and conditional routing. The workflow first performs AI-driven processing, then presents the result to a human reviewer. The reviewer decides whether the workflow should proceed, be rejected, or be modified.

Human approval is commonly used in deployment pipelines, financial transactions, legal reviews, healthcare systems, regulatory workflows, customer communications, and document publishing systems. These scenarios involve decisions that may carry business, legal, financial, or operational risk.

A human approval workflow generally follows this lifecycle:

AI Processing
      ↓
Approval Request
      ↓
Human Decision
      ↓
Approve / Reject
      ↓
Continue Workflow


b) Purpose

1. Prevent High-Risk Automated Decisions

AI systems can generate recommendations and automate processes, but some decisions are too important to be fully automated. Examples include approving large payments, publishing legal documents, or deploying software to production environments. Human approval ensures that a qualified person reviews the output before an irreversible action is performed. This reduces operational risk and improves accountability.

2. Improve Trust in AI Systems

Organizations are more likely to adopt AI solutions when they maintain control over critical decisions. Fully autonomous systems may create concerns regarding accuracy, governance, and compliance. By placing human approval before critical actions, businesses can safely integrate AI while maintaining confidence in workflow outcomes.

3. Support Compliance Requirements

Many industries require documented approvals for auditing and regulatory purposes. Financial institutions, healthcare providers, and government agencies often require approval records. Human approval workflows create clear decision points and make auditing easier because approval decisions become part of workflow history.

4. Enable Exception Handling

AI may produce outputs that require clarification, correction, or additional information. Human reviewers can identify such situations and prevent incorrect execution. Approval workflows provide a structured mechanism for handling exceptions before they impact downstream systems.

c) Core Components

1. Approval Request

The approval request contains the information that the human reviewer must evaluate. It may include AI-generated content, recommendations, reports, summaries, or proposed actions.

A well-designed approval request provides enough context for a reviewer to make an informed decision without needing to manually gather additional information. Clear approval requests improve workflow efficiency and reduce review time.

{
    "report": report_content,
    "requested_action": "publish"
}


2. Human Reviewer

The human reviewer is responsible for evaluating the approval request and making a decision. Depending on the workflow, the reviewer could be a manager, compliance officer, engineer, legal expert, or business stakeholder.

The approval system should clearly capture who approved the request and when the decision was made. This information is often required for auditing and governance purposes.


{
    "reviewer": "manager_01"
}


3. Approval Decision

The approval decision determines whether the workflow continues or stops. Most approval systems support at least two outcomes: approved and rejected. Additional outcomes such as revision required, escalation required, or additional information needed may also be supported in more advanced workflows.

{
    "approval_status": "approved"
}


4. Conditional Routing

After the decision is captured, the workflow must determine the next execution path. Conditional routing reads the approval status and directs the workflow to the appropriate node. This separation between decision-making and routing keeps workflows easier to maintain and test.


if state["approval_status"] == "approved":
    return "publish"
else:
    return "reject"


d) Architecture Diagram


┌───────────────────┐
│       START             │
└─────────┬─────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌───────────────────┐
│ AI Generates            │
│ Business Report         │
└─────────┬─────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌───────────────────┐
│ Approval Request        │
│ Created                 │
└─────────┬─────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌───────────────────┐
│ Human Reviewer          │
│ Approve / Reject        │
└─────────┬─────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌───────────────────┐
│ Conditional             │
│ Routing                 │
└──────┬─────┬──────┘
         │       │
         │       │
         ▼       ▼
┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐
│ Approved    │ │ Rejected     │
│  Path       │ │   Path       │
└────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘
       │               │
       ▼               ▼
┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐
│ Continue    │ │ Workflow    │
│ Workflow    │ │ Stopped     │
└────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘
       │               │
       └──────┬─────┘
               ▼
┌───────────────────┐
│        END              │
└───────────────────┘

e) Code Example

from operator import add
from typing import Annotated, Literal

from typing_extensions import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import (
    StateGraph,
    START,
    END,
)

from langgraph.types import (
    interrupt,
    Command,
)

from langgraph.checkpoint.memory import (
    MemorySaver,
)


# =========================================================
# 1. State Definition
# =========================================================

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    report: str

    approval_status: Literal[
        "pending",
        "approved",
        "rejected",
    ]

    final_message: str

    events: Annotated[
        list[str],
        add,
    ]


# =========================================================
# 2. Generate Report
# =========================================================

def generate_report(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Generate Report")

    return {
        "report":
        "Annual revenue increased by 22%",
        "events": [
            "Report generated"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 3. Human Approval
# =========================================================

def request_approval(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Human Approval")

    decision = interrupt(
        {
            "message":
            "Approve the report?",
            "report":
            state["report"],
        }
    )

    return {
        "approval_status":
        decision
    }


# =========================================================
# 4. Routing Function
# =========================================================

def route_after_approval(
    state: WorkflowState,
):

    if (
        state["approval_status"]
        == "approved"
    ):
        return "approved"

    return "rejected"


# =========================================================
# 5. Approved Node
# =========================================================

def approved_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    return {
        "final_message":
        "Report approved and published.",
        "events": [
            "Approval granted"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 6. Rejected Node
# =========================================================

def rejected_node(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    return {
        "final_message":
        "Report rejected.",
        "events": [
            "Approval denied"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 7. Build Graph
# =========================================================

builder = StateGraph(
    WorkflowState
)

builder.add_node(
    "generate_report",
    generate_report,
)

builder.add_node(
    "request_approval",
    request_approval,
)

builder.add_node(
    "approved_node",
    approved_node,
)

builder.add_node(
    "rejected_node",
    rejected_node,
)

builder.add_edge(
    START,
    "generate_report",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "generate_report",
    "request_approval",
)

builder.add_conditional_edges(
    "request_approval",
    route_after_approval,
    {
        "approved":
        "approved_node",
        "rejected":
        "rejected_node",
    },
)

builder.add_edge(
    "approved_node",
    END,
)

builder.add_edge(
    "rejected_node",
    END,
)

graph = builder.compile(
    checkpointer=MemorySaver()
)

# =========================================================
# 8. Configuration
# =========================================================

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "approval-demo"
    }
}

# =========================================================
# 9. Start Workflow
# =========================================================

graph.invoke(
    {
        "report": "",
        "approval_status": "pending",
        "final_message": "",
        "events": [],
    },
    config=config,
)

# =========================================================
# 10. Human Approves
# =========================================================

result = graph.invoke(
    Command(
        resume="approved"
    ),
    config=config,
)

# =========================================================
# 11. Display Result
# =========================================================

print("\nFinal Message:")
print(
    result["final_message"]
)

print("\nEvents:")

for event in result["events"\]:
   print("-", event)


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Human Approval in LangGraph?

Human Approval is a workflow pattern where execution pauses and waits for a person to approve or reject an action before continuing. It is commonly implemented using interrupts, checkpointing, and conditional routing.

Q2. Why is human approval important in enterprise workflows?

Many enterprise operations involve legal, financial, security, or compliance-related risks. Human approval ensures that important decisions are reviewed by qualified individuals before execution proceeds.

Q3. How is human approval implemented in LangGraph?

Human approval is usually implemented by triggering an interrupt, collecting a decision from a reviewer, storing the decision in state, and then using conditional routing to choose the next workflow path.

Q4. What happens if a request is rejected?

When a request is rejected, the workflow can stop, return an error, request modifications, escalate the case, or follow a separate rejection path depending on business requirements.

Q5. What is the difference between Interrupts and Human Approval?

Interrupts are the technical mechanism that pauses execution. Human approval is a business workflow pattern that often uses interrupts to collect a user's decision before continuing.

Q6. What are common use cases for Human Approval?

Common use cases include production deployments, expense approvals, contract reviews, content publishing, compliance checks, document sign-offs, financial transactions, and customer communication reviews.


Topic 3: Human Feedback


a) Definition

Human Feedback is a Human-in-the-Loop mechanism where a workflow pauses and requests feedback from a user, reviewer, domain expert, or business stakeholder before continuing execution. Unlike Human Approval, which typically expects a simple approve/reject decision, Human Feedback captures richer input that can modify, improve, or correct workflow results.

In many AI applications, the output generated by an LLM is not immediately accepted. Users may want to refine a summary, modify generated content, correct factual information, adjust recommendations, or provide additional business requirements. Human feedback enables this collaborative interaction between AI systems and humans.

In LangGraph, human feedback is commonly implemented using interrupts, checkpointing, state updates, and resume mechanisms. The workflow generates an initial result, pauses for feedback, incorporates the user feedback into the state, and then continues processing using the updated information.

Human feedback is widely used in document generation systems, content creation workflows, report writing, code generation, customer support automation, legal document review, and AI-powered knowledge assistants.

Example:

			User Request
                             │
                             ▼
		   AI Generates Summary
  			     │
                             ▼
                     Request Feedback
                             │
                             ▼
                 Human Provides Corrections
                             │
                             ▼
                    AI Refines Summary
                             │
                             ▼
                       Final Result

b) Purpose

1. Improve AI Output Quality

AI-generated content may be technically correct but still fail to meet user expectations. The output may require additional detail, simplification, corrections, or formatting changes before it becomes useful.

Human feedback allows users to guide the workflow toward a more accurate and contextually appropriate result. This creates higher-quality outputs and better user satisfaction.


2. Incorporate Domain Expertise

Many business processes involve specialized knowledge that an AI model may not fully understand. Subject matter experts often possess important context that is unavailable to the model. Human feedback enables experts to contribute their knowledge during workflow execution, improving the final outcome and reducing errors.


3. Create Collaborative AI Systems

Modern AI systems are most effective when humans and AI work together. Rather than replacing human decision-making, AI can generate drafts, suggestions, and recommendations that humans refine. Human feedback creates a collaborative workflow where both AI and human expertise contribute to the final result.


4. Correct Hallucinations and Errors

Large Language Models occasionally generate inaccurate information, unsupported conclusions, or misleading recommendations. Such errors may propagate through downstream workflow steps if not corrected. Human feedback allows reviewers to identify and correct issues before the workflow continues, improving reliability and trustworthiness.


c) Core Components

1. Feedback Request

A feedback request is the information presented to the human reviewer. It usually contains AI-generated content and instructions indicating what type of feedback is needed.

A good feedback request provides sufficient context so that reviewers can quickly understand the output and identify areas needing improvement. Clear requests lead to better feedback and faster review cycles.

{
    "summary": generated_summary,
    "request": "Provide corrections"
}

2. Human Reviewer

The reviewer is the person responsible for evaluating the generated content and providing feedback. Depending on the workflow, this may be an end user, manager, analyst, editor, engineer, or domain specialist.

The quality of feedback directly affects the quality of the refined output. Therefore, selecting the right reviewer is often a critical workflow design decision.

{
    "reviewer": "content_editor"
}

3. Feedback Data

Feedback data contains the corrections, suggestions, comments, or new requirements supplied by the reviewer. This information becomes part of the workflow state. The workflow can later use this feedback to modify AI-generated results, trigger additional processing, or drive routing decisions.

{
    "feedback":
    "Add more details about revenue growth."
}

4. Feedback Integration

Feedback integration is the process of incorporating reviewer input into the shared workflow state. After feedback is collected, downstream nodes use it to improve, regenerate, or validate content. A well-designed workflow clearly separates feedback collection from feedback processing, making the workflow easier to maintain and extend.

d) Architecture Diagram

┌────────────────────┐
│       START               │
└─────────┬──────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌────────────────────┐
│ Generate Initial          │
│ Content                   │
└─────────┬──────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌────────────────────┐
│ Request Feedback          │
│ (Interrupt)               │
└─────────┬──────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌────────────────────┐
│ Human Reviewer            │
│ Provides Feedback         │
└─────────┬──────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌────────────────────┐
│ Update Workflow           │
│ State                     │
└─────────┬──────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌────────────────────┐
│ Refine Output             │
└─────────┬──────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌────────────────────┐
│        END                │
└────────────────────┘

e) Code Example

from operator import add
from typing import Annotated

from typing_extensions import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import (
    StateGraph,
    START,
    END,
)

from langgraph.types import (
    interrupt,
    Command,
)

from langgraph.checkpoint.memory import (
    MemorySaver,
)


# =========================================================
# 1. Define Shared State
# =========================================================

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    topic: str

    generated_summary: str

    feedback: str

    final_summary: str

    events: Annotated[
        list[str],
        add,
    ]


# =========================================================
# 2. Generate Summary
# =========================================================

def generate_summary(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Generate Summary")

    summary = (
        "RAG combines retrieval and generation "
        "to improve LLM responses."
    )

    return {
        "generated_summary": summary,
        "events": [
            "Initial summary generated"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 3. Request Human Feedback
# =========================================================

def collect_feedback(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Collect Feedback")

    feedback = interrupt(
        {
            "message":
            "Provide feedback",
            "summary":
            state["generated_summary"],
        }
    )

    return {
        "feedback": feedback
    }


# =========================================================
# 4. Refine Content
# =========================================================

def refine_summary(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Refine Summary")

    improved_summary = (
        f"{state['generated_summary']} "
        f"Human feedback: {state['feedback']}"
    )

    return {
        "final_summary":
        improved_summary,
        "events": [
            "Summary refined using feedback"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 5. Build Graph
# =========================================================

builder = StateGraph(
    WorkflowState
)

builder.add_node(
    "generate_summary",
    generate_summary,
)

builder.add_node(
    "collect_feedback",
    collect_feedback,
)

builder.add_node(
    "refine_summary",
    refine_summary,
)

builder.add_edge(
    START,
    "generate_summary",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "generate_summary",
    "collect_feedback",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "collect_feedback",
    "refine_summary",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "refine_summary",
    END,
)

graph = builder.compile(
    checkpointer=MemorySaver()
)

# =========================================================
# 6. Configuration
# =========================================================

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "feedback-demo"
    }
}

# =========================================================
# 7. Initial Execution
# =========================================================

graph.invoke(
    {
        "topic": "RAG",
        "generated_summary": "",
        "feedback": "",
        "final_summary": "",
        "events": [],
    },
    config=config,
)

# =========================================================
# 8. Human Feedback
# =========================================================

result = graph.invoke(
    Command(
        resume=
        "Include vector databases and embeddings."
    ),
    config=config,
)

# =========================================================
# 9. Display Result
# =========================================================

print("\nFinal Summary:")

print(
    result["final_summary"]
)

print("\nEvents:")

for event in result["events"\]:
    print("-", event)


f) Interview Questions and Answers

Q1. What is Human Feedback in LangGraph?

Human Feedback is a Human-in-the-Loop workflow pattern where users provide corrections, suggestions, or additional information that helps improve AI-generated outputs. The workflow captures this feedback and incorporates it into subsequent processing steps.

Q2. How is Human Feedback different from Human Approval?

Human Approval usually results in a simple approve or reject decision. Human Feedback collects richer information such as suggestions, corrections, comments, and requirements that can be used to modify workflow behavior or output quality.

Q3. Why is Human Feedback important for AI systems?

AI systems may produce incomplete, inaccurate, or suboptimal outputs. Human feedback helps refine these outputs, incorporate domain expertise, and improve overall system performance and user satisfaction.

Q4. How is Human Feedback implemented in LangGraph?

Human feedback is commonly implemented using interrupts. The workflow pauses, collects reviewer feedback, stores it in the workflow state, and then resumes execution to process the updated information.

Q5. What types of workflows benefit from Human Feedback?

Document generation, content creation, report writing, code review, customer support systems, legal review processes, educational systems, and AI assistants commonly benefit from feedback-driven workflows.

Q6. Can feedback influence workflow routing?

Yes. Feedback can be stored in workflow state and used by conditional routing functions. For example, positive feedback may continue processing while negative feedback may trigger revisions or escalation paths.


4. Pause and Resume


a) Definition

Pause and Resume is a Human-in-the-Loop workflow pattern that allows a LangGraph workflow to temporarily stop execution and continue later without losing its progress. Unlike a system failure or crash, a pause is an intentional suspension of workflow execution.

In real-world business processes, workflows frequently need to wait for external events before continuing. These events may include human approvals, feedback submissions, document uploads, API responses, customer actions, or business decisions. Instead of continuously consuming compute resources while waiting, the workflow can pause and persist its state.

LangGraph achieves pause and resume through a combination of interrupts, checkpointing, state persistence, and resume commands. When a pause occurs, the workflow state is saved using a checkpointer. The workflow then remains inactive until new information arrives.

Once the required input is available, the workflow can resume from the exact point where it was paused. Previously completed nodes do not execute again, making the process efficient and reliable.

Example:

Workflow Started
       │
       ▼
Generate Draft
       │
       ▼
     PAUSE
       │
 Wait For Manager Review
       │
       ▼
Resume Workflow
       │
       ▼
Publish Document


b) Purpose

1. Wait for External Events

Many workflows depend on information that may not be immediately available. Examples include approval decisions, customer responses, uploaded documents, or third-party API callbacks. Pause functionality allows the workflow to safely stop execution while waiting for those events instead of continuously polling or restarting later.

2. Support Long-Running Business Processes

Enterprise workflows often span hours, days, or even weeks. Examples include onboarding processes, compliance reviews, contract approvals, and procurement workflows. Without pause and resume capabilities, maintaining workflow continuity over long periods would be extremely difficult and resource-intensive.

3. Reduce Infrastructure Costs

Keeping a workflow actively running while waiting for human input wastes compute resources. Servers, containers, and memory remain occupied even though no useful work is happening. By pausing the workflow and storing its state, organizations can significantly reduce infrastructure usage while preserving execution progress.

4. Improve Reliability and User Experience

Users may not provide feedback immediately. Human decisions can take minutes, hours, or days depending on business requirements. Pause and resume ensures that users can return later and continue exactly where the workflow stopped, creating a seamless experience.

c) Core Components

1. Pause Point

A pause point is the location in the graph where execution intentionally stops. It is typically implemented using an interrupt and indicates that external input is required before further processing can continue.

The pause point acts as a controlled checkpoint in the workflow. Once reached, downstream nodes do not execute until new information is provided. This prevents incomplete or unauthorized processing.

interrupt( "Waiting for manager feedback" )

2. Checkpoint Store

When a workflow pauses, the current state must be preserved. The checkpoint store saves all workflow information required to continue execution later. Checkpoint stores such as MemorySaver and SqliteSaver ensure that progress, intermediate results, and workflow metadata remain available during the pause period.

checkpointer=MemorySaver()

3. Resume Command

The resume command provides the information needed to restart execution. It typically contains user input, approval decisions, or feedback collected while the workflow was paused. LangGraph injects this information into the workflow state and continues execution from the interruption point rather than starting from the beginning.

Command(resume="approved" )

4. State Restoration

Before execution continues, LangGraph restores the workflow state from the saved checkpoint. This ensures that all previously completed work remains available. State restoration eliminates the need to repeat completed nodes and guarantees workflow consistency throughout the execution lifecycle.

graph.get_state(config)

d) Architecture Diagram

┌───────────────────┐
│       START             │
└─────────┬─────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌───────────────────┐
│ Generate Draft          │
└─────────┬─────────┘
             │
             ▼

             │
             ▼

└─────────┬─────────┘
             │
             ▼

│ Human Feedback           │
│ / Event Arrives         │
└─────────┬─────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌───────────────────┐
│  Restore State           │
└─────────┬─────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌───────────────────┐
│ Resume Workflow         │
└─────────┬─────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌───────────────────┐
│ Final Processing        │
└─────────┬─────────┘
             │
             ▼
┌───────────────────┐
│        END              │
└───────────────────┘


e) Code Example

from operator import add
from typing import Annotated, Literal

from typing_extensions import TypedDict

from langgraph.graph import (
    StateGraph,
    START,
    END,
)

from langgraph.checkpoint.memory import (
    MemorySaver,
)

from langgraph.types import (
    interrupt,
    Command,
)


# =========================================================
# 1. Shared State
# =========================================================

class WorkflowState(TypedDict):
    document_title: str

    document_content: str

    review_status: Literal[
        "pending",
        "reviewed",
    ]

    final_message: str

    events: Annotated[
        list[str],
        add,
    ]


# =========================================================
# 2. Draft Creation Node
# =========================================================

def create_draft(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Create Draft")

    return {
        "document_content":
        "AI generated project proposal.",
        "events": [
            "Draft created"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 3. Pause Workflow
# =========================================================

def pause_for_review(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print("\n[NODE] Pause For Review")

    review_input = interrupt(
        {
            "message":
            "Please review the document",
            "content":
            state["document_content"],
        }
    )

    return {
        "review_status":
        "reviewed",
        "events": [
            f"Reviewer comment: {review_input}"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 4. Final Processing
# =========================================================

def complete_workflow(
    state: WorkflowState,
) -> dict:

    print(
        "\n[NODE] Complete Workflow"
    )

    return {
        "final_message":
        "Document review completed.",
        "events": [
            "Workflow completed"
        ],
    }


# =========================================================
# 5. Build Graph
# =========================================================

builder = StateGraph(
    WorkflowState
)

builder.add_node(
    "create_draft",
    create_draft,
)

builder.add_node(
    "pause_for_review",
    pause_for_review,
)

builder.add_node(
    "complete_workflow",
    complete_workflow,
)

builder.add_edge(
    START,
    "create_draft",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "create_draft",
    "pause_for_review",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "pause_for_review",
    "complete_workflow",
)

builder.add_edge(
    "complete_workflow",
    END,
)

graph = builder.compile(
    checkpointer=MemorySaver()
)

# =========================================================
# 6. Configuration
# =========================================================

config = {
    "configurable": {
        "thread_id": "pause-resume-demo"
    }
}

# =========================================================
# 7. Start Workflow
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("INITIAL EXECUTION")
print("=" * 70)

graph.invoke(
    {
        "document_title":
        "Project Proposal",
        "document_content": "",
        "review_status":
        "pending",
        "final_message": "",
        "events": [],
    },
    config=config,
)

# =========================================================
# 8. Simulate Human Response
# =========================================================

print("\n" + "=" * 70)
print("RESUME WORKFLOW")
print("=" * 70)

result = graph.invoke(
    Command(
        resume=
        "Proposal looks good. Continue."
    ),
    config=config,
)

# =========================================================
# 9. Display Result
# =========================================================

print("\nFinal Message:")
print(
    result["final_message"]
)

print("\nEvent History:")

for event in result["events"\]:
    print("-", event)


f) Interview Questions And Answers

Q1. What is Pause and Resume in LangGraph?

Pause and Resume is a workflow capability that allows execution to stop temporarily and continue later without losing progress. It is commonly used when workflows need to wait for human decisions, feedback, approvals, or external events.

Q2. How is Pause different from a workflow failure?

A pause is an intentional workflow suspension designed by the developer. A failure is an unexpected interruption caused by errors, crashes, or infrastructure problems. Pauses are controlled, whereas failures are accidental.

Q3. How does LangGraph resume a paused workflow?

LangGraph loads the saved checkpoint, restores the workflow state, injects the new input through a resume command, and continues execution from the interruption point instead of restarting the graph.

Q4. Why are checkpoints important for Pause and Resume?

Without checkpoints, workflow state would be lost when execution pauses. Checkpointing ensures that all progress, node outputs, and workflow metadata remain available when execution later resumes.

Q5. What are common real-world use cases for Pause and Resume?

Examples include manager approvals, contract reviews, customer feedback collection, human validation of AI outputs, compliance verification, deployment approvals, and waiting for external business events.

Q6. Can a paused workflow remain suspended for days?

Yes. When persistent checkpoint storage such as SqliteSaver or PostgresSaver is used, a workflow can remain paused for extended periods and resume later while preserving its complete state.


5. Review Workflows

a) Definition

A Review Workflow is a Human-in-the-Loop workflow pattern where AI-generated output, workflow results, documents, recommendations, or decisions are examined by one or more human reviewers before the workflow proceeds. The primary goal is to ensure quality, accuracy, compliance, and business correctness before further processing occurs.

Unlike a simple approval workflow, a review workflow often allows multiple outcomes. The reviewer may approve the result, reject it, request modifications, provide feedback, escalate it to another reviewer, or send it back for reprocessing. This makes review workflows more flexible and suitable for complex business processes.

In LangGraph, review workflows are commonly implemented using interrupts, checkpointing, conditional routing, and feedback mechanisms. The workflow pauses when review is required, stores its current state, waits for human evaluation, and then resumes based on the review outcome.

Review workflows are widely used in content publishing systems, legal document validation, contract reviews, software quality assurance, financial reporting, healthcare documentation, compliance checks, and AI-generated content moderation.


b) Purpose

1. Improve Output Quality

AI-generated responses, reports, and recommendations may contain inaccuracies, incomplete information, or formatting issues. Allowing a reviewer to inspect results before they move forward helps ensure higher-quality outputs. Review workflows provide an additional quality-control layer that catches problems early. This is particularly important when outputs are customer-facing or business-critical.

2. Reduce Operational Risk

Many workflows involve sensitive business operations where mistakes can have financial, legal, or reputational consequences. Automatically executing every AI-generated result may introduce unnecessary risk. Human review acts as a safeguard by ensuring critical decisions are validated before downstream actions occur.

3. Support Compliance and Governance

Organizations often require evidence that certain actions were reviewed before execution. Industries such as finance, healthcare, insurance, and government frequently have regulatory requirements around review processes. Review workflows create traceable records showing what was reviewed, who reviewed it, and what decision was made.

4. Handle Complex Decision-Making

Not every workflow can be reduced to simple approve/reject logic. A reviewer may identify issues that require clarification, correction, escalation, or additional analysis. Review workflows support these more complex business scenarios by allowing multiple review outcomes and flexible routing paths.


c) Core Components

1. Review Request

The review request contains the information that must be evaluated by the reviewer. This may include AI-generated summaries, recommendations, reports, code changes, contracts, or business documents.

A good review request provides sufficient context so that the reviewer can make informed decisions without needing to gather additional information. The quality of the review process often depends on the completeness of the review request.

{
    "document": generated_report,
    "review_type": "compliance_review"
}

2. Reviewer

The reviewer is the person or group responsible for evaluating the submitted content. Depending on the workflow, the reviewer might be a manager, legal expert, compliance officer, editor, or technical specialist.

The reviewer's decision directly affects workflow progression. Therefore, reviewer roles and responsibilities should be clearly defined within enterprise systems.

{
    "reviewer": "compliance_manager"
}

3. Review Outcome

The review outcome captures the result of the evaluation process. Unlike approval workflows, review workflows typically support multiple outcomes such as approve, reject, revise, or escalate.

The workflow uses the review outcome to determine the next execution path. Storing review decisions within state also improves auditability and observability.

{
    "review_status": "revise"
}

4. Review Routing

Review routing determines which node should execute after the review is completed. Different outcomes may lead to different workflow branches.

For example, approved content may continue to publication, rejected content may terminate the workflow, and revision requests may return content to an AI-generation node for improvement.

if state["review_status"] == "approve":
    return "publish"

d) Architecture Diagram

┌────────────────────┐
│       START               │

             │
             ▼
